
## Agent Insights Suggestions
<!-- agent-insights-auto -->
- After completing 3 search cohorts in a research session, explicitly assess evidence saturation and declare stopping criteria (e.g., 'no new themes emerging across last 2 cohorts') before continuing incremental searches.
- After completing an aggressive research session with multiple tool calls, explicitly synthesize findings into the user-requested structured format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks, JSON arrays for SOURCES and CLAIMS) and confirm delivery of the final output block before ending the session.
- After completing an exhaustive research loop, always explicitly emit the promised structured-output block (or JSON/XML envelope) before invoking synthesis or Agent tools, and confirm the block's presence in the response text before claiming task completion.
- After completing an exhaustive research session with a 'structured output' requirement, explicitly emit the full STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START...END block with all required fields (FINDINGS, SOURCES, CLAIMS) before ending the session; treat this block as a hard requirement, not optional.
- After completing an exhaustive research task, always emit the final structured output block (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END) with full findings, sources, and claims before ending the session, and explicitly confirm delivery of the formatted result to the user.
- After completing exhaustive research sessions with structured output requirements, explicitly state the final structured output block in a separate marked section before concluding, ensuring the deliverable is unmistakably present in the transcript for verification.
- After completing multi-phase research sessions with web search + fetch + local inspection, always explicitly confirm that the structured output (FINDINGS, SOURCES, CLAIMS) was generated in full and is ready for delivery before closing the session.
- After completing multi-tool research sessions, always output the structured findings block (FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS) before concluding, even if synthesis is incomplete, so the user can assess result quality rather than tool execution alone.
- After completing the first pass of web search results (SERP position >3), deduplicate query variants and move immediately to internal/premium tools (NECP, rag-slack, engineering context) rather than repeating Boolean permutations on the same search engine.
- After each tool wave, log the set of unique sources found and the distinct claims extracted; if a new tool call is about to fetch a source already covered or search a query very similar to the prior one, explicitly decide whether to proceed or synthesize.
- After every 10 tool calls, pause to synthesize findings, assess coverage gaps, and confirm whether additional calls would yield new information; avoid mechanical tool repetition without hypothesis-driven direction.
- After every 15 tool calls in a research task, pause to assess coverage against the claim space ("Do I have enough sources to answer the core questions?") before continuing. Exhaustiveness is relative to the question, not absolute. If sources plateau (same claims repeated), begin synthesis rather than continue collecting.
- After every 5 tool calls in a research session, provide a brief bullet-point summary of findings so far, gaps identified, and next search direction. This prevents silent exploration and allows early course correction.
- After executing search or fetch tool chains, always output intermediate summaries (e.g., 'Found 12 results mentioning AI agent adoption; fetching 5 highest-relevance sources') before pivoting strategy, so the user can track progress and detect incomplete results early.
- After exhausting a tool category's search space (evidenced by repeated queries returning no new results or similar results), explicitly note saturation and pivot to the next tool category rather than re-running similar commands.
- After exhaustive research is complete, always emit the promised structured output format in full before concluding the session, with explicit labels (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END) and validation that all three sections (FINDINGS, SOURCES, CLAIMS) are non-empty.
- After identifying a productive tool pattern (e.g., RAG manuals + enterprise search APIs), do not abandon it for exploratory local filesystem searches without explicit user redirection. If a local tool fails, escalate to the user rather than iterating on inaccessible file paths.
- After identifying a temporal fact (e.g., person departed organization X on date Y), explicitly filter subsequent searches to exclude that person by adding time-bound exclusions to query logic or adding a note to the session context to prevent redundant searches.
- After multiple tool invocations in a research task, always synthesize findings into the requested output structure before moving to the next research phase, ensuring partial results are checkpointed and visible.
- After tool-gathering cycles, explicitly enumerate all claims discovered and their corroboration count (single_source vs. multiple_sources vs. none) before writing final structured output; iterate tool calls to fill gaps where claims lack independent verification.
- Always buffer and validate the complete STRUCTURED_OUTPUT block before emitting when the user specifies an exact output format; if near token limit, emit a progress summary first and request continuation rather than sending truncated structured output.
- Always call TaskOutput/poll for background task results immediately rather than announcing 'I'll check when it completes'. Use Monitor only for long-running processes (>30min), never as a substitute for checking a completed task.
- Always call ToolSearch or inspect tool schema before the first invocation of a new tool, even if the parameter names appear obvious; validate parameter names and required fields before executing production queries.
- Always check if content is already in-line in the user message before issuing separate Read tool calls; only use Read for genuinely external/remote files. Large in-line specs can be analyzed directly and streamed incrementally to save tokens and latency.
- Always check whether tool results are empty or error-state before continuing iteration; after 3 consecutive failed fetches to a URL pattern, explicitly state that those resources appear unavailable and pivot strategy rather than retrying similar queries.
- Always complete and deliver the final structured output before ending the session, even if confidence in the answer is moderate. If the session context supports generating output, do so explicitly and return the STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END block rather than describing work-in-progress findings.
- Always complete structured output blocks (XML, JSON, marked sections) before resuming narrative text, and validate that block schemas match the spec before responding. If context will run short, pause and ask for permission to continue in a new turn rather than truncating.
- Always conclude deep-research tasks by explicitly formatting and returning the final structured output block (JSON or similar) in the final turn's text response, even if intermediate turns executed many tool calls. Verify the requested output format is included before ending the session.
- Always conclude exhaustive research sessions by explicitly outputting the requested structured result (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END, JSON, Markdown table, etc.) before the final turn, and confirm completion with a summary statement.
- Always conclude research tasks with an explicit STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END block containing synthesized findings, even if incomplete, rather than stopping after tool invocation. Verify and echo back the required output structure before marking task complete.
- Always confirm delivery of required output formats (structured blocks, JSON, code) by explicitly echoing the opening delimiter before generating content, and explicitly closing with the closing delimiter. If the response will be long, state 'Output follows:' before beginning.
- Always confirm file access and validate that external file references (seeds, configs, context) are successfully loaded before proceeding with tool-driven research; if inaccessible, prompt the user to inline critical context.
- Always confirm file read success or provide fallback analysis plan within the same turn; if a referenced file cannot be accessed, explicitly state this and offer to proceed with analysis of provided spec text only, rather than silently continuing.
- Always confirm the definition and organizational scope of any internal initiative before synthesizing budget data across multiple teams; explicitly state whether claims span a single P&L or federated budget, and flag any corroboration gaps.
- Always deliver final structured output blocks exactly as specified in the prompt before concluding; if synthesis is incomplete, explicitly state gaps and confidence levels rather than truncating output.
- Always deliver final structured outputs inline and explicitly, restating the user's required format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START|FINDINGS|...|SOURCES|...|CLAIMS|...|STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_END) and confirming all required sections are populated before concluding a research session.
- Always deliver promised structured outputs (JSON, markdown tables, STRUCTURED_OUTPUT blocks) as the final turn before exiting a research/analysis session, even if content is partial; signal blockers explicitly rather than attempting unilateral workarounds.
- Always deliver promised structured outputs in the exact requested format before ending the session, with intermediate checkpoints: after 25% of turns, confirm format/structure with the user before continuing tool exploration.
- Always deliver structured outputs before ending a research session; if the session times out or requires iteration, explicitly state what was found so far and what remains incomplete, with a visible structured block showing partial results rather than leaving the user without synthesized output.
- Always deliver structured outputs explicitly in full before concluding the response, verifying that arrays (SOURCES, CLAIMS) are populated and each claim includes source attribution and recency_class before signaling task completion.
- Always deliver structured outputs or requested formats as the final action, explicitly confirming format compliance (e.g., 'STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START ... STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_END') before concluding; if format delivery is blocked, report the blocker and fallback plan rather than omitting it.
- Always deliver the complete promised structured output block (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END with FINDINGS, SOURCES, and CLAIMS) in the final turn, even if tool results are incomplete; explicitly flag any claims that could not be corroborated rather than omitting them.
- Always deliver the exact output format requested by the user before concluding a research or analysis session, even if incomplete. If findings are partial, wrap them in the structured format with a 'confidence' or 'completeness' field rather than omitting the format entirely.
- Always deliver the explicitly specified output format/structure before session end, even if data is incomplete; surface synthesis failures and intermediate results to the user rather than silently terminating.
- Always deliver the final output in the exact format requested by the user before concluding the session, even if prior tool calls failed; validate against the prompt's output specification and echo back key metadata (tool call count, source count, claim count) to confirm completeness.
- Always deliver the final requested output format (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks) as the last message in a research task, even if incomplete, showing all tool calls executed and partial findings discovered so far.
- Always deliver the final required output block or explicit structured summary before session end, even if data is incomplete; use 'PARTIAL_FINDINGS' or 'IN_PROGRESS' markers if final corroboration is pending, and explicitly list claims by confidence tier (high/medium/low corroboration) rather than abandoning the output format.
- Always deliver the promised structured output or final synthesis *within the session*, not after. If tool execution is incomplete or results are sparse, report that explicitly before ending, and offer to continue rather than leaving the user with tool execution logs and no conclusions.
- Always echo back the required output format (structured blocks, JSON, markdown) immediately after beginning a research or analysis task, and explicitly confirm format compliance before concluding; use a checklist if the output has multiple required sections.
- Always echo or summarize the content of files read during analysis before producing output, so the user can verify that the analysis is based on actual topic content rather than defaults.
- Always emit explicit reasoning ('SEARCH STRATEGY:', 'PIVOT RATIONALE:', 'EVIDENCE GAP:') between tool call batches, especially when switching between local and web resources or when returning to a previous tool modality to avoid appearing to repeat prior work.
- Always emit structured output (or intermediate checkpoints) in the same turn as tool execution completes, rather than batching results for an assumed final turn. If the session terminates unexpectedly, the user still receives partial findings.
- Always emit the complete final deliverable (all promised sections: FINDINGS, SOURCES, CLAIMS with closing delimiters) in a single atomic turn before declaring synthesis done; validate that STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_END appears and all arrays are non-empty before signaling completion.
- Always emit the complete structured output block (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START...STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_END) explicitly in the response text before concluding a research task, even if synthesis is still in progress; do not rely on implicit completion.
- Always emit the complete structured output block (including STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END markers and all required JSON arrays) as the final turn of research sessions, even if partial, to ensure verifiability of synthesis against raw tool findings.
- Always emit the final deliverable (structured output, code, report, etc.) in a visible turn BEFORE session completion; if using multiple turns to construct it, show at least a draft version mid-session so the user can verify format/content alignment early.
- Always emit the final deliverable artifact (formatted output block, code, report, or summary) as the last turn before concluding, with explicit signposting (e.g., 'FINAL DELIVERABLE BELOW') to ensure no ambiguity about completion.
- Always emit the final deliverable block (structured output, code file, or result object) explicitly in text immediately after tool execution completes, even if intermediate turns show only tool declarations; do not assume the user can infer completion from tool counts alone.
- Always emit the final requested deliverable (structured output blocks, code artifacts, reports) explicitly at the end of the session, even if it must be truncated or marked incomplete due to tool failures or data sparsity.
- Always emit the final structured deliverable (code, prose output, or data block) as an explicit, visible artifact at the end of the session, even if it was incrementally constructed. Verify that every promised output format from the initial prompt is delivered before the final turn.
- Always emit the final structured output block (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END) inline in the response text, not deferred to file or subsequent turns, and explicitly confirm block completion before declaring research finished.
- Always emit the final structured output block explicitly at the end of a research session, even if content must be truncated or marked as incomplete. Explicitly signal if the output block was not generated due to token limits or other constraints.
- Always emit the final structured output or deliverable explicitly as visible text in your final response before claiming task completion; never rely on implicit synthesis or assume the user can infer results from tool call names alone.
- Always emit the promised structured output block (or explicit 'UNABLE_TO_COMPLETE' notice with reasons) before ending the session; treat it as a non-negotiable deliverable, not an optional final step.
- Always emit the requested output structure/format at the START of your response, even if incomplete or partial, rather than producing preamble prose before the structured section. If analysis is ongoing, mark sections [INCOMPLETE] but commit to the format.
- Always emit the required output format explicitly before conducting research, verify format requirements match deliverables, and synthesize findings into the mandated structure before final turn — do not end with open loops or incomplete output sections.
- Always emit the required structured output (FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS blocks) as the final turn before completing a research session; validate that all output sections are populated and contain substantive synthesis before declaring the session complete.
- Always emit the required structured output block before ending the session, even if incomplete or low-confidence. Verify the output format matches the specified template (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END markers, all required fields) and display it in the final turn.
- Always emit the required structured output block before the final turn closes; use an intermediate synthesis checkpoint at turn 50% to validate claims meet corroboration thresholds before continuing search, avoiding orphaned evidence.
- Always end research sessions with explicit synthesis and output generation. Before completing a task, verify that all required output structures (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT blocks, JSON arrays, tables, etc.) have been populated and rendered in the final response, even if research is incomplete.
- Always execute all explicitly-requested 'read file' or 'gather context' instructions before synthesis begins; treat 'understand the landscape first' as a blocking requirement, not optional preparation.
- Always execute at least one tool call with visible results (log search query + result count) before claiming 'I will search in parallel'; if a tool fails, immediately report the error name and pivot to an alternative tool rather than silently continuing.
- Always execute internal tool probes (Jira, MCP queries, Slack searches) BEFORE beginning external research synthesis; if synthesis is incomplete, explicitly state which probes are pending and re-run them before claiming the session is ready to output, rather than deferring to post-synthesis execution.
- Always explicitly confirm delivery of required structured output blocks (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END, JSON arrays) by echoing section headers in final response before attempting to deliver content, rather than assuming the content wrapping is implicit.
- Always explicitly deliver the requested output format in full before concluding a research session, even if tool execution is complete; verify structured output compliance before final response.
- Always explicitly display the final structured output block or deliverable before terminating the session, even if it's large; include a summary statement like 'Research complete. Structured output follows:' to signal transition to required format.
- Always explicitly output the final structured result requested by the user at the end of the session, even if some tool calls are still in progress; use a summary note to indicate which sources are provisional or incomplete rather than omitting the output entirely.
- Always explicitly output the final structured/formatted result block immediately before ending the session, and confirm in plain text that the output is complete and ready for consumption.
- Always explicitly output the required structured response format (tables, JSON, markdown blocks) before or immediately after the final tool call; verify the closing delimiter appears in the response body, not in post-processing.
- Always explicitly synthesize and output required structured formats (JSON blocks, markdown sections, code blocks) before declaring research complete; do not end on tool invocations without delivering the mandated output container.
- Always flag ambiguous or non-standard technical terms before executing long research chains; ask the user to clarify the specific source, publication, product, or context (e.g., 'Is Jina's late chunking a published paper, a product feature, or an internal technique?') to avoid 30+ tool calls on a misdirected target.
- Always fully read seed context files and vocabulary bootstrap BEFORE executing any searches; establish scope and terminology first, then parallelize tool calls in informed batches.
- Always generate a best-effort STRUCTURED_OUTPUT block with all data successfully extracted, even if final validation or cross-referencing API calls fail; mark any unverified claims with 'corroboration:none' rather than abandoning output delivery.
- Always invoke Skill tool when entering any research, investigation, or exploratory task, even if already dispatched as a subagent. Skip only if an explicit SUBAGENT-SKIP-SKILLS marker is present; SUBAGENT-STOP is not sufficient.
- Always log intermediate findings and tool results in real-time during long research tasks. If a tool call fails or returns empty results, immediately communicate the blocker to the user rather than proceeding silently. Produce the promised structured output (or an error explanation) before ending the session.
- Always materialize structured output blocks (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END) before the final turn, and confirm delivery in the response text rather than relying on tool-call sequences alone; for long research tasks, synthesize and output findings after every 10-15 tool calls to avoid incomplete final delivery.
- Always output the exact requested structured format (XML/JSON blocks, markdown sections, etc.) at the end of research/analysis tasks, even if incomplete; if research fails, explicitly state that in the structured block rather than omitting it.
- Always output the final structured deliverable (tables, JSON, markdown blocks, or XML) immediately after tool research completes, before session conclusion. Do not defer synthesis to an implicit 'next turn'.
- Always output the final structured result block before the session ends, even if incomplete, to ensure the required format is fulfilled. Include a summary of tools attempted and any constraints encountered (e.g., 'internal tools unavailable, proceeded with WebSearch only').
- Always output the final structured result in the exact format requested before concluding a research session, even if intermediate findings are incomplete; explicitly confirm format delivery via echo-back or placeholder if data gathering is still in progress.
- Always output the promised structured format immediately upon completion, even if intermediate sections are empty. Do not rely on implicit format assumptions; if the user requests STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks or specific schemas, render the closing tag before ending the turn.
- Always output the requested structured format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START...END, JSON blocks, markdown tables, etc.) explicitly as the final turn of the session, even if research is incomplete; include a 'completion_status' field noting gaps and caveating incomplete data.
- Always output the user's requested structured format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END, JSON, XML, etc.) before concluding a research session, and confirm completion explicitly in narration.
- Always parse and validate constraint statements (especially negations like 'Do NOT') before executing any output-generating tools, and flag internally if a constraint conflicts with the task goal before proceeding.
- Always probe tool availability with a lightweight query before launching parallel calls; if a tool is down, fall back to alternates immediately rather than discovering the failure across multiple wasted attempts.
- Always produce and display the requested structured output format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START...STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_END) before continuing to new research phases; include a turn explicitly dedicated to synthesis and corroboration verification, with a visible checklist of required fields.
- Always produce intermediate synthesis or partial results after each cluster of tool calls; do not accumulate tool invocations without showing findings. If a session will involve many turns, checkpoint after every 5 tool calls with a preliminary 'FINDINGS_SO_FAR' block.
- Always read seed context files (seed-topic.md, vocabulary_bootstrap.json, etc.) in turn 1 before issuing any tool calls on research tasks; explicitly acknowledge the research direction and boundary in turn 2.
- Always read seed files, context files, or explicit direction documents before making exploratory tool calls; treat these as requirements clarification that can reshape the entire approach.
- Always render the complete structured output block (with all required sections) in the final turn of a research session, even if partial. If tool results are unavailable, explicitly state 'No findings retrieved' rather than omitting the output entirely.
- Always render the final deliverable block (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END, JSON, markdown table) explicitly in the response before declaring task completion, and announce when you are doing so. If tools are unavailable, explicitly note what could not be sourced rather than omitting it.
- Always render the final required output format (structured blocks, code, JSON, etc.) as an explicit, separate step at the end of a research/synthesis session; validate that all required schema fields (FINDINGS, SOURCES, CLAIMS) are populated before marking the session complete.
- Always request or verify availability of referenced input files before attempting synthesis tasks; ask the user to provide file paths, content, or upload mechanisms in the same turn as the synthesis request, and do not proceed until confirmed.
- Always return full structured output blocks, including FINDINGS, SOURCES, and CLAIMS sections, even if results are sparse or inconclusive. Do not assume the user can see tool call results directly; explicitly echo key findings back in natural language before and/or after the structured block.
- Always return structured outputs in the exact format requested before terminating the session; if the format is explicitly specified in the prompt (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks with FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS sections), confirm completion of that format before reporting task completion.
- Always return the explicitly requested structured output format (FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS) even if research is incomplete; include a 'completion_status' field noting gaps and confidence levels rather than silently omitting the deliverable.
- Always surface intermediate tool results in a checkpoint section before pivoting strategy or declaring completion; include the count and summary of each tool's findings. If a tool call fails or returns empty, explicitly report it rather than silently moving to the next tool.
- Always synthesize and deliver the promised output format before session end, even if research is incomplete; include a 'research_status' note (e.g., 'in_progress', 'limited_public_docs') rather than truncating without the required structure.
- Always synthesize and deliver the promised structured output or final artifact before exhausting tool budget; reserve at least 3 turns for assembly, validation, and formatting of results, and signal when synthesis is beginning.
- Always synthesize and emit the final required output block before turn count reaches 80% of expected session length; if research is incomplete, emit a partial STRUCTURED_OUTPUT block explaining what was found vs. what gaps remain, rather than leaving the user with only evidence of tool invocations.
- Always synthesize and return results immediately after tool execution completes; do not invoke tools without a follow-up turn that explicitly presents findings in the requested format, even if partial or incomplete.
- Always synthesize and return structured output to the user before session close, even if tool results are partial. Do not leave tool execution dangling; verify that all required output sections (FINDINGS, SOURCES, CLAIMS) are populated and formatted correctly before considering the task complete.
- Always synthesize and return the required output format immediately upon gathering sufficient sources (e.g., 3+ independent sources per claim), rather than continuing to loop through search queries indefinitely. Define explicit stopping rules in research prompts: 'Return structured output after sourcing N claims from M independent sources, or after 25 turns, whichever comes first.'
- Always synthesize and return the required structured output block immediately upon completing tool search phases, even if data is sparse; timestamp each tool failure or null result, and explicitly note gaps (e.g., 'paywalled sources', 'broken links') so the user understands what evidence exists vs. what was unreachable.
- Always validate JSON output syntax before returning structured data; use a JSON parser to verify well-formedness when structured output format is explicitly requested by the user.
- Always validate foundational claims and premises in research directions before beginning exhaustive investigation; flag potential factual errors (e.g., acquisition misattributions) immediately in turn 1 or 2, not mid-session.
- Always validate required parameters (namespace, search scope, date ranges) for all MCP tool calls before execution; maintain a parameter checklist for each tool type (NECP, rag-*, PIPELINE) to avoid syntax-driven rework.
- Always validate that the final structured/JSON output block is complete and well-formed before declaring the turn finished; if output is cut off, rewind and re-emit in smaller chunks rather than risk stall-flagging. For long outputs (>2000 tokens), proactively split into multiple turns or emit summary first then details.
- Always validate the factual premise of research questions before executing multi-turn investigations; surface assumptions explicitly and probe for contradictory evidence in first 3 turns, especially when investigating negatives ("why didn't X happen") that may be false premises.
- Always validate tool API signatures and required parameters before invocation; if uncertain, check documentation or perform a dry-run with explicit parameter logging before executing against live APIs.
- Always validate tool endpoint patterns and parameter formats against examples from prior successful calls before issuing requests to unfamiliar MCP tools; if documentation is unclear, issue a test call with minimal parameters first.
- Always validate tool input constraints (auth requirements, file format expectations, max line length) before invoking tools on untested data. For WebFetch on internal domains, default to authenticated NECP/RAG/search_api first. For Grep/parsing, check file format (minified vs. pretty-printed) via a lightweight head/preview tool before full invocation.
- Always verify that structured output templates explicitly required in the prompt are fully generated and included in the response; treat 'return ONLY as [format]' as a hard constraint that must be validated before session completion.
- Always verify the full structured output block renders completely before reporting research complete; for large payloads (50+ claims), break structured output into chunks or write to file, then return file path to user rather than rendering inline.
- Always verify tool availability and success criteria before executing sequences of commands; if a tool fails, explicitly diagnose the failure mode (permission denied, command not found, syntax error) and pivot to an alternative tool class before retrying similar commands.
- Before accepting a research prompt with specific named entities (companies, studios, people), scan the prompt for internal consistency and flag embedded false premises (e.g., 'Atomic Arcade acquired by Netflix in 2025' when it was actually a WotC shutdown) as a clarification checkpoint before executing full-scale tool searches.
- Before accepting a research task, explicitly list available tools and verify their accessibility; if any required tool is unavailable, immediately inform the user and propose alternatives rather than proceeding with an incomplete toolkit.
- Before attempting a research task requiring access to internal/proprietary systems, explicitly list which tools are available and which (if any) are unavailable, inaccessible, or require authentication. If critical tools are unavailable, propose an alternative strategy using accessible sources or decline the task transparently rather than silently failing.
- Before attempting authentication-required tool calls (Jira, GitHub Enterprise, Confluence), first execute a lightweight 'probe' call (e.g., mcp__jira__getAccessibleAtlassianResources or a test curl with timeout) to confirm service availability and credential scope; if the probe fails, immediately pivot to fallback sources rather than retrying the same endpoint multiple times.
- Before attempting external web fetch/file access tools, always make one test call first and trap errors gracefully; if the tool is unavailable, pivot to alternative sources immediately rather than retrying the same tool consecutively.
- Before attempting internal/authenticated tool calls (Jira, Confluence, proprietary APIs), explicitly check tool availability and auth status; if unavailable after one attempt, immediately pivot to external-only strategy and log the decision rather than retrying failed auth.
- Before attempting to use specialized tool categories (engineering context, internal documentation, pipeline inspection) mentioned in a research prompt, first confirm their existence and accessibility with a targeted test call or contextual check; if unavailable, focus remaining effort on confirmed-accessible tools rather than speculative calls.
- Before beginning exhaustive research on a specific named system or timeline, validate that the system exists and is referred to by that exact name, or explicitly ask the user to confirm the entity name and suggest alternative names to search if initial queries yield no results.
- Before beginning exhaustive research tasks, explicitly confirm tool availability and constraints in the session environment; if requested tools are unavailable, propose alternative search strategies and expected coverage gaps upfront rather than proceeding under a false mandate.
- Before beginning research or tool-heavy tasks, explicitly confirm (via a single clarifying message or status output) which tools are available in the environment, or request the user provide a priority-ordered fallback tool list. Do not spend more than 2 turns searching for tools; escalate or pivot immediately.
- Before beginning synthesis of large research outputs, generate a mental outline of all sections (FINDINGS, SOURCES, CLAIMS) and commit to completing all three in a single turn; if length risk exists, split across turns but mark checkpoints explicitly ('Section 1 of 3 complete, proceeding to Section 2').
- Before calling WebFetch on internal URLs or authenticated systems, check tool capability documentation or run a lightweight ToolSearch query to confirm the target is world-accessible. If authentication is likely, immediately pivot to MCP tools (netflix_search_data, rag-manuals, rag-slack) rather than retrying failed fetches.
- Before calling an unfamiliar MCP tool for the first time, emit a brief check-plan in a thinking block that lists the expected parameters, required namespaces, and error modes, then execute. This prevents namespace mismatches and reduces retry cycles.
- Before calling specialized internal tools (RAG services, Slack search, internal APIs), verify availability or catch failures gracefully without retrying identical calls; pivot to alternative sources immediately.
- Before calling specialized internal tools (RAG, engineering context, MCP bridges), verify tool availability and required parameters by attempting a minimal query first; if the tool fails, pivot to confirmed-working alternatives rather than retrying identical calls.
- Before calling specialized/namespaced MCPs (e.g., mcp__sourcegraph__nls_search), verify exact parameter signatures in available tool documentation or use a simpler fallback variant first if signature is uncertain.
- Before composing a large structured output block after extensive research, pre-compose the full output in memory and estimate remaining token budget, issuing a warning if fewer than 4000 tokens remain. If budget is tight, deliver structured output incrementally (FINDINGS first, then SOURCES, then CLAIMS) rather than risking a truncation stall.
- Before composing lengthy structured outputs with multiple sections, estimate token requirement for each section and verify sufficient budget remains; if near limit, truncate gracefully or signal incompleteness explicitly rather than cutting mid-sentence.
- Before conducting exhaustive research, ask the user which tools have access to internal systems vs. public web, and confirm the definition of domain-specific terms (e.g., 'UQ', 'calibration claims') to avoid wasted exploratory turns.
- Before dispatching tool calls, validate tool availability with a dry-run or metadata lookup; if a tool fails, immediately pivot to a confirmed alternative rather than retrying the same tool multiple times in sequence.
- Before dispatching web searches, explicitly enumerate all available tool types (code search, documentation search, engineering context, chat search, pipeline inspection, web search, web fetch) and attempt at least one call per type, in priority order, before pivoting to web-only strategies. If an internal tool returns no results, log the decision to move to web search with reasoning.
- Before executing a long multi-turn research session, validate the factual premises of the research question (e.g., confirm launch dates exist and are in the future). If premises appear invalid or outdated, flag this immediately and request clarification rather than conducting 78 turns of investigation on a flawed foundation.
- Before executing a multi-turn research campaign (>10 turns of data collection), explicitly map research questions to tool strategies and set convergence criteria (e.g., 'stop when 3+ independent sources corroborate a claim') to avoid redundant tool calls and mid-session drift.
- Before executing a multi-turn research session on an internal topic (team, product, initiative), spend one turn validating the entity exists and clarifying acronyms, asking the user if necessary, rather than committing tool budget to potentially undefined searches.
- Before executing a query against an internal resource, check access/metadata first (via get_table_metadata or a lightweight test query); if access is denied, immediately document the blocker and pivot to an alternative source rather than attempting the full query.
- Before executing a research task spanning 40+ tool calls, always explicitly confirm the existence and scope of the target artifact (e.g., 'CLAUDE.md files') and clarify whether the user has a pre-existing corpus or expects the AI to discover/synthesize one.
- Before executing any exploratory tool calls, explicitly state your understanding of the required output format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END, FINDINGS, SOURCES, CLAIMS structure) and confirm that you will synthesize and return results as inline text, not files, at the end of the session.
- Before executing any tool calls, inventory available tools, test tool access (especially RAG-based or specialized tools), and confirm parameter formats with a single test call per tool type rather than retrying failed access patterns across multiple turns.
- Before executing batch tool calls in research workflows, run a single test call to each tool class (e.g., one Jira query, one NECP call, one WebSearch) to validate authentication and availability; only then proceed with parallel exhaustive calls.
- Before executing broad exploratory searches, ask the user to confirm: (1) whether the target data is in source code, operational systems, or both; (2) the primary storage system (databases, logs, APIs, codebase); (3) time granularity and scope. This prevents 5-10 wasted exploratory turns when the right tool is unknown.
- Before executing exhaustive multi-source research on private individuals or internal corporate figures, explicitly confirm that the research targets are public personas, published thought leaders, or have given consent to be researched; flag any research on internal-only individuals (those not mentioned in public Netflix communications) as requiring access validation.
- Before executing exhaustive multi-source research, spend one turn clarifying metrics definitions (e.g., 'capacity split' means total GPU count per region, not percentage or cost) and desired recency to avoid redundant searches across misaligned targets.
- Before executing exploratory searches, first use engineering context or repository listing tools to establish explicit product/system names and teams, then target subsequent searches with validated identifiers rather than generic keywords.
- Before executing file-write operations (TodoWrite, FileWrite, etc.), explicitly verify that the prompt permits file creation; if the prompt contains 'Do NOT write any files' or equivalent, skip all write operations and consolidate output for text-only response.
- Before executing searches in a research task, enumerate available tools and their expected coverage (e.g., 'session_search covers internal transcripts, ToolSearch covers code/docs, web_fetch covers external URLs') to avoid speculative tool calls and pivot faster to high-signal sources.
- Before executing tool calls on unfamiliar internal systems, query tool documentation or metadata (via ToolSearch or equivalent) to confirm data coverage and parameter requirements, rather than trial-and-error across 5+ speculative calls.
- Before executing tool calls, validate all required parameters (namespaces, query syntax, field mappings) against the tool's schema to avoid runtime errors; document expected parameter structure in a quick reference before tool invocation loops.
- Before fetching URLs in research sessions, validate URL accessibility patterns (internal vs. external) and skip or flag unresolvable internal endpoints rather than retrying failed fetches—immediately pivot to alternative sources and log the inaccessible URL in findings.
- Before generating large structured outputs (especially JSON with nested arrays), estimate total output tokens needed. If approaching budget limit, output FLAWS array first as a complete, valid JSON object in isolation, then state explicitly 'GAP_REPORTS will follow in next turn' rather than attempting to fit everything in one turn and risk truncation.
- Before initiating web searches on proprietary products, always enumerate internal data sources (MCP tools, databases, code repos) and confirm they lack relevant data; external web search should only be a fallback when internal tools confirm data absence.
- Before initiating web-fetch-heavy research, quickly probe tool reliability with a single test call; if WebFetch fails, immediately switch to Bash/curl for primary-source retrieval to avoid rework cycles.
- Before invoking WebFetch on external URLs, check URL domain and file type (e.g., Google Docs, Confluence, paywalled sites) that commonly require authentication; if auth-only, pivot immediately to internal RAG or API alternatives rather than attempting fetch.
- Before invoking a tool for the first time in a session, read or recall its full schema/parameters rather than inferring them; verify namespace, filter, and pagination requirements in a single dry-run if the tool is unfamiliar.
- Before invoking any tool in a research/investigation session, always retrieve and log its full signature (required parameters, optional parameters, output format) from tool discovery metadata. If signature is unavailable, make a low-cost test call with minimal parameters and inspect the error response to infer API contract before scaling to production queries.
- Before invoking internal knowledge bases (RAG, Slack, manuals) for entity-specific queries, verify in a preliminary web search that the entities exist and are publicly documented; only escalate to internal systems if public reconnaissance confirms relevance.
- Before invoking internal/MCP tools, validate API signatures and required parameters in a dry-run or schema check; if a tool is unavailable or fails, explicitly notify the user of the constraint change and confirm the revised research strategy before continuing.
- Before invoking specialized internal tools (RAG APIs, code search, Slack queries), retrieve and validate their required parameters/namespaces in a planning turn to avoid wasted invocations and self-correction cycles.
- Before invoking unfamiliar tools (Agent, ToolSearch, subagent modes), validate availability with a dry-run or explicit capability check. Report tool errors immediately to the user rather than silently continuing.
- Before invoking web fetch or auth-gated tools, scan URLs for common auth patterns (google.com/docs, internal wikis, OAuth-protected services) and prefer search/RAG tools as fallback; log failures with 'auth_gated' reason to avoid retry loops.
- Before issuing 5+ sequential queries to a single data source, explicitly test 1-2 queries first and evaluate whether results are present/actionable; if the first query returns empty results, pivot tool/strategy rather than retry the same source with minor phrasing variations.
- Before launching a multi-turn research session with 5+ tool calls per direction, validate the core premise with a single focused query. If the prompt assumes a specific forum/system/outcome exists, search its charter/description first to confirm it operates as described.
- Before launching broad multi-tool searches in research tasks, explicitly ask the user to confirm whether internal/private sources are accessible and should be prioritized, to avoid misdirected effort on public-only results.
- Before launching broad search queries, confirm the search index domain and result cardinality via a narrow test query; if results exceed 100 items, add explicit filters or pagination rather than brute-force retrieval and post-hoc extraction.
- Before launching exhaustive multi-turn research, perform a rapid 2-turn premise-validation phase: execute 3–5 queries across high-confidence sources (Sourcegraph, RAG archives, recent web results) to verify the core claim is not already falsified or inverted, and flag any contradiction to the user immediately rather than proceeding under a false assumption.
- Before launching exhaustive multi-turn research, validate whether the research question has measurable public or internal evidence; if evidence is sparse, shift to mapping the landscape of unknowns (what would need to be true, where gaps exist) rather than chasing single-source fragments.
- Before launching tool queries on an internal enterprise research task, explicitly confirm scope boundaries and tool-selection rationale (e.g., 'Focusing on rag-slack-prod and rag-manuals-prod for migration narratives; deprioritizing code search as MDM repos already documented') to avoid redundant lookups and justify deviations from stated tool diversity.
- Before launching tool queries, spend 1–2 turns searching for tool schema/documentation in parallel, then execute data queries in a coordinated batch rather than iteratively. This amortizes discovery cost.
- Before launching web search loops, read and parse any provided context files (e.g., vocabulary_bootstrap.json, seed-topic.md) and extract key search terms and constraints to inform tool selection and query strategy. Verify all promised tool categories are available and attempted before executing primary searches.
- Before making multi-tool research calls, validate MCP namespace syntax against the system's actual tool registry by making a single exploratory call first, then parallelize subsequent calls once format is confirmed. This prevents batch failures.
- Before making the first call to any MCP tool with complex schema, read the tool's definition and list required parameters aloud. Validate parameter names and types against the schema before execution to prevent schema validation errors.
- Before making tool calls, always load and read the complete tool schema/signature for unfamiliar APIs (especially RAG tools with namespace/query parameters), and perform a single test call with minimal arguments to validate signature before scaling to parallel calls. Avoid retry loops on the same approach when file format or API error patterns suggest a structural mismatch.
- Before making web search calls on internal system architecture questions, attempt at least 3 internal-only search modalities (ToolSearch, session_search, Bash grep on local docs). Reserve WebSearch for vocabulary bootstrap or public context only; document the decision if web search is necessary.
- Before pivoting research strategy from internal tools to web search, explicitly confirm the scope boundary: ask whether internal confidential sources (code repos, internal wikis, chat logs) are in scope, and if not, state that research will rely exclusively on public sources.
- Before pivoting to web search for internal organizational data (headcount, org structure, confidential memos), exhaust all local documentation sources first and explicitly assess whether the information is public or confidential in nature.
- Before querying new data sources, verify the schema and sample row count against the research goal to avoid pursuing dead-end tables; if a table has <10 rows or unexpected structure, explicitly query an owner or documentation before investing 10+ turns.
- Before starting multi-turn analysis of large specs, estimate output size and token budget. If budget is tight, emit a warning and ask the user whether to proceed, summarize first, or split the task across multiple sessions.
- Before starting synthesis on large prior-work de-duplication tasks, output a brief checklist of detected prior dimensions and identified gaps in the first response, to confirm alignment with the user and surface ambiguities in 'genuinely new' before spending compute on file I/O.
- Before using unfamiliar internal tools (Slack RAG, Netflix APIs), make one exploratory call first with verbose error handling to understand the API contract (authentication, namespace format, query syntax) rather than retrying blindly after failures.
- Define explicit termination criteria for research tasks: specify a target number of independent sources per claim, a confidence threshold per finding, and a hard turn/token limit; require the AI to validate coverage against these criteria before delivering structured output.
- For analysis tasks with structured output formats, explicitly check output completeness before marking the turn complete. If a stream is truncated mid-JSON or mid-structure, regenerate or continue rather than submitting incomplete output.
- For deep research tasks requesting structured output with claims/sources/corroboration, synthesize and output the final structured block explicitly in the final turn, confirming format compliance and inviting the user to request specific claim verification or source expansion before closing.
- For deep research tasks requiring exhaustive tool usage, establish explicit 5-10 turn batching cycles with mandatory synthesis output at cycle boundaries to prevent drift and ensure intermediate corroboration checks before final compilation.
- For deep research tasks requiring structured output, explicitly confirm and display the final structured block before concluding the session, even if intermediate findings are substantial. This prevents ambiguity about whether the promised output format was delivered.
- For deep research tasks spanning 50+ turns, maintain a visited-artifacts registry and actively avoid re-probing the same file/search pattern within 10 turns; prefer breadth-first exploration of new paths until evidence saturation is reached.
- For deep research tasks targeting proprietary/undocumented systems, proactively surface confidence levels and note gaps in available information. If after systematic searching no authoritative sources exist, explicitly state 'no public documentation found' rather than proceeding to output phase with potentially speculative findings.
- For deep research tasks with explicit tool call quotas, implement a tool-call counter and checkpoint summary every 10-15 turns to avoid diminishing-return queries; if tool diversity goals are met and results plateau, explicitly signal to the user before continuing.
- For deep research tasks with multiple search directions, organize tool calls into explicit batches separated by brief synthesis turns, and track evidence thresholds (corroboration count, recency class, source diversity) before declaring a direction complete.
- For deep research tasks with ranked tool priorities, invoke codebase/internal tools (Grep, code search, engineering context) in the first wave, then expand to web sources. Reserve web search for corroboration and external validation only if internal sources are exhausted.
- For deep-research prompts requiring structured synthesis (FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS), always materialize and display the final structured output block inline in the response before concluding, rather than deferring it or embedding it in external files.
- For deep-research tasks lasting 40+ turns, insert brief (2–3 line) 'coverage checkpoints' every 10 turns to explicitly list which sources have been searched and which gaps remain; this prevents redundant searches and keeps the user informed of progress without requiring manual status requests.
- For deep-research tasks requesting 'exhaustiveness,' establish explicit stopping criteria upfront such as: 'Continue searching until claims reach ≥2 independent sources or 3 completed searches yield diminishing returns.' This prevents ambiguity about when sufficient evidence has been gathered.
- For deep-research tasks requesting exhaustive cross-referencing, explicitly log and display corroboration chains inline during the research phase (e.g., 'Claim: QPS = 50k. Source A: X. Independent corroboration: Y.') before final synthesis, and include a summary of claim confidence by corroboration count in the final output.
- For deep-research tasks targeting proprietary regulatory documents (FDA submissions, medical device audits, confidential validation studies), explicitly ask the user upfront whether they have access to paywalled sources, internal databases, or regulatory portals; if not, acknowledge in turn 1 that certain claims will be corroborated only via press releases, SEC filings, and published research rather than primary documents.
- For deep-research tasks with exhaustive tool requirements, document the tool budget and ordering strategy upfront: specify target tool counts per phase, preferred source hierarchies (e.g., internal docs > papers > blogs), and minimum corroboration thresholds for claim acceptance to reduce subjective inference.
- For deep-research tasks with explicit tool count mandates, explicitly log and tally tool calls by category (internal/external/validation) at each major phase boundary, and alert if any named tool category (e.g., 'pipeline inspection', 'engineering context') remains unused after exploring sources.
- For deep-research tasks with explicit tool multiplicity requirements, structure tool invocations in rotating cycles across tool families (documentation → context → messaging → codebase → web) rather than exhausting one tool before moving to the next, to maximize corroboration signal and minimize redundancy.
- For deep-research tasks with explicit tool-call volume targets, batch similar queries together and report tool-call counts at key checkpoints (e.g., 'Deployed 4 Netflix search API calls, 5 rag-manuals queries, 3 web searches so far') to demonstrate compliance and allow mid-session priority shifts.
- For deep-research tasks, after 80% of allocated tool budget is consumed, synthesize findings into draft structured output and ask user whether additional sources would change claims/corroboration ratings before continuing exploration.
- For deep-research tasks, explicitly verbalize tool-selection criteria at phase transitions (e.g., 'Switching from local code exploration to external documentation because X') to help the AI justify tool choices and allow the user to validate strategy mid-execution.
- For design review tasks with rigid structured output requirements and large spec inputs, proactively split delivery across multiple responses and confirm turn-count budget with the user before beginning analysis, especially if total tokens may exceed 60% of context limit.
- For exhaustive internal research tasks lasting >20 turns, include a turn-by-turn tool selection log in intermediate summaries (every 10 turns) that justifies why switching tools or retrying searches, to improve transparency and allow course correction.
- For exhaustive multi-directional research tasks, structure tool calls into logical phases (discovery, deep-dive, corroboration) and log a summary of findings between phases before continuing, to prevent information loss and ensure structured output is produced even if session terminates early.
- For exhaustive multi-source research tasks (3+ tools, 10+ queries), use a persistent accumulation pattern: maintain a local working notes buffer (via TodoWrite or internal state) to track findings by source, mark queried topics to avoid redundant re-querying, and consolidate findings into synthesis buckets before final structured output.
- For exhaustive multi-source research tasks with no explicit stopping criterion, emit a brief checkpoint summary every 8–10 tool calls (e.g., 'Sources hit: RAG-manuals, NECP, search-api. Coverage: 12/17 product areas confirmed. Next: validate incidents, talent, competitive positioning') to maintain user visibility and allow early course correction.
- For exhaustive multi-turn research tasks: after every 4–5 tool-calling turns, emit an explicit 'sufficiency checkpoint' (e.g., 'I have found X claims from Y independent sources; proceeding with Z more targeted calls') to signal progress and enable early exit if the user is satisfied.
- For exhaustive research prompts listing specific tools, explicitly execute calls to each named tool category (web search, documentation, chat/messaging, engineering context, pipeline tools) even if results may be empty, rather than defaulting to available tools only.
- For exhaustive research prompts, track tool call counts per category (RAG vs Slack vs web) and explicitly log search term evolution in reflections to demonstrate coverage iteration and prevent redundant queries.
- For exhaustive research requests with tool-use emphasis, execute tool calls in rapid parallel batches (3-5 per turn) with explicit phase labeling rather than single tool calls per turn; this reduces turn overhead while maintaining clarity and maximizes the productive-turns ratio for token-constrained sessions.
- For exhaustive research tasks flagged 'MAXIMUM thoroughness' or 'aggressively,' begin parallel tool calls by turn 3 without exploratory pauses; batch related queries (e.g., all Netflix context calls, then all RAG calls, then web searches) to maximize information flow and reduce latency.
- For exhaustive research tasks marked with explicit tool-count minimums, commit to a documented tool-call budget (e.g., 'I will make 50+ tool calls') and report final count before synthesis; this surfaces search strategy confidence and satisfies audit trails for due-diligence research.
- For exhaustive research tasks over 50+ turns, implement explicit evidence-collection checkpoints every 10-15 turns with documented source counts and claim coverage gaps to prevent redundant search cycles in later stages.
- For exhaustive research tasks requesting structured output with source corroboration, alternate systematically between broad public-source searches (phase 1: web), internal codebase searches (phase 2: code), and verification passes (phase 3: targeted web + code), rather than interleaving randomly, to minimize redundant tool calls and maximize convergence speed.
- For exhaustive research tasks requesting structured output, always conclude by explicitly generating and returning the promised STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START...END block before reporting completion; include explicit counts (e.g., '42 sources found', '87 claims extracted') to signal output readiness.
- For exhaustive research tasks requiring cross-referenced claims, execute tool calls in distinct waves with explicit pause points between waves to assess information density and adjust search vocabulary; document rationale for each wave's keyword selection to enable audit trail.
- For exhaustive research tasks requiring multi-source validation, batch tool calls by hypothesis/subtopic and explicitly confirm source corroboration before synthesis; use recency_class and corroboration tags defensively to disambiguate single-source vs. empirically-grounded claims.
- For exhaustive research tasks requiring structured output, explicitly track progress through tool categories (code_search: 0/n, web_search: 5/10, doc_search: 2/5) and emit a mid-session summary before final synthesis to ensure context coherence and prevent off-transcript completion.
- For exhaustive research tasks requiring structured output, generate and explicitly present the final STRUCTURED_OUTPUT block before session completion, with visible source URLs and corroboration evidence. Validate all required fields are populated before declaring the research phase complete.
- For exhaustive research tasks spanning multiple tools, log intermediate synthesis after each tool cluster (e.g., after all RAG calls, briefly summarize findings and note gaps before moving to NECP). This prevents redundant queries and confirms the 5-10 call target was met with intent, not accident.
- For exhaustive research tasks with aggressive tool directives, cache search parameters and track which sources have been queried to avoid redundant API calls; resume with orthogonal queries or refined filters rather than repeating prior searches.
- For exhaustive research tasks with ambiguous scope definitions, explicitly enumerate inferred scopes (e.g., 'routing = LLM model selection, task prioritization, and fallback escalation') and request user confirmation before investing 40+ tool calls; surface assumptions at turn 3–5 rather than embedding them implicitly.
- For exhaustive research tasks with explicit deliverable structure, always conclude by generating the required structured output block (FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS) in full before reporting session completion, and verbally confirm all data is present before marking done.
- For exhaustive research tasks with explicit tool budgets, batch parallel tool calls in coordinated groups (search first, then fetch results) rather than interleaving, and emit a turn-by-turn tool execution summary before entering synthesis phase to allow early validation of search strategy.
- For exhaustive research tasks with explicit tool minimums, track tool diversity (number of distinct tool types deployed) and source diversity (number of independent corroboration paths) in running commentary; report when shifting tool categories (e.g., 'external sources sparse; escalating to internal APIs') to show adaptive methodology.
- For exhaustive research tasks with explicit tool prioritization, enumerate and invoke all local context tools (code search, documentation, engineering context, pipeline inspection) before web search, and synthesize findings into the required structured format before session termination.
- For exhaustive research tasks with explicit tool use mandates, maintain a visible search strategy log in thinking blocks — name the search categories targeted (academic, vendor, internal, product) and track coverage to ensure systematic parallel exploration rather than sequential greedy search.
- For exhaustive research tasks with multi-turn tool execution, consolidate synthesis/planning into fewer turns and batch tool calls when possible to reduce token overhead; track keyword hypothesis refinement explicitly to avoid redundant searches.
- For exhaustive research tasks with multiple search modalities (web + code), explicitly alternate tool types (e.g., WebSearch→Sourcegraph→WebSearch) to maximize corroboration surface area; document query intent per search to guide tool selection and reduce redundant retries.
- For exhaustive research tasks with multiple tool types available, execute searches in phases: (1) broad public web sources, (2) internal/proprietary tools, (3) gap-filling targeted queries. Halt when corroboration patterns stabilize rather than continuing indefinitely.
- For exhaustive research tasks with multiple tool types, structure queries into discrete 'research waves' (internal → external → validation) and explicitly track which sources remain unexplored until all major tool categories have been queried; add a summary checkpoint at 50% completion to confirm adequate coverage and source diversity before deepening any single axis.
- For exhaustive research tasks with no explicit stopping criterion, confirm token/cost budget and wall-clock time constraints in the first response before launching multi-phase tool campaigns. If none specified, cap discovery at 'diminishing returns' (e.g., 3+ turns of fetching with <5% new unique information) and emit a checkpoint summary before proceeding to synthesis.
- For exhaustive research tasks with open-ended stopping criteria, explicitly track and report tool success/failure rate, number of unique sources found, and claims with single vs. multiple corroboration before outputting final structured result.
- For exhaustive research tasks with structured output requirements, always emit the final structured block (FINDINGS, SOURCES, CLAIMS) at the end of the session, even if partial, and explicitly confirm: (1) total tool calls made, (2) whether saturation thresholds were met, (3) gaps in source coverage. Use a summary checklist turn before returning results.
- For exhaustive research tasks with structured output, verify the full output block is complete and parseable before concluding; if truncated, signal the issue and continue to completion rather than assuming success.
- For exhaustive research tasks with unbounded tool budgets, explicitly set a 'diminishing returns' checkpoint (e.g., 'stop when 3 consecutive tool calls return no new claims or sources') to avoid redundant searches while maintaining thoroughness.
- For exhaustive research tasks with unspecified success criteria, explicitly confirm scope boundaries (time horizon, product categories, corroboration thresholds) with the user before spending >50% of token budget, or risk delivering findings that don't meet unstated definitions.
- For exhaustive research tasks without explicit stopping criteria, propose a resource budget (token or turn limit) after the first synthesis phase and ask the user to confirm before expanding beyond it. Reference the budget in subsequent turns.
- For exhaustive research tasks, adopt a 4-turn rhythm: batch 3-4 parallel tool calls per turn, then synthesize findings in a reflective turn before the next batch. This prevents both circular searching and premature stopping.
- For exhaustive research tasks, after 10 tool calls, pause and summarize findings by hypothesis (e.g., 'Voyager architecture confirmed via X sources; transfer lessons: [list]; gaps: [list]'), then explicitly state which gaps remain and whether continuing yields diminishing returns.
- For exhaustive research tasks, construct and execute a multi-tool plan in turn 1: list 2-3 specific queries per tool (NECP, web search, code search) with target coverage areas BEFORE issuing any tool calls. Commit to equal distribution across sources unless evidence shows one is exhausted.
- For exhaustive research tasks, create a written search strategy map (topics × search keywords) before executing queries, then check off completed searches to avoid redundancy while maintaining coverage; this balances aggressive tool use with precision.
- For exhaustive research tasks, define a 'sufficiency threshold' early: specify minimum number of independent sources per claim (e.g., 2), minimum tool categories to query (e.g., 5), and explicit stopping conditions (e.g., 'stop when no new sources contradict or extend findings from previous 5 turns'). This prevents both premature synthesis and unbounded research spirals.
- For exhaustive research tasks, define an explicit stopping criterion upfront (e.g., 'stop when 3 independent sources corroborate each major claim' or 'stop after 40 turns unless new source categories are being discovered'). Track confidence per claim and terminate iteration once confidence thresholds are met, not just tool counts.
- For exhaustive research tasks, define stopping criteria explicitly (e.g., 'continue until 3+ independent sources corroborate each claim or 50 total calls reached') to balance thoroughness against token waste; include a note that the AI should announce when sufficient evidence is gathered rather than continuing past redundancy.
- For exhaustive research tasks, emit a final STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START…STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_END block in the final turn verbatim, even if synthesis is incomplete; signal when moving to synthesis phase with explicit turn commentary like 'Synthesizing findings now' to avoid ambiguity about completion.
- For exhaustive research tasks, establish a 'corroboration rule' in the initial system context: e.g., 'Mark claims as multiple_sources only if independently verified by ≥2 distinct sources with no editorial overlap; single_source if mentioned in only 1 source; none if contradicted or unverifiable. Prioritize peer-reviewed or archived sources over blog posts for technical claims.'
- For exhaustive research tasks, establish an explicit per-source corroboration threshold and document it in the structured output; e.g., 'Claims marked multiple_sources require at least two independent, non-marketing sources' to ensure reproducibility and clarity on evidence strength.
- For exhaustive research tasks, establish explicit corroboration thresholds upfront (e.g., 'multiple_sources' requires ≥2 independent domains; 'single_source' allowed only for recent ephemeral content) and recency cutoff dates before beginning tool invocations, to prevent late-stage redundant validation cycles.
- For exhaustive research tasks, establish explicit stopping criteria (e.g., 'stop when you've found 3+ independent corroborations per claim or exhausted source categories') and report against them in the final output to signal research completion confidence rather than continuing until token limits.
- For exhaustive research tasks, explicitly define upfront what counts as 'corroboration' (single source vs. multiple sources) and distinguish between 'mentioned in a paper,' 'implemented in open-source code,' and 'claimed in proprietary/blog context' as separate evidence classes; include these distinctions in the structured output.
- For exhaustive research tasks, explicitly log and summarize tool selection rationale before starting (e.g., 'Available tools: WebSearch, WebFetch, CodeSearch, DocSearch. Strategy: X searches on topic A, Y searches on topic B, fetch top N results, then verify with CodeSearch'). This ensures all requested tool categories are attempted or explicitly noted as unavailable.
- For exhaustive research tasks, explicitly log which available tools were considered but not deployed, and explain prioritization reasoning when skipping tool categories marked 'ALL available'.
- For exhaustive research tasks, explicitly log which specialized tools (code search, documentation search, engineering context) were attempted and why they were not used, if unavailable or inapplicable, to distinguish between tool unavailability and deliberate strategic choice.
- For exhaustive research tasks, explicitly narrate tool stratification strategy upfront (internal tools → vendor docs → OSS → web → academic) and execute batches of 4-5 parallel calls before synthesis pauses to maximize throughput and source diversity.
- For exhaustive research tasks, explicitly output the structured result block (with all FINDINGS, SOURCES, and CLAIMS sections fully populated) before concluding, even if the AI is uncertain about completeness, to ensure the deliverable is visible in the transcript and to signal completion to the user.
- For exhaustive research tasks, explicitly request tool output summaries in agent responses (e.g., 'After searching X, Y, Z: found N relevant papers') to verify that tool invocations returned substantive results, not phantom confidence.
- For exhaustive research tasks, explicitly return intermediate summaries and source inventories after each tool escalation phase (broad → targeted → deep-fetch) to allow user course-correction before final synthesis.
- For exhaustive research tasks, explicitly track 'direction completion checklist' in turn narration: for each research direction (e.g., 'max model size', 'p99 latency floor'), log the tool calls deployed and sources found before pivoting. This ensures balanced coverage across all sub-goals and prevents premature conclusion.
- For exhaustive research tasks, implement a turn-by-turn tool-call budget tracker and surface it in reasoning (e.g., 'Turns 11-19: 9 calls executed toward 5-10 target; proceeding to phase 2') to maintain transparency on thoroughness progress and avoid perception of undersearching.
- For exhaustive research tasks, implement explicit search phase boundaries with turn limits per phase (e.g., 'Phase 1: Broad discovery (max 15 turns), Phase 2: Deep validation (max 20 turns), Phase 3: Corroboration (max 10 turns)') to avoid open-ended tool drift while maintaining investigative thoroughness.
- For exhaustive research tasks, include explicit intermediate synthesis points every 8-12 turns (e.g., 'Turn 16: Summarize findings so far and identify gaps') and always ensure the final turn includes generation of the promised output format, not just a data collection tool call.
- For exhaustive research tasks, insert explicit reflection and cross-validation turns every 10-15 tool calls to summarize findings, identify evidence gaps, and adjust search strategy, rather than executing all tool calls in linear sequence.
- For exhaustive research tasks, maintain a running checklist of tool modalities (web search, web fetch, code inspection, documentation, academic papers) and turn counts; after each logical research phase, validate that all modalities have been used and minimum tool call targets have been met before pivoting to synthesis.
- For exhaustive research tasks, structure the session into explicit phases (broad discovery, targeted deep-dive, local validation, synthesis) and signal phase transitions to the user with turn commentary; this maximizes clarity and enables early feedback if coverage is off-target.
- For exhaustive research tasks, track tool diversity across turns: ensure at least 2 different tool categories (e.g., Bash + MCP + web search) are used before turn 15; pivot to domain-specific tools (MCP/internal APIs) by turn 20 if available. Log tool category diversity to avoid local search redundancy.
- For financial or cost-attribution research in large organizations, explicitly identify and document the authoritative cost model (EI dashboard vs chargeback vs budget system vs actual invoice) before synthesizing, and call out any misalignment between sources in the findings.
- For high-priority deep-research tasks with unbounded tool budgets, explicitly define a pivot point: once a high-confidence evidence anchor (e.g., a blocking ticket or memo) is found, switch from broad exploration to targeted corroboration of that anchor, rather than continuing to broaden the search horizon indefinitely.
- For high-priority research tasks with vague quantitative targets ('5-10 tool calls'), ask for clarification on total effort budget or per-source depth before executing, rather than inferring maximum effort and risking over-research or under-research.
- For internal research tasks spanning 3+ data sources, explicitly map source-to-research-question relationships upfront (e.g., 'NECP for org structure, Slack for incident patterns, Sourcegraph for code patterns') and reference this map in each tool-call narration to avoid redundancy and lost context.
- For internal-only research requests without external sources, explicitly confirm scope with the user before turn 5: 'Do you want me to search external web sources (Braintrust vendor docs, papers, public discussions) in addition to internal Netflix systems, or focus internal sources only?' This prevents misalignment on evidence source types and avoids wasted tool calls on unavailable sources.
- For large design document reviews with structured output requirements, confirm artifact availability (file paths, URLs, database queries) in the first turn before proceeding to analysis, and set explicit token/time budgets in the system prompt to ensure the analysis phase is not squeezed.
- For large design reviews (>300 lines), estimate token budget and explicitly plan flaw output in batches to avoid truncation; include a checkpoint after each major section.
- For large document analysis tasks requesting structured output, confirm token budget sufficiency and emit STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START immediately, then report flaw count and emit output incrementally to avoid mid-structure cuts.
- For large organizational audits (>10 product areas, peer relationship mapping), use explicit research stage gates: reconnaissance (breadth), depth (high-friction areas), synthesis (cross-reference), with turn budgets per stage. After each stage, explicitly summarize coverage gaps before pivoting.
- For large-file critique tasks (>1500 lines), explicitly acknowledge character/token budget constraints upfront and offer to chunk analysis into multiple responses if needed, rather than attempting full analysis and risking mid-stream truncation.
- For large-scale information synthesis tasks with explicit tool-call budgets, launch the first parallel tool volley immediately (within 1 turn); defer synthesis until after initial results are received, never before.
- For large-scale synthesis tasks (>100 findings), structure ingestion in three phases: (1) read coordinator/index to establish topology, (2) iteratively read detail files and build intermediate structures, (3) aggregate and write output. Confirm completion of each phase before moving to the next.
- For long internal-research tasks on unfamiliar systems, begin by listing key unknowns and disambiguation hypotheses (e.g. 'Is Pandora a masking engine, config system, or client library?') before deep-diving, then verify hypotheses via targeted grep on known files before broad exploration.
- For long research sessions >50 turns, emit structured output incrementally (per direction/major finding cluster) rather than deferring compilation to the final turn; this prevents loss of work if context limits are reached.
