- For long-running research sessions with truncated or missing final deliverables, explicitly confirm final output generation in a separate turn before marking completion, including a brief summary of claims count and source diversity achieved.
- For low-priority research directions on niche topics, establish a saturation criterion upfront (e.g., 'stop after 3+ independent sources corroborate each claim' or 'complete within 30 turns') to avoid over-investment relative to priority level.
- For multi-phase research tasks with explicit tool call minimums, organize searches into named waves with explicit pause-and-review turns (e.g., 'WAVE 1: General frameworks [5 searches], WAVE 2: Regulatory [5 searches]') to maximize cross-reference efficiency and avoid subtle redundancy in later phases.
- For multi-platform feature comparison research tasks, explicitly define the comparison matrix (e.g., list the 5-8 specific capabilities to evaluate) and source quality tiers (official docs > vendor blogs > community posts) before launching tool calls, to eliminate inference ambiguity and reduce redundant fetches.
- For multi-source research tasks with unspecified tool ecosystems, allocate 2-3 turns to parallel tool discovery (Read bootstrap files, ToolSearch in parallel) before executing substantive queries, then pivot immediately to N-way parallelization rather than sequential refinement.
- For multi-source research tasks, explicitly state tool selection strategy (which tool for which claim) before executing, and log all tool errors with a retry/fallback decision rationale before proceeding to synthesis.
- For multi-tool research sessions, organize searches into explicit thematic waves and announce the theme before executing each wave (e.g., 'Let me dig deeper into vector DB costs...') to signal deliberate coverage and maintain user confidence in thoroughness.
- For multi-turn exhaustive research tasks, always confirm the structured output delivery is complete and visible in the final turn; if the response risks truncation, explicitly summarize findings before formalization to ensure the user receives actionable data.
- For multi-turn research and synthesis tasks, show intermediate results (findings, sources found, partial claims) after every 3-5 tool calls, rather than deferring to a final synthesis phase. This ensures the user sees progress, enables course correction, and prevents loss of source attribution.
- For multi-turn research tasks with structured output requirements, synthesize and return the final structured block immediately after tool calls complete, even if data is partial, rather than allowing the session to end without the promised deliverable format.
- For multi-turn research tasks with unspecified termination, explicitly output intermediate structured results after each major phase (e.g., after every 10 tool calls) so the user can see real-time progress and signal when sufficiency is reached, rather than waiting until the end of a long session.
- For open-ended exhaustive research tasks, AI should periodically (every 15-20 turns) explicitly list tools deployed, categories of sources covered, and any known gaps, then ask user to confirm scope sufficiency before final synthesis.
- For open-ended exhaustive research tasks, establish an explicit stopping criterion by Turn 3 (e.g., 'Stop when three consecutive tool calls yield no new sources' or 'Stop at 30 tool calls') and propose it to the user for confirmation before heavy iteration begins.
- For open-ended research tasks longer than 20 turns, checkpoint output every 10-15 turns: at the checkpoint, deliver a partial FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS block and ask the user if additional directions are needed before continuing. This prevents sessions from running long without deliverable visibility.
- For open-ended research tasks requesting 'exhaustive' audit across 10+ dimensions, explicitly confirm scope and success criteria (e.g., 'Map all 17 pillars to owner, repos, and lifecycle stage; GPU allocation must include budget figures with sources') before tool execution begins, and provide a structured checklist at session start to avoid ambiguity-driven rework.
- For open-ended research tasks with 'exhaustive' or 'aggressive' directives, establish explicit stopping criteria (token budget, source saturation threshold, or claim count target) in the first clarifying turn before executing tool calls, and confirm with the user.
- For open-ended research tasks with 'maximum thoroughness' instructions, explicitly state stopping criteria after 20% of tool calls (e.g., 'I will stop when I have 3+ independent sources per claim or after 15 total searches, whichever comes first') to signal research planning to the user and enable course correction.
- For open-ended research tasks with explicit tool-call minima, include a turn-based budget check: after every 10 turns, explicitly state tool count achieved and confirm remaining directions require additional calls to meet the minimum.
- For open-ended research tasks with explicit tool-use requirements, escalate tool deployment systematically from broad to targeted searches, validate early papers before drilling into quantitative comparisons, and always deliver structured output blocks verbatim in the final response even if synthesis required multiple turns.
- For open-ended research tasks with tool-heavy directives, parallelize tool discovery (turns 1-11) by bundling ToolSearch calls and vocabulary bootstrap in a single composite query, then immediately pivot to primary research sources; avoid sequential initialization when the prompt permits concurrent setup.
- For open-ended research tasks with tool-use instructions, explicitly announce the research plan and phase boundaries at the start, then log tool call count per direction to the user every 10 turns to provide accountability and allow mid-course correction.
- For open-ended research tasks, after exhaustive tool exploration, explicitly synthesize findings into the requested output format (structured output, report, etc.) before declaring completion. Do not assume the user will prompt for synthesis—deliver it proactively once tool investigation reaches diminishing returns.
- For open-ended research tasks, define an explicit saturation criterion (e.g., 'stop when 3 consecutive searches yield no new sources') and a tool-switching rule (e.g., 'if a tool yields 0 results twice, deprioritize it') to avoid diminishing-returns grinding.
- For open-ended research tasks, define explicit stopping criteria (e.g., 'until you have at least 3 independent sources per framework' or 'stop when web searches return <5% new results') rather than relying on agent judgment alone.
- For open-ended research tasks, explicitly confirm tool selection strategy (batch vs. sequential, external vs. internal sources only) in the first clarification turn if scope or tool list appears ambiguous, even if the user's intent seems clear.
- For organizational audit tasks requiring roster/manager data: explicitly validate coverage per team (e.g., 'Found X of ~Y expected ICs for [sub-team]') and flag single-source findings before synthesizing structured output to improve transparency and allow user course-correction.
- For organizational audits with temporal qualifiers ('last 24 months', 'worst'), ask the user to define the ranking metric (e.g., 'worst' = max downtime hours, max users impacted, max data loss rows, or security severity) before beginning research, and output findings ranked by that metric.
- For organizational/internal research tasks, explicitly define tool priority order (internal-first), acceptable external data sources, and fallback thresholds before transitioning strategies to prevent scope creep or data quality compromise.
- For queries about individual employee status at large tech companies, prioritize Slack activity logs and code repository commits over employee directory searches; only use directory searches if the prior sources yield no activity within the past 18 months.
- For research directives requesting structured output (FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS blocks), explicitly confirm completion of all sub-questions before synthesizing, and signal in an intermediate turn when pivoting between research phases (e.g., 'High-level context gathered; now drilling into incident history').
- For research or multi-turn data-gathering tasks exceeding 20 turns, emit a brief progress checkpoint every 10-15 turns showing (1) tools used, (2) data acquired, (3) gaps identified, and (4) next direction. This prevents silent exploration loops and gives users confidence in execution.
- For research or synthesis tasks, always output the promised structured format (tables, JSON blocks, delimited sections) before exiting, even if tool data is partial. Verify the user's specified output format exists in your final response before considering the task complete.
- For research prompts with explicit format requirements (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END), preserve exact formatting and section names from the user's template, and avoid intermediate note-taking tools mid-research that create mental context switches; continue aggressive tool calls until the research phase is explicitly closed.
- For research sessions marked 'MAXIMUM thoroughness', emit brief one-sentence progress summaries before each tool batch (e.g., 'Querying engineering context for team ownership...') rather than empty commentary turns, to reduce non-productive output and keep token budget visible.
- For research sessions with explicit structured output requirements, always display intermediate findings and the final synthesized output block in the response, even if the full session continues longer. Confirm output format delivery explicitly before marking research complete.
- For research synthesis tasks requiring structured output, always explicitly call out the structured output block in the final turn before returning results, and verify its completeness (FINDINGS prose ≥3 sentences, SOURCES array ≥5 items, CLAIMS array ≥3 items with all required fields) before marking the task complete.
- For research tasks demanding 'maximum thoroughness' or 'exhaustive' coverage, always retry failed data source queries (especially NECP, rag-manuals, rag-slack) with exponential backoff before pivoting to alternative sources; log the retry attempt and document any unresolved gaps in the final output.
- For research tasks demanding structured output, explicitly generate and present the final FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS block in-turn before concluding, even if preliminary or partial, rather than assuming downstream tool results will be integrated automatically.
- For research tasks exceeding 30 minutes or 40+ tool calls, implement explicit checkpoint synthesis at turn intervals (every 10 tools), storing intermediate SOURCES and CLAIMS arrays to prevent redundant re-fetches and allow resumability if session times out.
- For research tasks flagged 'aggressive' or 'exhaustive', estimate tool call budget upfront (e.g., 'This task will require ~30–50 tool calls'), and report tool count at each major phase transition to prevent premature closure.
- For research tasks involving internal tools (MCP, Slack APIs, internal search), validate tool access and return data quality in first use before building analysis on those sources; explicitly log whether internal queries returned results or errors.
- For research tasks on proprietary systems, explicitly request source credibility tiers (e.g., official docs > blog posts > leaked artifacts > third-party inference) in the initial prompt to prevent over-weighting of speculative sources.
- For research tasks on proprietary/opaque systems, establish explicit corroboration thresholds upfront (e.g., 'single-source claims acceptable only for regulatory filings; engineering claims require 2+ independent sources') and declare when those thresholds cannot be met due to lack of public disclosure.
- For research tasks requesting 'MAXIMUM thoroughness' on proprietary systems, proactively ask for a domain terminology glossary (team names, internal tool names, repo structures) in the first turn before launching tool searches, to avoid 10+ corrective discovery turns mid-session.
- For research tasks requesting 'MAXIMUM thoroughness' or 'exhaustive' coverage, execute tool calls in explicit waves with diminishing-return detection: after 3 sequential searches return no new source URLs, pivot to different tool categories rather than re-querying the same terms.
- For research tasks requesting 'aggressive' multi-source gathering, identify the 3-5 highest-value tool sources in the first 2 turns via lightweight probing, then commit deeply to whichever sources return domain-specific data; avoid exploratory tool iteration beyond turn 5.
- For research tasks requesting 'exhaustive' analysis with structured output, explicitly confirm in the final response that all three claim-corroboration levels (single_source, multiple_sources, none) are represented, and that recency_class distribution is documented (e.g., 'X claims from 2026, Y from 2025, Z from 2024 or older').
- For research tasks requesting 'exhaustive' evidence gathering, emit a search plan (topics, sources, cross-reference strategy) before tool invocation, then execute in waves with explicit evidence-collection summaries between phases to maintain transparency and enable early user feedback if direction drift is needed.
- For research tasks requesting 'exhaustive' multi-tool investigation, explicitly confirm the structured output block was generated and returned in the final turn before marking completion; don't assume success from tool call volume alone.
- For research tasks requesting exhaustive multi-source corroboration with structured claims output, explicitly log tool call count and source diversity milestones mid-session (e.g., 'Deployed 12 web searches, 4 internal API queries, 3 Slack RAG searches') before final synthesis to ensure threshold compliance is visible and auditable.
- For research tasks requesting exhaustive tool use across internal systems, explicitly log each tool invocation with parameters and results (even null/error results) in real-time, and include a summary of tool call count and source coverage at the end of each major turn, so the user can validate that 'aggressive' tool use is actually occurring.
- For research tasks requesting exhaustive tool use, prioritize Jira/Confluence searches early (high signal-to-noise in enterprise settings) before attempting local Bash/Grep queries; if local code search returns sparse results after 2 attempts, pivot to web/documentation sources rather than retrying.
- For research tasks requesting exhaustiveness, establish explicit stopping criteria at the start (minimum source count, corroboration threshold, recency cutoff) and report against these metrics in the final output to reduce ambiguity around completeness.
- For research tasks requesting structured output in a specific format (FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS), explicitly draft the structured output block at the end of Turn 1 with placeholders, and iteratively populate it through subsequent turns, showing cumulative progress before session termination.
- For research tasks requesting structured output, define a tool-call budget and synthesis checkpoint: After exhausting tool budget or achieving diminishing returns on new sources, immediately synthesize findings into the requested output format before continuing search. Do not accumulate tool calls beyond the stated range without producing intermediate synthesis.
- For research tasks requesting structured synthesis, explicitly confirm tool invocation count and source diversity before generating final output; if actual tool calls fall below stated minimum (e.g., <5 per direction), surface the gap and either extend research or explain why the minimum was infeasible.
- For research tasks requiring 'exhaustive' cross-referencing, include explicit stopping criteria in the prompt: either 'stop when you have N corroborating sources per claim', 'stop when tool query results plateau (redundant answers)', or 'stop after K total tool calls', rather than leaving exhaustiveness open-ended.
- For research tasks requiring structured final output, explicitly render the complete STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END block in the final response before summarizing, even if partial, to allow verification that the output format requirement was met.
- For research tasks requiring structured output (FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS blocks), always echo back the final structured output in full before completing the turn, even if the response body is long, to allow users to verify format compliance.
- For research tasks requiring structured output delivery, always emit the final structured block in the final turn before concluding, and explicitly confirm completion of the output format requirements.
- For research tasks requiring structured output exceeding ~8KB, break final output generation into multiple turns: draft sections incrementally (one per turn), validate no truncation, then assemble. Never attempt to generate all sections in a single turn response.
- For research tasks requiring structured output in a specific format, execute a synthesis turn after the final tool call to confirm the output block is generated and well-formed before declaring task complete.
- For research tasks requiring structured synthesis output, set explicit search budget (e.g., 'max 15 web searches') and mandatory synthesis checkpoint after budget consumed. Do not enter open-ended search loops; after reaching search budget, immediately consolidate findings into the requested schema before returning.
- For research tasks requiring structured synthesis, mark tool-gathering phase complete before synthesis; explicitly enumerate which specialized tools are unavailable and pivot accordingly; draft the required output structure (FINDINGS/SOURCES/CLAIMS) incrementally during research, not after, to ensure final deliverable completeness.
- For research tasks targeting internal organizational data (budgets, metrics, confidential records), explicitly decline public WebSearch and clarify access scope before beginning; ask the user to confirm which internal systems contain the target information rather than guess.
- For research tasks with 'aggressive' tool directives, implement explicit search query expansion: after initial results plateau (2-3 rounds), auto-pivot to domain-specific internal APIs (vendor search, Slack RAG, manuals) without waiting for user redirect.
- For research tasks with 'exhaustive' or 'aggressive tool use' directives, explicitly structure tool waves (e.g., turns 1-6 setup, turns 7-25 wave 1, turns 26-35 wave 2) with synthesis pauses between waves, and output intermediate evidence summaries before final synthesis to validate coverage.
- For research tasks with 'exhaustive' scope, perform explicit search → assessment → pivot decision points after every 3-5 tool calls. State out loud what was found, what gaps remain, and what the next search variant should be. This structures open-ended research and prevents both premature stopping and infinite refinement loops.
- For research tasks with 'low priority' sub-questions embedded in larger audit contexts, ask the user to explicitly prioritize: 'Should I focus depth on the marked priority or attempt full coverage of all questions?' rather than defaulting to exhaustive multi-question investigation.
- For research tasks with a structured output requirement, set an explicit turn budget for synthesis (e.g., final 3–5 turns reserved for collating findings into the promised format) and signal transition to synthesis phase once tool calls yield diminishing returns, rather than continuing tool calls until token limit.
- For research tasks with corroboration requirements, establish explicit acceptance criteria for claim classification (e.g., 'single_source = one source, multiple_sources = ≥2 independent sources reporting same fact') before tool execution begins, rather than inferring thresholds.
- For research tasks with exhaustive data-gathering requirements, maintain a running tally of tool calls per direction and explicitly report progress (e.g., 'Completed 8 searches on vendor X pivots, 12 on timeline validation, moving to final cross-reference phase') before final compilation to reinforce methodological rigor and give the user confidence in thoroughness.
- For research tasks with explicit 'aggressive' or 'exhaustive' tool-use requirements, immediately launch external/remote tools (web search, fetches, APIs) in the first 2 turns; reserve local file reading for supplementary context only after external search is underway.
- For research tasks with explicit 'exhaustive' or 'aggressive' directives requiring structured output, batch tool calls into parallel waves of 4-5 per cycle, verify execution before transition turns, and deliver structured output immediately upon research saturation without waiting for additional prompts.
- For research tasks with explicit STRUCTURED_OUTPUT requirements, establish a hard stop-and-synthesize rule: after N productive tool calls or when evidence saturation is detected, immediately compile and return results in the prescribed format rather than continuing to search indefinitely.
- For research tasks with explicit multi-source corroboration requirements, establish and document the evidence threshold upfront (e.g., 'single_source claims require at least one independent mention in Slack or docs; major timeline events require Sourcegraph + NECP + RAG corroboration') before starting parallel queries, and summarize corroboration logic in the final structured output.
- For research tasks with explicit output schema (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END, JSON, or similar), produce and return the complete artifact before or immediately after the final tool call, not after intermediate investigation. Validate that FINDINGS, SOURCES, and CLAIMS are populated from gathered data before concluding the session.
- For research tasks with explicit output-format constraints, parse and internally flag forbidden tool categories (e.g., file-write tools when format is 'text response only') before beginning tool execution, and log rejection if those tools are invoked.
- For research tasks with explicit structured output requirements, allocate a final 'synthesis turn' that consolidates gathered evidence into the required format before concluding. Treat structured output blocks as non-negotiable deliverables, not optional extras.
- For research tasks with explicit structured output templates, confirm the definitions of baseline categories (e.g., 'leader-tier agents', 'persistent memory', recency thresholds) before dispatching tool calls, either by requesting clarification or by documenting assumptions in the final output's prose.
- For research tasks with explicit tool budgets ('at least 5-10 tool calls per direction'), skip local file reads unless they are critical for understanding scope; move immediately to external sources and track tool call counts visibly to ensure threshold is met.
- For research tasks with explicit tool call minimums, begin tool invocation immediately in turn 1-2 without a planning phase; move planning inline between tool calls if needed.
- For research tasks with explicit tool call minimums, include a running count of tool calls executed in intermediate reasoning steps, and explicitly confirm the threshold is met before returning final output.
- For research tasks with explicit tool diversity requirements, deploy at least one operation using each specified tool category before pivoting to dominant strategy; track tool usage against user spec and surface any omissions in interim reports.
- For research tasks with explicit tool lists and quantified requirements (e.g., '5-10 tool calls'), log each tool type used before execution and explicitly report if any required tool type is unavailable, rather than silently substituting with alternative tools.
- For research tasks with explicit tool-call quotas ('at least 5-10 calls'), explicitly count and report tool invocations before synthesis, confirming the quota was met before returning final output.
- For research tasks with inherent scope ambiguities (e.g., 'reaction,' 'relevant'), ask clarifying questions in the first turn before tool deployment if the prompt lacks explicit boundary definitions. Document assumptions explicitly in output.
- For research tasks with structured output requirements, always explicitly confirm delivery of the required format block before marking the task complete. Include a checklist in reasoning: 'Required output format: STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END ✓ FINDINGS ✓ SOURCES ✓ CLAIMS ✓'.
- For research tasks with structured output requirements, explicitly calculate minimum tokens needed for synthesis (rule of thumb: final output ~20-30% of total budget) before committing to tool execution loops, and terminate tool calls early if synthesis budget becomes constrained.
- For research tasks with undefined data access, explicitly clarify in turn 1 which data sources are queryable (Slack, Jira, internal APIs) vs. inferred, and note limitations in FINDINGS prose to avoid false confidence in inaccessible dimensions.
- For research tasks with underspecified scope (e.g., 'token-cost limits'), conduct a 2-3 turn clarification phase before entering aggressive tool deployment: ask the user to define key terms (consolidation rate, baseline assumptions, internal vs. external source priority) to avoid spreading search budget across tangential topics.
- For research tasks, always emit the final structured output block (FINDINGS/SOURCES/CLAIMS) before marking the turn as complete, with explicit confirmation that all required fields are populated. Do not rely on tool execution alone; finalize and surface deliverables in visible text.
- For research-heavy tasks requesting structured output with multi-source corroboration, parallelize tool calls by pillar/component (e.g., simultaneous Bash exploration of directory X + WebSearch for component Y + Read of README Z) rather than serial tool use; explicitly track corroboration sources (internal monorepo + external docs + team ownership) to satisfy cross-referencing requirements upfront and reduce mid-stream rework.
- For research-heavy tasks requiring exhaustive multi-source investigation, deploy tool calls in explicitly planned waves (broad discovery → targeted verification → deep technical investigation) and vocalize the strategy before execution to signal confidence and enable early course correction.
- For research-intensive sessions, output intermediate structured results after every 10-15 tool calls so the user can verify progress and corroboration quality without waiting for full completion; explicitly confirm delivery of required output format (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START block) with a visible success marker before session end.
- For research-only sessions with explicit structured output requirements, always echo the STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END block with fully populated FINDINGS, SOURCES, and CLAIMS sections before the final turn, even if intermediate results are incomplete—this ensures visibility of synthesis and prevents token-limit surprises from hiding final deliverables.
- For research-synthesis tasks with explicit tool-call minimums, establish explicit stopping criteria (e.g., 'stop when X frameworks documented with Y independent sources each' or 'continue for N minutes then synthesize') to prevent indefinite refinement loops and clarify when comprehensive coverage is reached.
- For research/analysis tasks with structured output templates, explicitly confirm completion and delivery of the template format at the end of the workflow before concluding the session, or ask the user for format validation.
- For research/discovery tasks, implement a multi-phase search strategy with explicit phase transitions: (1) broad discovery, (2) targeted deep-dives, (3) artifact validation. Pause between phases to reflect and adjust parameters based on findings, signaling progress to the user.
- For research/investigation tasks, after the first round of tool calls, explicitly summarize findings and pivot strategy if primary sources are unreachable, rather than repeating similar queries; checkpoint at ~25% through the task to validate direction alignment with user intent.
- For research/synthesis tasks requiring structured output, always log and validate the final output block before ending the session. Include a visible STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END wrapper in the response text, with inline examples of expected FINDINGS, SOURCES, and CLAIMS structure.
- For research/synthesis tasks with structured output templates, after completing tool collection, explicitly emit a brief turn stating the total tool calls made and sources cross-checked before writing the final output block. This provides auditability and allows the user to validate research completeness before reading findings.
- For self-directed multi-turn research tasks, explicitly pause and summarize findings every 10–15 tool calls to verify coverage sufficiency and redirect search strategy; avoid exceeding ~50 tool calls without intermediate user confirmation unless the prompt explicitly requests exhaustion without checkpoints.
- For structured critique or analysis tasks with large input specs (>10K tokens), explicitly inform the user of response length limits upfront and offer to chunk the analysis (e.g., 'I will critique components 1–3 in this response; request components 4–6 separately'). Avoid attempting tool invocations for pure text-generation tasks.
- For structured outputs longer than ~5 KB (spec.md, design documents, code modules), when producing STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks, emit the complete output in a single response without truncation, or explicitly partition across numbered blocks (e.g., PART 1 of 2) with clear resumption markers to avoid ambiguity during error recovery.
- For structured research requests with explicit output format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks), validate and emit the complete block before declaring task done; if tool results are unavailable or empty, state this explicitly and offer partial findings rather than ending with tool invocation names only.
- For structured research synthesis tasks with large prior artifact context (100+ pre-existing directions): Always output the JSON array immediately after the STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START tag with no preamble or prose explanation. Pre-compute priority/novelty heuristics before synthesis to avoid mid-stream hallucination of criteria. If synthesis will exceed token budget, pre-warn the user of truncation risk rather than silently timing out.
- For structured research tasks with explicit tool quotas and output formats, confirm tool availability and success rates in first turn before beginning systematic searches to avoid wasted calls on unavailable tools.
- For structured-output tasks, lead with the required JSON/array block (wrapped in STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END markers), then append any explanatory prose or metadata. Never emit preamble before the payload.
- For synthesis tasks on large documents (>10k tokens input), always estimate output size and warn the user if the full response will not fit within available budget. Offer to stream output to a file, split into multiple structured blocks, or defer lower-priority sections if truncation is imminent.
- For tasks requesting structured output (JSON arrays, XML, markdown tables with strict schemas), always emit the complete structure in a single response, even if the analysis is partial or identifies zero items. Use empty arrays if no items found, never omit the structure itself. Completion of format is non-negotiable.
- For tasks requiring structured JSON output with fixed schemas, always validate completeness by echoing back the schema before starting analysis. If response may exceed token budget, estimate flaw count and trim scope rather than producing partial output.
- For tasks with explicit output format requirements, emit STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START before any narrative, populate the required fields, and complete the block before adding commentary. Do not interleave prose and structured markers.
- If a primary metric (count, rate, SLA) is requested but not found after 10 tool calls across 3+ distinct sources, explicitly declare the metric inaccessible and pivot to delivering supporting evidence rather than continuing circular searches. Report findings with confidence boundaries (e.g., 'Runway team identified, model count not accessible via [sources tried]').
- If a required input file is missing, malformed, or inaccessible after one read attempt, immediately halt task execution and report the error to the user with the full file path and error message; do not infer content or make assumptions about user intent.
- If a research or data collection task reports 0 results or complete failure (e.g., findings-index with 0 directions, malformed responses in upstream systems), explicitly surface this to the user with a halt-and-ask decision point: do not proceed to generate a hollow report structure without confirmation.
- If a research request specifies output format (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks with specific sections), generate and return that format before ending the session, even if some sources are single-sourced or undated; incomplete structured output is worse than incomplete research.
- If a research target does not exist, ambiguous, or ill-defined (e.g., a product name does not map to a real system), surface this discovery to the user immediately at the moment of detection, with a clarifying question. Do not silently pivot to broader searches. Format: 'I cannot find evidence of [X] as a unified product. Did you mean [alternatives]? Please clarify before I continue.'
- If a research task specifies a structured output format (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END with named sections), synthesize and emit that format before turn-count exhaustion; check remaining context budget and produce output by turn N-2 if N is the max turn limit.
- If a structured analysis task requires output in a specific format (XML, JSON, marked sections), before starting the analysis, echo back the exact format you will use and confirm token budget for completion; if insufficient, propose chunking or phased delivery upfront rather than truncating mid-response.
- If a structured output format is specified (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks with named fields like FINDINGS, SOURCES, CLAIMS), always synthesize and emit that block explicitly at the end of the session, even if partial. Never leave tool exploration results unsynthesized.
- If a task explicitly mandates specific tool calls and you cannot access those tools, stop and ask for clarification in the first response rather than proceeding with silence or pivoting to alternatives. Name which tools are unavailable and ask the user to confirm what you should do instead.
- If a tool search direction yields empty results after 2–3 attempts, explicitly report that direction as a dead end with evidence, then pivot to fallback sources (archive, cached reports, or web). Do not silently loop or switch strategies without narrating the pivot to the user.
- If composing a large structured output near context limits, break it into smaller sequential responses (e.g., 'Emitting FINDINGS...' [full prose], then in next turn 'Emitting SOURCES...' [array], etc.) rather than attempting to emit all sections in one turn; explicitly confirm completion with 'STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_END' and summary line count before ending.
- If initial tool calls fail silently or return empty, explicitly ask the user for clarification on tool availability and acceptable fallback strategies before continuing; do not self-correct pivots without confirmation.
- If the user explicitly forbids writing files (e.g., 'Do NOT write any files. Return your results ONLY as a structured output block'), do not invoke file-write tools (TodoWrite, Bash write) anywhere in the session, even as intermediate steps. Perform all analysis in-context and return the final output as text only.
- In high-context research synthesis tasks (>30k tokens), explicitly validate the final structured output against the format spec before reporting completion. If output is truncated due to token limits, emit a truncation warning and indicate which directions were dropped.
- Never invoke file-write tools (TodoWrite, file editors, etc.) in sessions where the user explicitly forbids file output; use internal reasoning and turn commentary instead to track progress.
- Never self-impose tool or capability restrictions based on invented internal rules ('SUBAGENT-STOP', 'superpowers') unless explicitly instructed by the user to do so. If uncertain whether a tool is available, use it and let the system reject it rather than declining preemptively.
- Never self-invoke constraints like SUBAGENT-STOP or skip requested tool categories without explicit user permission. If tool limitations exist, surface them transparently in the first turn rather than silently degrading scope.
- Never unilaterally decline or skip tool categories without explicit user approval; if a tool is unavailable, state it and ask for redirection, but do not silently drop requested capabilities.
- On research tasks requiring 'aggressive' tool use with explicit call minimums, execute at least the stated minimum in the opening 3 turns before pivoting to synthesis, even if early results are sparse; this ensures pacing alignment and avoids perception of under-effort.
- On research tasks with explicit tool-use mandates, map available tools to search dimensions upfront (public docs, code repositories, internal chat, engineering artifacts) and rotate through them in parallel for the first 10 turns before settling into dominant tools.
- Prioritize authenticated internal tools (netflix_search_data, NECP, rag-* variants) for Netflix-internal research before attempting generic WebFetch; map tool authentication tiers upfront (unauthenticated < basic auth < Netflix token-required < service account).
- Subagent dispatch bypass of skill invocation requires explicit user confirmation in the same turn; 'SUBAGENT-STOP' markers in skill documentation do not override user instructions to use skills 'even if there is a 1% chance.' Always invoke a relevant skill when user instructions are present, then skip if the skill content itself says 'stop if subagent.'
- Subagent dispatch does not exempt skill checks. Always invoke Skill tool for any new task, regardless of dispatch context. The skill itself contains logic to detect subagent status and skip if needed.
- When 'exhaustive' research is requested, define a stopping heuristic (e.g., 'stop after 3 consecutive searches return no new source domains' or 'stop after 40 turns unless explicitly asked to continue') and apply it before completing to avoid token waste on diminishing-return queries.
- When RAG or NECP queries return truncated/incomplete results, immediately pivot to bash/grep parsing of saved output files or check for multi-file concatenation before re-querying the same tool.
- When RAG or large-file reads fail, skip retry loops after 2 attempts and pivot immediately to alternative sources (Sourcegraph, web fetch, API) rather than spending >8 turns on bash workarounds; signal tool-switching intent to user after failed attempt 2.
- When RAG/API queries return large unstructured payloads (>10KB JSON), immediately narrow the query or add filters rather than attempting to parse full results; prefer iterative refinement over single large fetch-and-parse cycles.
- When WebFetch or internal tools are blocked, explicitly log the blockage and automatically scale search query volume and variance rather than retrying the same blocked tool. Prefer broader, multi-angle search over repeated fetch attempts.
- When a URL fetch fails repeatedly (>2 attempts), log the failure explicitly in reasoning, mark the source as inaccessible, and pivot to alternative sources rather than retrying the same URL. This prevents circular retry patterns while maintaining audit trail.
- When a URL fetch returns 404, immediately pivot to alternative strategies (e.g., bash file discovery, alternative URL patterns, or Sourcegraph structural search) rather than retrying the same URL; document the fallback reason in working notes.
- When a WebFetch fails on a primary source (PDF, official document), immediately log the failure and pivot to 2–3 pre-identified secondary sources (legal summaries, news articles, blog posts from domain experts) rather than retrying the same URL; mark all downstream claims from those secondary sources as 'single_source' or lower confidence in the corroboration field.
- When a broad search strategy times out, pause and ask the user whether to switch to narrower tools or retry with different query scoping, rather than unilaterally pivoting to a different source class. Prioritize the stated corroboration requirement over speed.
- When a context-boundary recovery summary instructs 'do not call tools' but the original task requires tool calls to complete, interpret this narrowly: the text-only instruction applies to the summary response itself, not to subsequent task continuation. Resume required tool calls (like file reads) without re-prompting.
- When a critical signal indicates prior findings are stale (e.g., '(Deactivated)' status, outdated timestamps, org chart conflicts), immediately flag findings as suspect and re-prioritize source verification; do not continue pursuing stale contacts without confirming current status first.
- When a critical term or acronym appears in a research direction, perform a 2-turn hypothesis validation phase early (turn 2–3) before committing to 10+ downstream tool calls. Query manuals, code, and chat for competing definitions, and surface ambiguities to the user before proceeding.
- When a critical tool (e.g., Slack RAG, primary metrics API) fails repeatedly across 3+ attempts, explicitly enumerate fallback source priorities and document why each alternative was selected; do not retry the same failing tool more than twice without switching strategy.
- When a data source is empty, missing, or malformed, always output the requested structured format with null/zero/empty values rather than reverting to descriptive status reports. Honor output contracts even when input is degenerate.
- When a data source is too large to read sequentially, immediately pivot to streaming/chunked extraction (bash, jq, awk) rather than attempting full-file reads; attempt pattern-based extraction only after confirming file structure via head/tail inspection.
- When a data warehouse query times out (turn 57, 74, 77), explicitly state the timeout before pivoting strategy, and always attempt a simplified SELECT * LIMIT 100 to verify table existence before complex aggregations.
- When a deep-research prompt specifies metric definitions (e.g., 'days to first model deployed') without explicit scope (calendar vs business days, definition of 'deployed'), insert a brief clarification request in turn 1 before tool invocation, listing the ambiguities and proposed inference rules; proceed with research only after user confirms or provides specifics.
- When a deep-research task explicitly requests 'exhaustive' multi-source investigation with minimum tool-call counts, treat pauses or empty tool calls as a sign to resume immediately with a new search angle rather than halting; use explicit reasoning to justify when you believe saturation is reached, rather than signaling uncertainty via empty turns.
- When a deep-research task involves exhaustive tool searches across multiple sources, establish an explicit evidence-sufficiency stopping rule upfront (e.g., 'stop when you have ≥3 independent corroborations for the top claim') and communicate it to the user before starting, to avoid premature cutoff or scope creep.
- When a deep-research task lists multiple sub-questions (e.g., 10 research directions), prioritize them explicitly with the user in the first 2 turns, and track coverage incrementally. Report early if any direction is blocked or requires disproportionate effort.
- When a deep-research task specifies a mandatory structured output format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks), always reserve final turns explicitly for synthesis and format compliance; implement a hard stop at 75% of turn budget to ensure structured output delivery regardless of research completeness.
- When a deep-research task specifies tool-availability constraints (e.g., 'use internal RAG tools'), explicitly ask the user to confirm which tools are actually accessible in the environment before spending 10+ turns on exploratory ToolSearch calls with unclear results; pivot to confirmed-available tools immediately.
- When a fetch or API call fails due to formatting (e.g., trailing slashes, URL encoding), try the corrected format once before pivoting to alternative data sources; document the correction attempt in reasoning but do not enter circular retry loops.
- When a file extraction or data access attempt fails twice with the same error/approach, immediately pivot to an alternative strategy (summarize available results, request format conversion, escalate constraint) rather than retrying the same method. Log the constraint and move on.
- When a file is too large to read or grep output exceeds display limits, immediately request or attempt chunking by byte range, line number, or pattern-filtered extraction rather than retrying the same operation; escalate to the user if tool constraints prevent extraction after 2 failed attempts.
- When a file path is referenced in a prompt but the content is already provided inline in the session, verify whether the external file is necessary before reading it; assume inline content is the primary source unless explicitly told otherwise.
- When a file read fails due to size limits, immediately propose a fallback strategy (e.g., filtering, chunking, or proceeding without baseline) and ask for user confirmation rather than retrying the same read with parameter variations.
- When a file read or tool invocation fails or returns empty output, explicitly report the failure to the user before proceeding. Do not silently skip missing context and claim you will use it later. Example: 'I attempted to read concept.md but received no output; proceeding with analysis of the provided SPEC section only, which may miss context.'
- When a file-read operation fails (turn 32), immediately switch to a line-extraction tool (grep, head, tail) or indexed search API rather than retrying the same operation; log the pivot and rationale to avoid circular rework.
- When a full specification or design document is provided inline in a prompt, do not attempt to read external files with similar names; use the provided content as the source of truth and proceed directly to the requested analysis or output task.
- When a git/API query yields partial or unexpected results, immediately test the query scope (e.g., date range filtering) with a small validation query before proceeding to full data extraction; avoid compounding shallow data through the entire pipeline.
- When a high-level RAG or search tool has located the primary source document answering a research question, synthesize findings from the search result metadata and pointers rather than pivoting to low-level file extraction; reserve local text processing only for data already in-memory or after exhausting high-level tool options.
- When a key fact (e.g., a person's job level or reporting chain) is established from a reliable source, do not re-research it in subsequent turns unless new contradictory evidence emerges; instead, document it and move to unexplored angles.
- When a long research task requires synthesis of multiple data sources, defer intermediate documentation/checkpoint writes until the final synthesis turn; execute tool calls continuously in tight loops and consolidate findings once, not iteratively.
- When a lookup tool returns zero results across 3+ consecutive calls, pause and query for the tool's schema/documentation before continuing; if unavailable, switch tools immediately rather than retrying.
- When a multi-phase research task pivots scope (e.g., from broad org audit to narrow technical direction), explicitly surface the change and ask for confirmation before proceeding. If proceeding, mark prior context as 'archived' and summarize baseline findings before entering new search loops. At TURN 10+, emit an intermediate findings summary before entering new search waves to validate convergence and avoid redundant tool calls.
- When a multi-source research task specifies 'exhaustive' and 'aggressive tool use,' organize parallel tool batches by source type (RAG/code/NECP) and iterate through discovery→drilling→validation phases; synthesize only after coverage goal is reached, not before.
- When a multi-tool research task is requested, explicitly map available tools to the request at turn 1 before executing any searches. If internal tools (code search, documentation search, engineering context, pipeline inspection) are listed but unavailable, state this upfront. Always synthesize and structure findings as you go, not after tool calls complete, and deliver the required output format in the final turn before any context cutoff.
- When a multi-tool research task specifies 'ALL available tools', explicitly audit and call each tool type at least once before declaring completion; if a tool is inaccessible, note that limitation in the output rather than silently omitting it.
- When a multi-turn research task exceeds 20 turns and involves historical data spanning 8+ years, proactively surface data-availability risks in the first tool-call wave: identify and note which time periods have sparse internal records (e.g., 'pre-2022 headcount snapshots not found in HRIS'), so the user can supply archival sources or adjust scope before 50+ turns elapse.
- When a primary data source (API call, query, script execution) hangs or fails for >3 retries with different recovery strategies, immediately search for alternative authoritative sources (data warehouses, cached reports, observability dashboards) rather than continuing circular debug attempts on the same failing approach.
- When a primary data source becomes unhealthy (e.g., RAG Slack upstream error), immediately attempt 2-3 alternative sources (e.g., rag-manuals for incident docs, Sourcegraph for SLO code definitions, web fetch for postmortems) rather than waiting for retry, to maintain research velocity.
- When a primary document (e.g., requirements doc, RFC, design doc) is explicitly referenced by the user but unavailable after 3+ targeted searches, flag explicitly in FINDINGS that the primary source was not retrievable and note which downstream inferences rely on secondary sources only.
- When a primary source is found to be incomplete or contradicted by a secondary source (e.g., roster doc lists 7 but GitHub team lists 10), explicitly articulate the conflict and corroboration strategy before proceeding to avoid silent assumption of incompleteness.
- When a primary source returns 403 (infrastructure barrier), verbalize the reroute strategy explicitly and document the alternate source used, so audit trails show deliberate adaptation rather than omission.
- When a programmatic approach (e.g., Python library import) fails twice, immediately pivot to direct API/CLI queries rather than retrying the same code path. Document the failed attempt briefly and switch to the next most direct method within 1 turn.
- When a prompt contains a semantic mismatch or conflates two unrelated concept categories (e.g., regulatory frameworks vs. UQ methods), explicitly ask the user to confirm the intended research direction before proceeding, rather than silently pivoting strategy.
- When a prompt contains explicit task modifiers like 'aggressively', 'exhaustively', or 'maximize X', skip summarization preambles and immediately dispatch parallel tool calls at maximum feasible width (3+ tools per turn) rather than sequential or scalar tool usage.
- When a prompt contains multiple research directions but explicitly marks only one as high-priority with a 'Direction (WHERE)' label, confirm scope ambiguity with the user before executing: ask whether to tackle the prioritized direction first or attempt all directions in parallel, and estimate turns/tokens required.
- When a prompt contains multiple sections (seed, key questions, direction statement with priority label), explicitly parse and rank them at turn 1 and allocate tool budget proportionally. If a section is marked 'high priority,' spend ≥60% of tool calls on that direction unless explicitly told otherwise.
- When a prompt contains underspecified technical terms (e.g., synonymy, classification boundaries, time windows), explicitly ask the user for clarification via a structured question block in the first 3 turns, rather than inferring definitions through exploratory tool calls.
- When a prompt demands parallel tool execution ('aggressively'), batch all tool calls in a single turn via concurrent requests rather than sequential Agent tool invocations; flag tool latency >30s to the user immediately.
- When a prompt demands specific output format (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks), always conclude the final turn with the complete formatted deliverable. Verify the deliverable is present in your response before marking the task complete, and never rely on intermediate tool calls alone as proof of completion.
- When a prompt embeds multiple scopes or numbered tasks alongside a labeled 'Direction', explicitly state which you are prioritizing and why before executing research; pause to ask for clarification if priorities conflict.
- When a prompt emphasizes 'aggressive' or 'parallel' execution, batch all tool invocations in a single turn using independent/non-blocking calls (bash subshells, concurrent web searches) rather than sequential reads before launching parallel phases.
- When a prompt enumerates specific tools (e.g., 'code search, documentation search, chat search'), attempt to invoke each one at least once and explicitly note in reasoning if a tool is unavailable or returned no results, rather than defaulting to web-only searches.
- When a prompt explicitly demands aggressive or parallel tool use, begin tool calls in turn 1 or 2; avoid silent planning turns. If synthesizing context first is necessary, announce that explicitly and set a hard turn limit (e.g., 'synthesizing in turn 2, tool calls begin in turn 3') before committing to it.
- When a prompt explicitly demands aggressive tool use with quantified minimums (e.g., '5–10 tool calls per direction'), begin tool invocation immediately in the first turn without preamble; reserve commentary for inter-turn state checks and gap analysis, not startup narration.
- When a prompt explicitly demands specific tools by name (e.g., 'code search', 'engineering context', 'pipeline inspection'), always attempt to invoke them explicitly in the first 3 turns; if they are unavailable, state this constraint clearly to the user before proceeding with fallback tools rather than silently substituting web-only access.
- When a prompt explicitly demands structured output in a specific format (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks), always generate and include that output in the final response, even if it means repeating or synthesizing prior reasoning. Validate the output format against the prompt specification before concluding the session.
- When a prompt explicitly demands tool invocations or external data gathering, immediately attempt the first tool call and report success/failure/unavailability to the user in the same turn; do not proceed to subsequent turns without either executing tools or explaining their unavailability.
- When a prompt explicitly designates internal tools (e.g., 'NECP, Sourcegraph, rag-manuals, rag-slack, netflix_search_api') as primary sources, prioritize them on the first relevant turn and reserve external web search for corroboration only, not initial reconnaissance.
- When a prompt explicitly enumerates specific tools or sources and emphasizes 'ALL available' or 'every available,' verify that you invoke each category of tool at least once before relying on a single tool type, even if that tool appears effective.
- When a prompt explicitly forbids an action ('Do NOT write any files'), add that constraint to your memory at the start of the session and validate against it before invoking any write-class tools; if tempted to write, explain why in reasoning before refusing.
- When a prompt explicitly forbids an action (e.g., 'Do NOT write any files'), never invoke tools that perform that action, even if they seem helpful for intermediate work. Organize findings in-context instead.
- When a prompt explicitly forbids file writes (e.g., 'Do NOT write any files'), avoid using file-write tools (TodoWrite, Write) even if the research phase feels incomplete; synthesize findings directly into the required output format instead.
- When a prompt explicitly forbids file writes and requests structured output-only delivery, never invoke file-writing tools (TodoWrite, file creation commands) — use only in-memory collection and text-response formatting. If scratch work is needed, ask the user first.
- When a prompt explicitly forbids file writes, flag any TodoWrite or file-output calls as constraint violations and skip them unless the user explicitly reverses the constraint in a subsequent turn.
- When a prompt explicitly forbids output to files (e.g., 'Do NOT write any files'), treat write/file-creation tools as out-of-scope and skip them unless the user reverses the constraint mid-session.
- When a prompt explicitly instructs 'use all available tools to understand the landscape before generating,' execute all file reads immediately in the first turn before any synthesis or output, and verify file contents were successfully loaded before proceeding to generation.
- When a prompt explicitly lists multiple tool types before requesting aggressive usage, attempt at least one call from each specified tool category before defaulting to web search, even if web search appears most directly applicable.
- When a prompt explicitly lists required tools and emphasizes 'USE ALL AVAILABLE TOOLS AGGRESSIVELY,' treat this as a hard constraint: document which tools were attempted, explain why each tool was/wasn't applicable, and if a tool category (e.g., code search, engineering context) is unused, explicitly state why in a preamble before the final structured output.
- When a prompt explicitly lists tool categories (code search, chat search, engineering context, web search, web fetch), prioritize using all listed categories before defaulting to familiar tools. If some tools are unavailable, state which ones and why rather than silently omitting them.
- When a prompt explicitly mandates 'MAXIMUM thoroughness' and specifies tool counts (e.g., '5-10 tool calls'), immediately execute a pre-planned multi-tool strategy rather than exploratory ToolSearch first; map tools only if the prompt does not specify which ones to use.
- When a prompt explicitly mandates a specific tool set ('Use ALL available tools: X, Y, Z, ...'), attempt at least one call per tool type before concluding the investigation, or explicitly report unavailability with error context. Do not silently omit tool types even if they seem low-probability.
- When a prompt explicitly names internal tools (code search, engineering context, pipeline inspection) and provides local file paths (e.g., /root/.sagaflow), prioritize exploring local resources and invoking named internal tools FIRST before defaulting to web search. Treat web search as a fallback only after exhausting local options.
- When a prompt explicitly prohibits writing files, treat all file-writing tools (TodoWrite, file creation, etc.) as off-limits, even for intermediate note-taking or caching. Use only read/search/analysis tools and return results inline.
- When a prompt explicitly requests 'aggressive' tool usage with quantified minimums (e.g., '5-10 tool calls per direction'), treat that as a floor, not a ceiling; prioritize exhausting internal sources (RAG, NECP, API) before external (WebFetch); do not pause mid-research to reflect without explicit user request—maintain momentum until the specified output format is complete.
- When a prompt explicitly requests 'aggressive' tool use with a specific cadence (e.g., '5-10 tool calls per direction'), parallelize tool calls early (turns 1-3) rather than ramping up incrementally. Issue 3-5 independent queries in the first turn to establish baseline evidence, then iterate.
- When a prompt explicitly requests a structured output format (JSON, XML, or custom blocks), always execute a final synthesis turn to generate and display that output before marking the session complete, even if token budget is tight.
- When a prompt explicitly requests aggressive, immediate tool execution (e.g., 'Use ALL available tools aggressively'), skip meta-commentary turns and launch the first tool call on the subsequent turn without an intervening planning statement.
- When a prompt explicitly requests exhaustive multi-source research with a minimum tool-call count, skip planning turns and begin with the highest-confidence tool (session_search for org queries, codebase search for code queries) in turn 1 rather than spending 5+ turns discovering the tool ecosystem.
- When a prompt explicitly requests tool use ('use all available tools'), always show tool calls, parameters, and results inline in the response, or prefix the output with a summary of what was queried and why. If tools are unavailable or return no results, state this explicitly.
- When a prompt explicitly requires a structured output format (XML-like tags, JSON arrays, or specific schema), treat format compliance as a completion gate—do not end the session without verifying that the output block is present and well-formed, even if research is still ongoing.
- When a prompt explicitly specifies STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END format or JSON schema, produce that format in the first response, not as a narrative summary. If analysis is needed, perform it internally and embed the results directly into the required structure; do not use a separate 'thinking' turn unless the user explicitly asks for reasoning before the output.
- When a prompt explicitly specifies a minimum number of tool calls (e.g., '5-10'), a success threshold (e.g., 'MAXIMUM thoroughness'), or a required output format (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END), log the threshold at the start of work, track tool call count per turn, and fail explicitly with a summary before ending if the threshold is unmet.
- When a prompt explicitly specifies a required output format (especially with STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END markers), ensure the final turn synthesizes all gathered information and produces that exact format before concluding, even if intermediate results are still arriving.
- When a prompt explicitly specifies a structured output format (FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS with named fields), always output that exact block in full before ending the response, even if intermediate tool calls are still processing. Verify the format matches the specification verbatim.
- When a prompt explicitly specifies an output format (JSON, structured blocks, etc.), emit that format immediately without preamble or narrative framing, unless the prompt explicitly requests explanation.
- When a prompt explicitly states 'Do NOT write any files' or lists hard constraints, parse and validate those constraints before beginning tool execution; maintain a checklist of constraint violations and verify it at every 5-turn checkpoint before calling any I/O tools.
- When a prompt explicitly states 'Do NOT write any files,' avoid all file-creation and persistent storage tools (TodoWrite, write-file, etc.) unless the user explicitly reverses that instruction in a follow-up message.
- When a prompt frames a task as a prerequisite step (e.g., 'bootstrap vocabulary using Wikipedia') before a larger body of work, explicitly confirm the multi-phase scope in the first response and identify any blockers (missing credentials, access constraints, confidentiality concerns) that would prevent Phase 2 execution before proceeding.
- When a prompt includes a detailed specification inline AND requests reading an external file, attempt direct analysis of the inline spec first; use file reads only for clarification if the inline content is ambiguous. This avoids latency overhead on long-context tasks.
- When a prompt includes explicit output format requirements (especially structured formats like FLAWS|[...]), verify in the first response that you understand the format, then deliver it completely before elaborating. If analysis is long, emit the required structure first, then append narrative explanation.
- When a prompt lists 'ALL available tools' explicitly, enumerate them in turn 1-2 and justify why each is or is not applicable before issuing the first tool call; do not default to web search without first assessing code/documentation/chat/context tools.
- When a prompt lists internal tools (code search, documentation, engineering context) before web-based tools and emphasizes using 'ALL available tools aggressively,' exhaust internal tools first with minimum 2-3 calls per tool type before pivoting to web search, to respect tool hierarchy and avoid skipping available evidence.
- When a prompt lists multiple research questions (org structure, incidents, competitive analysis, talent) alongside a 'focused direction' marked 'medium priority', explicitly ask for clarification on scope hierarchy rather than inferring narrowing — or provide a clear mental-model hierarchy (e.g., 'focused direction overrides open questions unless otherwise stated') in system prompt.
- When a prompt lists multiple tool categories in priority order, attempt tool discovery in that order and explicitly note if a tool category is unavailable before defaulting to later options. Report which tools were attempted and which yielded results.
- When a prompt lists multiple tools as 'available' but only a subset are functional, explicitly surface this limitation in the first response and confirm which tools you actually have access to before attempting 3+ calls with unavailable tools.
- When a prompt lists specific internal tools (code search, chat search, engineering context), attempt each one explicitly before defaulting to external web search; if unavailable, report the limitation to the user rather than silently omitting them.
- When a prompt lists specific internal/proprietary tools by name, always attempt those tools first or explicitly request clarification on tool availability before defaulting to external research methods; never silently skip named tool categories.
- When a prompt lists specific named tools (e.g., 'code search,' 'documentation search,' 'chat search'), attempt to invoke them by name before falling back to generic bash/grep exploration. If these tools are unavailable, explicitly state which ones failed before substituting alternatives.
- When a prompt lists specific tool categories (code search, documentation search, chat search, etc.), make at least one attempt with each category before committing to a single strategy. If internal tools fail or are unavailable, explicitly note the attempt and reason for deprioritization in the first reasoning turn.
- When a prompt lists specific tool classes (e.g., 'code search, documentation search, chat search'), attempt those first before defaulting to fallback tools like web search. If unavailable, explicitly report the unavailability and explain the fallback strategy to the user.
- When a prompt lists specific tool names and emphasizes 'use ALL available tools', explicitly attempt each named tool in turn before defaulting to web search. If a tool fails or is unavailable, state so explicitly rather than silently omitting it from the strategy.
- When a prompt lists specific tools and requests 'aggressive' usage, explicitly note in the first turn which tools are available in the environment and which cannot be accessed; if unavailable, explain constraints and propose alternatives (e.g., 'Web search/fetch available; CodeQL search unavailable—using GitHub web API instead').
- When a prompt lists specific tools and specifies 'use ALL available tools aggressively,' attempt each named tool at least once before defaulting to fallback tools. If a tool is unavailable, explicitly report its absence rather than silently omitting it.
- When a prompt lists specific tools by name ('code search, chat search, pipeline inspection') and directs 'use ALL available tools,' attempt direct tool invocation by exact name before spending turns on tool discovery. Use rapid fire-and-fail patterns rather than ToolSearch.
- When a prompt lists specific tools by name (code search, documentation search, etc.), explicitly attempt each tool type before defaulting to web search; if tools are unavailable, acknowledge the constraint and explain fallback strategy in reasoning.
- When a prompt lists specific tools by name and says 'use ALL available tools aggressively,' attempt at least one invocation of each named tool before defaulting to a subset, even if web search seems most promising. Document which tools were unavailable vs. deliberately deprioritized.
- When a prompt lists specific tools in prioritized order, explicitly attempt each tool class before falling back to alternatives; if a tool is unavailable, note it and justify why the fallback strategy still achieves the goal stated.
- When a prompt lists specific tools to use exhaustively (e.g., 'code search, documentation search, chat search, engineering context'), prioritize attempting all listed internal tools before defaulting to web search, and explicitly signal if internal tools are unavailable rather than silently substituting with web-only approaches.
- When a prompt mandates 'exhaustive' or 'aggressive' research with no explicit stopping criteria, establish a stopping rule after the first pass: 'I will run one additional round of cross-checking on any claim with single-source corroboration, then consolidate findings.' Avoid >50 turns unless explicitly asked for iterative refinement.
- When a prompt mandates specific tool categories in a priority order (internal tools before external), always attempt at least one call from each category before pivoting, and explicitly log which tools were attempted and why some were skipped. Never rely solely on web search when code search or documentation search is explicitly required.
- When a prompt mandates use of specialized internal tools, test tool availability with a single call first. If the tool returns empty or no-op, immediately pivot to fallback strategy (e.g., public web search) rather than retrying the same tool sequentially.
- When a prompt mentions 'Netflix,' 'internal,' 'engineering context,' or company-specific org/product names, prioritize Netflix-native tools (NECP, Netflix Search API, rag-slack-prod, Sourcegraph) before attempting generic web search or ToolSearch, which are likely to fail on proprietary systems.
- When a prompt names specific tools and emphasizes 'ALL available tools,' prioritize named tools before defaulting to general searches. For research tasks, use internal/proprietary tools first (code search, engineering context, internal docs) before web search, as internal sources typically have higher relevance and recency for product feature work.
- When a prompt provides comprehensive reference material inline and also references an external file, begin with inline material immediately. If external context is truly needed, verify accessibility first; if inaccessible, escalate or proceed with available context rather than entering a read loop.
- When a prompt provides content inline AND references an external file, prioritize inline content immediately if file access fails once; do not retry the same read without explicit user permission.
- When a prompt references an external file, explicitly attempt the read in the same turn and report success/failure immediately. Do not defer file I/O to a later turn; it creates dead time and delays analysis.
- When a prompt references an undefined domain concept (e.g., 'Top 10 algorithms'), explicitly note the gap after initial tool search and signal to the user which specific definitions or reference documents would be needed to fully answer the question, rather than inferring and proceeding.
- When a prompt references external files as critical input, immediately surface any retrieval failure and ask for the content inline rather than proceeding with incomplete information.
- When a prompt references local file paths or internal systems (e.g., /root/.sagaflow/...), prioritize code-search, codebase inspection, and documentation-search tools before defaulting to web search; treat internal systems as primary sources and web patterns as secondary corroboration.
- When a prompt requests 'ALL available tools' and names specific internal tools (code search, documentation search, engineering context), attempt those tools first and explicitly note in response if they are unavailable, unsupported, or return no results—do not silently fall back to web-only search without acknowledgment.
- When a prompt requests 'ALL available tools' but only a subset are actually callable, explicitly report which tools are unavailable and why, rather than silently pivoting to available alternatives. Suggest alternative search strategies to compensate (e.g., searching for 'GitHub <repo> memory consolidation' to simulate code search via web index).
- When a prompt requests 'ALL available tools' by name, explicitly verify tool availability in the first turn and report which tools are inaccessible before proceeding, rather than silently omitting unused tools from the execution plan.
- When a prompt requests 'aggressive' multi-tool research with explicit minimums (e.g., '5-10 tool calls'), first map available tools via a quick discovery phase (ls /usr/bin, check MCP registries, etc.) before committing to a tool strategy. This avoids blind tool exploration and focuses effort on high-signal sources.
- When a prompt requests 'all available tools,' explicitly enumerate which tools will be used for which research dimensions before executing, and justify tool selection trade-offs (internal vs. external sources) to ensure balanced coverage rather than over-relying on a single tool category.
- When a prompt requests 'exhaustive' or 'aggressive' tool use without explicit stopping criteria (e.g., token budget, corroboration thresholds, time limit), establish a stopping rule after the first 15–20 productive tool calls: check if marginal new evidence is <10% novel vs. prior calls, and flag 'diminishing returns' to the user before continuing iteration.
- When a prompt requests 'exhaustive' or 'maximum thoroughness' with explicit tool counts (e.g., '5-10 tool calls'), define and communicate an adequacy threshold after first round of searches—e.g., 'X claims identified, Y sources corroborated, proceeding to synthesis' — to avoid diminishing-return iterations.
- When a prompt requests a 'structured output block' in text response, always emit that block as the final turn, even if partial, and clearly mark it STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END to signal completion of the research phase.
- When a prompt requests access to internal/proprietary tools (code search, internal wikis, engineering context) in the first turn, explicitly state in turn 2 which internal tools succeeded vs. failed to connect, and pivot the strategy accordingly rather than silently falling back to public web search.
- When a prompt requests analysis of external files (especially within a multi-round workflow), immediately ask the user to provide file paths, contents, or storage location in the first response if none are visible; do not proceed silently to a tool call or wait for Turn 2.
- When a prompt requests both a high-priority comprehensive audit and a low-priority supplementary research direction, explicitly acknowledge scope and sequence the work: clarify which sources will be queried first, state expected limitations (e.g., 'internal tools may not return Netflix org data'), and report constraints before beginning tool calls.
- When a prompt requests data with a time window (e.g., 'last 90 days') or freshness requirement, first check whether a real-time query tool (API, warehouse, live dashboard) is available; if not, explicitly surface this limitation in the output and note the staleness class of substitute evidence.
- When a prompt requests exhaustive use of multiple tool categories, explicitly verify tool availability and report which tools are unavailable before proceeding; enumerate tool attempts by category (e.g., 'Code Search: 3 attempts', 'Chat Search: 0 attempts—unavailable') in the final output to demonstrate completeness or justify fallbacks.
- When a prompt requests specific tools (code search, internal docs, chat search) that are not available in the current tool set, explicitly flag the gap in the first synthesis turn and confirm with the user whether to proceed with available tools only or halt until missing tools are enabled.
- When a prompt requests structured output (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END block), always conclude the session by emitting that block verbatim with fully synthesized findings, sources, and claims arrays—never leave it for assumed future completion.
- When a prompt requests structured output in a specific format (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START...END blocks), always explicitly confirm format compliance in the final turn and provide a complete rendered example of the output block before declaring research complete, even if token budget is nearly exhausted.
- When a prompt requires a specific structured output format (especially marked with START/END delimiters), always emit that exact format as the final output, even if the last tool call is still in-flight; prepend a status note if data gathering is incomplete.
- When a prompt specifies 'aggressive' tool usage with numeric thresholds (e.g., '5-10 calls per direction'), maximize parallelism by batching 4-6 tool calls per turn rather than sequential 2-3, and track aggregate call count per direction to ensure thresholds are met.
- When a prompt specifies 'use ALL available tools' and 'per direction', interpret this as parallel multi-source strategy: launch 2-3 tool types simultaneously per direction in round-robin fashion, not sequentially. After 4-6 calls to one tool show diminishing returns, immediately pivot to the next tool type rather than extending the series.
- When a prompt specifies 'use tools X, Y, Z aggressively' and lists tool-call minima, prioritize MCP/named tools over fallback local commands (e.g., Bash filesystem access) unless those tools are unavailable; self-correct if you deviate.
- When a prompt specifies a mandatory output format (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks, JSON arrays, numbered claims), explicitly track completion of each element and output the formatted result before concluding the session, even if research is ongoing.
- When a prompt specifies a mandatory output format with delimiters (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END), emit that block first and in full, before any narrative explanation. Validate token budget permits complete structured output before beginning narrative analysis.
