- When a prompt specifies a required output format (especially with structured markers like STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END), always return that exact format at the end of the session, even if research is incomplete; never skip the formatting step to avoid losing structured data.
- When a prompt specifies a required structured output format (BEGIN_FORMAT/SECTIONS/END_FORMAT), always generate and include that output in the final turn, even if synthesis is incomplete; truncate findings rather than omit structure.
- When a prompt specifies a structured output format (START/END markers, JSON arrays, enums), always emit that exact structure in your response, even if the content is empty. Use STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_END to delimit every analysis in this format, and never cut off mid-structure.
- When a prompt specifies a structured output format (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START...END), always emit the complete block at the end of your response, even if incomplete, and never omit it in favor of exploratory turns. Verify the final deliverable against the spec before concluding.
- When a prompt specifies a structured output template (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END), immediately print a draft skeleton of it in turn 1, confirm the structure with the user if ambiguous, and explicitly track synthesis as a separate phase distinct from research—allocate turns for gathering, then turns for writing the output block.
- When a prompt specifies an explicit 'Output format:' section with XML-like markers (START/END) or JSON structure, produce that structure immediately without prose preamble. Reserve narration for post-structure explanation only.
- When a prompt specifies an explicit output format (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks), always output that format before turning off — even if interim exploration is ongoing, provide the structured wrapper with in-progress findings to avoid truncation-without-delivery.
- When a prompt specifies an explicit output format with START/END delimiters, treat format compliance as a hard requirement: after tool gathering reaches saturation (diminishing returns observed or tool errors), immediately synthesize findings into the specified format block before concluding the response.
- When a prompt specifies both a broad mapping objective and a marked-high-priority narrow technical deep dive, explicitly surface the apparent scope hierarchy to the user (e.g., 'I note MServe architecture is marked high-priority; should I front-load that over organizational mapping?') before beginning tool calls, rather than inferring scope order.
- When a prompt specifies both a low priority and MAXIMUM thoroughness demands, ask for clarification on budget/scope before executing. Flag the contradiction explicitly and propose time/token limits to avoid over-investment on low-priority tasks.
- When a prompt specifies both a research direction and a structured output format, always end the session by delivering the structured output block verbatim, even if research is ongoing. Verify completion of the output template before reporting readiness.
- When a prompt specifies exact output formatting (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks with field requirements), always render the complete formatted output in the response and never substitute a narrative paraphrase, even if the content is similar
- When a prompt specifies exact tools to use and explicitly names them (e.g., 'NECP, Sourcegraph, rag-manuals, rag-slack'), begin with those tools immediately rather than using a discovery tool (ToolSearch) first; treat named tools as pre-vetted and avoid meta-discovery overhead.
- When a prompt specifies exhaustive use of multiple tool categories (code search, docs, chat, engineering context, web), deploy at least 2-3 from each category in parallel within the first 3 turns before settling on primary tools. Track tool diversity explicitly and call out if scope must be narrowed due to tool availability.
- When a prompt specifies explicit deliverable formats (structured output blocks, JSON arrays, etc.) and minimum tool call counts, immediately list available tools, acknowledge concrete execution plan, and deliver first-pass results within 2 turns before detailed refinement.
- When a prompt specifies explicit output format delimiters (START/END blocks, JSON schema, or structured templates), prioritize emitting those blocks early and completely, even if it means truncating narrative elaboration. The structured artifact is the primary deliverable.
- When a prompt specifies explicit tool diversity (e.g., 'code search, documentation search, chat search'), explicitly attempt each category before fallback to web search; if unavailable, state which tools were attempted and why they failed before proceeding with alternatives.
- When a prompt specifies internal tools (NECP, rag-slack, Netflix Search APIs) as primary sources, exhaust those tools first before pivoting to public web search; public sources should be validation/baseline only, not primary research vectors.
- When a prompt specifies multiple named tools to use in a particular order or priority (especially tools marked 'aggressive' or 'first'), invoke at least one of each category before pivoting to alternative tools, and explain any deviation from the specified strategy.
- When a prompt specifies multiple research directions with different priorities, always tackle the highest-priority direction first, then descend in priority order. Delay broad contextual searches until after the primary direction is exhausted, or integrate them as lightweight sidebar searches rather than dedicated turn sequences.
- When a prompt specifies multiple tool types (code search, documentation search, web search, etc.) and you lack access to some, explicitly name which tools are unavailable and explain why you're substituting alternatives before proceeding with research.
- When a prompt specifies tool counts and 'aggressiveness' (5-10+ calls per direction), implement explicit saturation logic: after 10 tool calls of each type, assess whether new results are novel; if 3+ consecutive calls yield zero new entities/concepts, halt that tool category and document the stopping reason in reasoning traces.
- When a prompt specifies tool priority order (internal tools before external), explicitly execute internal tools first and log findings before pivoting to web search, even if external sources seem more immediately fruitful. Document why you reorder if you deviate.
- When a required input file is missing or inaccessible after the first read attempt, explicitly ask the user for clarification or alternate input rather than performing multiple filesystem searches; state what you found (or didn't find) and propose next steps within 2 turns.
- When a required tool (e.g., Jira, proprietary database) fails authentication or connectivity, explicitly state in the next message which tools are unavailable and report a revised tool budget (e.g., 'Jira unavailable; reallocating 3 calls to Sourcegraph NLS and NECP deep dives to maintain thoroughness'). This signals adaptive strategy to the user and prevents ambiguity.
- When a research direction asks for specific metrics (median, p99, distribution by class, trends over time), prioritize database/table query tools over documentation/chat search; cost and performance questions almost always live in structured tables, not prose docs.
- When a research direction calls for 'exhaustive' cross-referencing, prioritize high-signal tools (peer-reviewed papers, official documentation) in the first 30% of turns, then broaden to secondary sources; abandon low-signal tools (e.g., code search on unfamiliar repos) after 2 failed attempts and redirect effort to proven channels.
- When a research direction contains a regulatory assumption (e.g., 'DSA Article 34 gates Netflix'), perform at least one early verification query to validate the assumption before expanding investigation scope; surface any invalidation immediately and explicitly reframe the direction rather than proceeding under false premises.
- When a research direction includes 'exhaustive' or 'maximum thoroughness,' establish and communicate a stopping criterion (e.g., 'I will stop when 3+ independent sources converge on the same claim, or after N search queries return no novel results') before entering the search loop, then track saturation explicitly.
- When a research direction is explicitly marked 'Priority: high', begin tool calls on that direction first, even if other context suggests broader groundwork. Use priority markers to order the sequence of investigation, not to justify eventual coverage.
- When a research direction is marked 'Priority: low' in a complex multi-part prompt, explicitly confirm with the user the expected depth and token budget before executing 80+ turns; aggressive tool use should scale with priority level and strategic importance.
- When a research direction is marked PRIOR-FAILURE, explicitly surface the hypothesis (what the direction assumes to be true) early in the investigation and test that hypothesis across sources before deep-diving into details; if the hypothesis fails, prioritize reporting the meta-finding (premise is flawed) over exhaustive detail-gathering.
- When a research direction is underspecified (no target vendors, no metric definitions, no known sources), ask clarifying questions in the first 2 turns before launching exhaustive search campaigns. Aggressive search breadth is productive only when aimed at clearly-bounded targets.
- When a research direction lacks explicit success criteria (cost threshold, timeline certainty), proactively ask clarifying questions before tool use begins rather than inferring assumptions in analysis.
- When a research direction specifies 'exhaustive' or 'aggressive tool use,' establish an explicit stopping rule after first discovering: corroboration from ≥2 independent sources for each core claim, ≥1 primary artifact (code, doc, Slack thread, or person mention), and a coherence check (new tool calls yield <10% novel information vs. prior 5 turns). Publish intermediate findings and ask for direction before continuing.
- When a research direction specifies 'exhaustive' search with 'STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END' delivery, explicitly state in the first response which tools are available and confirm the output format will be included in-turn, not deferred to a file. If unavailable tools are listed (e.g., 'code search, documentation search'), acknowledge the constraint and explain the fallback approach.
- When a research direction specifies a precise date (e.g., 'Aug 2024 reorg') for a sensitive organizational event, validate the date assumption with a quick search on Turn 1–2 and explicitly surface any temporal misalignment to the user before executing 40+ tool calls.
- When a research direction specifies exhaustive multi-tool strategies with numerical thresholds ('5-10 tool calls per direction'), explicitly track and report tool call counts per phase with a summary line like 'Phase X: [N] tool calls completed' to enable real-time verification of compliance.
- When a research direction specifies numerical targets ('5-10 tool calls'), explicitly track and log cumulative tool call counts by category in intermediate reasoning to verify threshold attainment before synthesis.
- When a research direction specifies tool diversity (e.g., 'code search, documentation search, chat search, engineering context, pipeline inspection') but only some tools are actually available in the environment, explicitly report which tools were attempted and which were unavailable rather than silently defaulting to web search alone.
- When a research direction uses vague nouns like 'contract', 'interface', or 'agreement' without specifying format (code|docs|API spec|org agreement), pause after 2-3 initial searches and ask the user to clarify the artifact type before continuing exhaustive searching.
- When a research direction yields zero corroborating evidence across 3+ independent tools, explicitly pause and validate the premise before continuing exhaustive searching; surface the null result as a finding rather than a failed search.
- When a research direction's core premise fails validation (e.g., a person doesn't exist in authoritative sources), explicitly report this non-existence as a finding with source evidence and corroboration class before pivoting to alternative search strategies.
- When a research direction's factual premise is discovered to be incorrect, explicitly state the factual error upfront in FINDINGS, then pivot to salvaging maximum value from orthogonal aspects of the topic rather than abandoning the research.
- When a research direction's premise is partially counterfactual (e.g., seeking 'multi-turn' limits for a single-turn system), explicitly flag this discovery early and propose adjusted research scope rather than continuing mechanically to gather non-existent data.
- When a research directive lists tools that may not be available (e.g., 'code search,' 'chat search'), silently skip unavailable tools and focus on aggressive use of available ones; do not pause to ask for clarification unless the available tool set is genuinely ambiguous.
- When a research directive specifies 'use X tool aggressively' and 'make N+ tool calls,' validate tool availability in the first turn, then execute the minimum tool call threshold before reading context files. Preparatory file I/O should not consume more than 1 turn; if it exceeds that, immediately escalate and report tool access issues rather than continuing the loop.
- When a research directive specifies future-dated or anomalous claims (e.g., April 2026 launch), pause and clarify temporal context before commencing exhaustive tool calls; document any assumptions about the research scenario's framing.
- When a research directive specifies internal system access (kragle, internal repos, chat search) and those tools yield no visible results after 2 consecutive attempts, immediately pivot to public-source-only mode with aggressive use of web search, public research archives, and LinkedIn/careers profiling rather than continuing to retry internal access patterns.
- When a research directive specifies tool count targets (e.g., '5-10 tool calls per direction') and 'MAXIMUM thoroughness,' batch tool calls in structured waves (e.g., 4×WebSearch, 4×WebFetch, 4×CodeSearch) rather than random sequencing; this demonstrates systematic coverage and makes progress transparent to users reading the transcript.
- When a research or analysis task demands a specific structured output format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END, JSON, markdown tables, etc.), explicitly confirm the structured block has been generated and visible in the response before ending the session, and never omit it even if token budget is tight.
- When a research or discovery task explicitly lists tools in priority order (e.g., 'code search, documentation search, chat search, engineering context, web search'), attempt all non-web tools first in sequence before defaulting to WebSearch, and do not proceed to synthesis until all tool categories have been attempted or explicitly ruled out.
- When a research or exploration prompt lists specific tools by name and requests 'ALL available tools aggressively,' attempt each named tool at least once before defaulting to a single tool type; if a tool fails or is unavailable, log the attempt and reason before proceeding. Do not omit tool categories without explicit justification.
- When a research or investigation prompt lists specific tool categories and uses emphatic language ('EVERY', 'ALL', 'aggressive'), construct and execute a diversified tool call plan covering each category before relying on a single tool type; verify plan completion before delivering results.
- When a research or investigation prompt specifies 'use ALL available tools', explicitly enumerate suspected tool names in the first reflection turn and validate availability via a test call, to prevent tool-discovery delays and ensure comprehensive coverage early.
- When a research or investigation task explicitly specifies minimum tool-call counts and tool types, immediately execute those tool calls in the first response before providing any synthesis. Do not announce intention to use tools and then defer tool execution to later turns; fulfill the tool-use contract immediately and exhaustively.
- When a research or investigation task explicitly specifies tool requirements and a structured output format, validate tool availability at the start; if tools are unavailable or unknown, immediately report this limitation with specific tool names and ask the user for clarification rather than attempting fallback Read actions.
- When a research or synthesis task specifies an exact output format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END, JSON, markdown table, etc.), always validate in the final turn that the complete formatted block is present in your response before stopping, and explicitly confirm format compliance to the user.
- When a research prompt assumes specific prior roles or artifacts, explicitly validate those assumptions with initial tool calls and flag any that cannot be corroborated before proceeding to speculative searches.
- When a research prompt contains ambiguous key terms (e.g., 'consolidation rate,' 'dominate'), propose two alternative interpretations with corresponding search strategies in the first response, asking the user to confirm focus before launching the full investigation. This prevents 4-6 wasted exploratory tool calls on edge interpretations.
- When a research prompt contains multiple distinct research questions (e.g., 'Netflix partnerships' vs. 'Anthropic vendor restrictions'), explicitly enumerate them and ask for prioritization before dispatching tools, to avoid wasting cycles on lower-priority scopes.
- When a research prompt contains multiple nested goals (e.g., 'map org' + 'focus direction: talent-flow'), explicitly ask the user in the first turn to clarify priority and allocate effort accordingly before committing to parallel tracks.
- When a research prompt contains multiple prioritized directions with different scopes, explicitly negotiate scope trade-offs with the user before beginning tool calls. For high-priority deep-dives paired with broader audit requests, clarify whether to (a) do both sequentially, (b) do high-priority fully and audit as secondary, or (c) timebox both equally. Default to asking rather than implicitly rebalancing.
- When a research prompt defines quantitative KPIs ('operational overhead per training-hour') without a measurement model, confirm the definition via structured question before synthesizing findings, or state your interpretation assumptions explicitly in the FINDINGS section.
- When a research prompt demands 'exhaustive' cross-referencing across multiple tool types (code search, docs search, web search, etc.), explicitly log which tools were attempted, whether they succeeded, and why some were skipped, before synthesizing findings. Distinguish between 'no evidence found' and 'tool unavailable'.
- When a research prompt demands exhaustive multi-tool investigation with explicit tool-call minimums, deploy tools in parallel batches and vary search strategies (keyword, NLS, context APIs) rather than sequential single-source queries to maximize coverage efficiency.
- When a research prompt demands structured output with specific formatting (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks), always reserve and explicitly track token budget for final synthesis and output assembly; if approaching limits, emit partial structured output with a 'research_incomplete' flag rather than terminating mid-research.
- When a research prompt emphasizes 'ALL available tools' and lacks quantitative targets, first clarify scope and definitions (consolidation rate, cost threshold, baseline model) in a single synthesis turn before launching tool calls; avoid tool saturation without alignment.
- When a research prompt emphasizes 'ALL available tools,' perform local/internal tool exhaustion (Read, Bash, code search) before pivoting to external tools (WebSearch, WebFetch). Only go external after confirming local context is depleted or inaccessible.
- When a research prompt emphasizes 'aggressive' tool deployment and 'exhaustive' investigation, interpret thresholds (e.g., '5-10 tool calls') as minimums rather than targets, and scale dynamically if research complexity warrants; always structure multi-phase campaigns (broad search → primary source fetch → deep drill) rather than scattered individual queries.
- When a research prompt emphasizes 'exhaustive' investigation with 'at least N tool calls' and 'aggressive' multi-source corroboration, immediately document available tools via a single meta-query (e.g., 'list all search/code/web/internal tools available') before launching individual searches, rather than probing via repeated trial-and-error calls. This reduces setup waste while preserving exhaustiveness.
- When a research prompt emphasizes exhaustive coverage with 'all available tools' and explicit tool-call minimums, explicitly track and announce tool counts (e.g., 'Tool calls so far: 23 of 30+') at progress checkpoints to transparently demonstrate thoroughness and help the user calibrate stopping criteria.
- When a research prompt emphasizes internal tools (code search, engineering context, pipeline inspection) alongside web tools, prioritize internal sources first—deploy Grep, Read, and context queries in the opening turns (1–10) before web harvesting, then cross-reference web findings against internal data to validate.
- When a research prompt enumerates specific tools and emphasizes 'aggressive' or 'exhaustive' use, attempt each tool category at least once before pivoting to a dominant strategy; log why tools are skipped (unavailable, empty results, low relevance) rather than assuming they're inaccessible.
- When a research prompt explicitly enumerates specific tools (code search, documentation search, etc.), proactively clarify tool availability before starting; if unavailable, state this and pivot to best-available alternatives, rather than silently defaulting to web search alone.
- When a research prompt explicitly lists multiple tool categories (internal + external) and emphasizes 'ALL available tools aggressively,' prioritize internal tools (code search, engineering context, internal RAG) in the first 2-3 research waves before defaulting to public web search, as internal sources often contain the most relevant and recent information.
- When a research prompt explicitly lists multiple tool types and demands aggressive exhaustive use, attempt at least one call to each mentioned tool type before settling on a primary search strategy, and document in reasoning why unavailable tools were skipped.
- When a research prompt explicitly lists specific tool categories (code search, documentation search, chat, engineering context, web), prioritize those specialized tools proportionally in the first 30% of turns rather than backloading them; if a specialized tool is mentioned by name, use it at least 3-5 times before falling back to generic alternatives.
- When a research prompt explicitly lists specific tools (code search, documentation search, chat search, etc.), treat each as a required modality to attempt, not optional. Before committing to a research direction, enumerate and attempt all named tool types, or explicitly explain why each is inaccessible.
- When a research prompt explicitly lists specific tools (e.g., 'code search, documentation search, chat search, engineering context, web search, web fetch, pipeline inspection'), attempt to use all of them in sequence, not just the most obvious ones (web search). If a tool is unavailable, state this clearly in the response.
- When a research prompt explicitly lists specific tools and says 'use ALL...aggressively,' attempt at least one call to each named tool category before defaulting to a single dominant tool. If a tool is unavailable, log the attempt and reason before proceeding.
- When a research prompt explicitly names a prioritized tool sequence (e.g., 'code search, documentation search, chat search, THEN web search'), enforce that sequence in order before pivoting; do not skip to web-first strategy unless internal tools are unavailable or explicitly exhausted.
- When a research prompt explicitly names specific tool types (code search, documentation search, engineering context), interpret these as *required attempts* rather than optional—make at least one call to each named tool before defaulting to web search, and note in reasoning if tools are unavailable rather than silently omitting them.
- When a research prompt explicitly narrows scope (e.g., 'Focus on the OTHER pillars and AIP-wide strategy' or 'don't re-research what's already covered'), prioritize tool calls matching the narrowed scope first; use broader discovery only to fill identified gaps, not as initial strategy.
- When a research prompt explicitly requests 'exhaustive' analysis across multiple sources and specifies minimum tool call counts, maintain a parallel tool execution strategy and document reasoning for system prioritization to prevent subjective scope drift.
- When a research prompt explicitly requests structured output (FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS) and specifies 'no files', deliver the complete structured block in plain text before the session ends, even if research is incomplete; mark uncertain claims clearly with recency_class=undated and corroboration=none rather than omitting them.
- When a research prompt explicitly specifies multiple tool types (web search, local search, documentation search) and emphasizes 'cross-reference across sources', issue at least one call to each major tool category within the first 25% of the session, then iterate. Do not batch external sources into the final 10% of the session.
- When a research prompt explicitly specifies tool count targets (e.g., '5–10 tool calls per direction') and 'aggressive' use, open with parallel multi-tool queries across specified tool families in the first 3 turns, rather than sequential single-tool exploration. Frontload diversity.
- When a research prompt instructs to use 'ALL available tools' and 'be exhaustive', explicitly clarify the stopping criterion with the user upfront (time budget, source count, or corroboration threshold) rather than conducting open-ended searches. If clarification is not possible, set a self-imposed limit (e.g., 'max 20 web searches', 'stop after 5 independent sources per claim') and state it in your first response.
- When a research prompt is ambiguous on scope dimensions (e.g., 'blast radius' without definition), ask for clarification on priority order or constraints before launching parallel research; if the user confirms 'exhaustive coverage anyway', proceed but flag the scope explicitly in the final output.
- When a research prompt is underspecified (e.g., missing time window, scope, or terminology definition), ask 2-3 clarifying questions in the first turn before launching tool calls, offering to proceed with reasonable assumptions if preferred.
- When a research prompt is underspecified on key thresholds (e.g., 'user-perceivable'), pause and ask clarifying questions before spending tokens on unfocused tool calls—prioritize 1-2 high-value searches that uncover the domain's actual definition of the threshold rather than 25 exploratory searches.
- When a research prompt lacks temporal scope, threshold definitions, or organizational boundaries, pause after tool inventory and ask for clarification before executing 10+ tool calls, especially when the cost is high (57k+ tokens) or the domain is unfamiliar.
- When a research prompt lists multiple proper nouns that are similar (e.g., 'ORCAA' and 'Babl'), explicitly verify whether they are distinct entities or variants of the same organization in your first tool batch; if ambiguous, surface the ambiguity to the user before proceeding with full research.
- When a research prompt lists multiple questions with only one marked 'Priority: high', explicitly ask the user to confirm scope boundaries (e.g., 'Should I defer questions 1–7 to focus entirely on the GDPR/retention direction?') rather than inferring prioritization through exploration, to avoid wasting turns on misaligned scopes.
- When a research prompt lists multiple specific tool types (code search, documentation search, engineering context, etc.), attempt to invoke each tool category explicitly before defaulting to web-only research; if a tool fails or is unavailable, acknowledge the limitation and explain why the fallback strategy was chosen.
- When a research prompt lists multiple tool categories (internal and external), exhaustively attempt all internal tools (CodeSearch, ChatSearch, EngineeringContext) before escalating to web tools. Log skipped tools with reasoning.
- When a research prompt lists multiple tool categories and explicitly mandates their use ('ALL available tools aggressively'), attempt each tool type explicitly before narrowing strategy. If tools are unavailable, state this constraint clearly and justify the revised approach rather than silently switching to a single tool category.
- When a research prompt lists multiple tool classes and requests 'exhaustive' or 'all available' tool usage, attempt each specified tool class at least once and log why each is abandoned. If an internal tool (code search, docs, context) fails, explicitly report the failure and explain the fallback strategy before proceeding.
- When a research prompt lists specific internal tools (code search, chat search, engineering context) that are unavailable, explicitly acknowledge this constraint early and propose a fallback strategy (e.g., 'Internal tools are unavailable; I will conduct exhaustive web research instead'). Do not silently omit tool calls from the requested set.
- When a research prompt lists specific tool categories (code search, documentation search, chat search, web search, web fetch), attempt at least one call per category before defaulting to the most accessible tool (web search). If some tools are unavailable, explicitly state which were attempted and why they failed.
- When a research prompt lists specific tool families (web search, web fetch, pipeline inspection, etc.), explicitly invoke each one at least once and document why/how each contributed evidence, rather than relying on a subset of tools. Narrate tool selection rationale explicitly before execution.
- When a research prompt lists specific tool types (code search, docs search, chat search, etc.), explicitly attempt each category before defaulting to web-only research. Log reasoning for any tools skipped, and prompt the user for clarification if a tool is unavailable rather than silently omitting it.
- When a research prompt lists specific tools (code search, documentation search, engineering context), attempt those tools first before defaulting to generic web search, even if availability is uncertain. Use tool errors to guide fallback strategy rather than assuming unavailability.
- When a research prompt lists specific tools (e.g., 'code search, documentation search, chat search, engineering context, pipeline inspection'), and the AI detects mention of deferred or unavailable tools in search results, pause and explicitly attempt to load or clarify availability of those tools before proceeding with only the subset that loaded successfully.
- When a research prompt lists specific tools and demands exhaustive use of 'ALL available tools,' explicitly check tool availability and report which tools are inaccessible before beginning research. If tooling is constrained, negotiate scope or note the constraint in the final output.
- When a research prompt lists specific tools, attempt each tool class explicitly (even if to log 'unavailable') before defaulting to web search; if tools fail or are unavailable, report the limitation in your response preamble.
- When a research prompt lists tool priorities (e.g., 'code search, documentation search' before 'web search'), attempt tools in that order before defaulting to web search; only pivot to web research after primary sources are exhausted.
- When a research prompt lists unavailable tools (e.g., internal code search, pipeline inspection) alongside available ones (web search, fetch), acknowledge the gap explicitly in your first response and confirm which tools you will prioritize, rather than silently omitting them. This sets user expectations and flags environment constraints early.
- When a research prompt mandates 'use ALL available tools' and lists 7+ tool types, explicitly attempt each category before defaulting to the most available tool (e.g., WebSearch), and report which tools were unavailable or inapplicable rather than silently omitting them.
- When a research prompt names specific tools (e.g., 'code search, documentation search, chat search'), clarify whether unlisted internal tools are in scope before deploying them. If internal tools are needed, ask first or note the assumption in your reasoning turn.
- When a research prompt prescribes 'at least N tool calls' with an 'aggressive' modifier, set a diminishing-return checkpoint at 2×N calls; if later calls return no novel corroboration, summarize findings rather than continuing to saturate the tool budget.
- When a research prompt provides local context files, vocabulary bootstrap, or references to internal systems, prioritize internal tools (code search, documentation search, chat/messaging, engineering context, pipeline inspection) in the first 30% of tool calls before escalating to external web search; treat local context provision as a signal that internal sources are primary.
- When a research prompt references a future date or speculative event, explicitly flag the temporal constraint, ask whether the user is describing a hypothetical scenario, leaked roadmap, or test case, and adjust corroboration standards accordingly before proceeding.
- When a research prompt references a specific team/project name (e.g. 'MDM') without defining it, make an explicit clarification query (via ToolSearch or NECP) by turn 5 to confirm whether it's a formal team name or functional grouping, then state the finding before executing the main research phase. This prevents late-stage pivots and ensures findings are grounded in correct team identity.
- When a research prompt requests 'MAXIMUM thoroughness' and specifies claim-corroboration tracking (single_source|multiple_sources|none), preemptively create a corroboration matrix before finalizing output to ensure at least 60% of claims are backed by multiple_sources, escalating search intensity for undercorroborated claims.
- When a research prompt requests 'aggressive multi-tool' investigation, explicitly alternate tool types in sequence (web search → web fetch → code search → web search) to ensure cross-source corroboration rather than clustering tool calls by type.
- When a research prompt requests 'aggressive tool use' and cross-referencing, make tool calls in parallel batches (3-5 per turn) and consolidate results asynchronously rather than sequentially; this reduces wasted turns on organization and accelerates evidence discovery.
- When a research prompt requests 'exhaustive' coverage with 'MAXIMUM thoroughness', interpret this as: first identify 5-10 highest-confidence source categories via quick reconnaissance; then allocate tool calls to depth-dive those categories rather than broad skimming. Validate tool success before reattempting.
- When a research prompt requests 'exhaustive' cross-source investigation, explicitly confirm internal system accessibility (which engineering context endpoints exist, whether Slack/Sourcegraph/manuals RAG have historical depth for the topic) in turns 1–3 before launching reconnaissance, to avoid exploratory dead-ends.
- When a research prompt requests 'exhaustive' findings across internal systems, structure tool activation in waves (public → internal docs → team communication → live metrics) and announce each pivot explicitly to maintain transparency and allow mid-session course correction.
- When a research prompt requests 'exhaustive' investigation with explicit tool mandates, define a stopping criterion upfront (e.g., 'stop when 3+ independent sources corroborate the same claim, or after 50 tool calls, whichever comes first') to avoid incremental redundant searches that consume tokens without adding evidence density.
- When a research prompt requests 'exhaustive' or 'aggressive' tool use, establish a stopping rule: halt fetching when no new corroboration paths emerge from the last 3 consecutive sources, or when claims are corroborated by 2+ independent sources, whichever comes first. Document this reasoning in internal reflection turns to avoid over-fetching.
- When a research prompt requests 'exhaustive' results with 'EVERY available tool', establish an explicit synthesis checkpoint after 50-60 tool calls and present preliminary findings before continuing deeper research, allowing the user to redirect or accept the current depth.
- When a research prompt requests exhaustive tool usage, use a tool inventory check early: explicitly list available tools, confirm scope of 'all available tools' with the user, and only skip categories if unavailable—avoid silent omissions of entire tool classes.
- When a research prompt requests specific quantitative data (e.g., metrics, counts, volumes), immediately validate access to the primary data source before executing exploratory queries; if access is denied, escalate this as a blocking issue within the first 3 turns rather than cycling through 40+ exploratory tool calls.
- When a research prompt requests structured output in a specific format (e.g., FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS), produce that exact output block immediately after tool execution completes, before attempting additional tool calls. Include a token-aware check: if >80% of budget consumed, synthesize findings and output rather than continue searching.
- When a research prompt requires access to auth-gated dashboards or data sources, clarify access constraints and request credentials/links at the start rather than discovering access failures mid-session; offer to proceed with publicly available data as fallback.
- When a research prompt seeds you with a specific definition or framing (e.g., 'MFH = Model Feature Hub'), validate that definition with a dedicated search query before launching full-scale investigation. Use a single targeted tool call to confirm the premise before scaling to exhaustive research.
- When a research prompt specifies 'MAXIMUM thoroughness' and 'at least 5-10 tool calls', track and report cumulative tool call count after major research phases to provide explicit progress against stated thresholds, rather than stopping when coverage feels sufficient.
- When a research prompt specifies 'MAXIMUM thoroughness' combined with 'medium priority' and token/cost constraints, explicitly estimate effort (tool calls, turns, tokens) before beginning and confirm with a brief summary of the research plan (e.g., 'I will conduct 5 search waves covering [topics], expecting ~40 turns'). This allows the user to course-correct scope before heavy tool usage.
- When a research prompt specifies 'MAXIMUM thoroughness' or 'be exhaustive,' define an explicit stopping condition upfront (e.g., 'stop when each claim has 2+ independent sources' or 'stop after 15 tool calls') rather than continuing until token budget pressure forces conclusion.
- When a research prompt specifies 'aggressive' tool usage across multiple named tool types, explicitly enumerate which tool types are being invoked in the first synthesis turn and confirm coverage; if a named tool cannot be invoked, state the constraint and justify why (e.g., 'CodeSearch unavailable; proceeding with WebSearch + WebFetch').
- When a research prompt specifies 'aggressive' tool use across a named list of tool types (code search, documentation search, chat search, etc.), explicitly attempt each tool type and document why it was skipped if unavailable, rather than implicitly prioritizing some tools over others.
- When a research prompt specifies 'aggressive' tool use with a numeric minimum, front-load an explicit check: list which tools are actually available in the environment before committing to promised tool counts, and notify the user immediately if promised tools (e.g., 'code search', 'engineering context') are unavailable, rather than silently substituting alternatives.
- When a research prompt specifies 'at least N tool calls per direction' or 'exhaustive search', explicitly report the tool call count and distribution (e.g., 'X calls to NECP, Y to Sourcegraph, Z to rag-slack') before moving to synthesis, to validate methodological compliance and avoid false checkpoints.
- When a research prompt specifies 'direct' relationships (e.g., 'feed direct GenAI commitments') without explicit criteria, ask the user to define the relationship before querying, rather than inferring from heuristics like naming patterns or tags.
- When a research prompt specifies 'exhaustive' and tool-count minimums, establish an internal rubric early (e.g., 'coverage threshold: 15+ distinct sources, 3+ tool types, claims cross-referenced where possible') and state it aloud to confirm alignment before executing 20+ tool calls.
- When a research prompt specifies 'exhaustive' or 'aggressive tool use' without explicit success criteria, explicitly confirm inferred context (e.g., 'I infer AIP is a Netflix internal monorepo — is this correct?') before beginning systematic searches, to avoid early-stage exploratory waste.
- When a research prompt specifies 'exhaustive' or 'maximum thoroughness' and tool count minimums, signal progress and intermediate findings at midpoint (after 50% of planned tool calls) rather than waiting until completion, so the user can course-correct on scope/sources if desired.
- When a research prompt specifies 'exhaustive,' 'aggressive,' and explicit tool-call minima (e.g., '5-10 per direction'), treat that as an implicit license for extended autonomous execution (20+ turns) without intermediate progress summaries unless explicitly requested.
- When a research prompt specifies 'maximum exhaustiveness' with multiple tools, prioritize corroboration of novel insights (e.g., 'Workbench is a platform, not a single IDE') over volume of calls; after identifying a key insight, use 2–3 targeted follow-up searches to verify rather than continuing broad surveys.
- When a research prompt specifies 'structured output only' and defines an explicit output schema, save and compile findings incrementally across turns, then emit the final STRUCTURED_OUTPUT block immediately before session conclusion to ensure the user receives the required format even if session context truncates.
- When a research prompt specifies 'use ALL available tools aggressively' and lists 8+ tools, enumerate them explicitly in the first turn and attempt at least one call per tool class before pivoting to deep-dive; if a tool is unavailable, log it and explain fallback strategy to the user.
- When a research prompt specifies 'use ALL available tools,' inventory and name all accessible tools in the first turn before tool execution, then explicitly sequence them by expected information yield (high-signal first). This ensures no tool categories are forgotten and provides the user visibility into the strategy.
- When a research prompt specifies a domain (e.g., 'MDM products') without explicit vendor or scope clarification, ask for or infer the scope from provided vocabulary bootstrap files before executing 5+ tool calls, and state assumptions clearly in the final output to flag potential scope misalignment.
- When a research prompt specifies a low-priority direction with domain-specific vocabulary (e.g., 'calibration as a safety property'), immediately query the vocabulary bootstrap file, then execute at least two parallel web searches with distinct phrasing before fetching; escalate to website fetches only after confirming search results are insufficient.
- When a research prompt specifies a minimum tool call threshold and requests exhaustive cross-referencing, execute tool calls in parallel batches by category (external → internal → implementation-specific) rather than sequential single calls; batch tool invocation reduces thinking-step overhead and accelerates convergence on comprehensive evidence.
- When a research prompt specifies a named system or product that appears not to exist in public sources after exhaustive searching, explicitly surface this finding early (before the final output) and ask for clarification on whether the user meant a different product name, a hypothetical system, or an internal-only system.
- When a research prompt specifies exhaustive scope (10+ questions, 17+ entities to map, multiple dimensions: org, OKRs, incidents, talent, competitive analysis) across >4 data sources within a single session, proactively estimate coverage feasibility after turn 5-8, and explicitly signal to the user if scope appears to exceed reasonable completion within the turn/token budget, offering to either narrow scope, extend budget, or parallelize across multiple sessions.
- When a research prompt specifies external tool categories (web search, documentation, chat search) before local tools, prioritize external searches first and reserve local file reads for context enrichment only after establishing the external evidence baseline.
- When a research prompt specifies internal tools (code search, documentation search, engineering context, pipeline inspection) before web search, prioritize local/internal tools first and explain why you are pivoting to web search if those tools are unavailable or fail.
- When a research prompt specifies internal/proprietary tool availability (e.g., netflix_search_api, rag-slack-prod), explicitly confirm scope before invoking them; if the prompt provides local seed files, skim but do not deeply read them before pivoting to primary research, as they are typically metadata, not content.
- When a research prompt specifies multiple independent search directions with 'at least 5-10 tool calls per direction,' batch tool calls into logical clusters (e.g., turns 9-10 for NECP, 11-23 for manuals, 25-27 for Slack) with reflection pauses between clusters rather than interleaving single calls, to maximize efficiency and surface high-confidence patterns early.
- When a research prompt specifies multiple sub-questions with mixed priority labels, explicitly confirm with the user which questions are in-scope before beginning tool execution, rather than inferring prioritization from labels alone.
- When a research prompt specifies multiple tool categories (code search, docs search, chat search, engineering context, web, pipeline), explicitly invoke at least one tool from each category before relying on web search alone; if internal tools fail or return empty, log that decision and explain why web sources became the fallback.
- When a research prompt specifies multiple tool categories and emphasizes 'aggressive' / 'exhaustive' use across sources, explicitly list and invoke each tool category before focusing depth on any one. Reserve final turns for structured synthesis of findings; never let single-tool exploration consume the entire turn budget without output synthesis.
- When a research prompt specifies multiple tool modalities by name, construct explicit tool-use batches before search execution: allocate minimum coverage targets per modality (e.g., '2-3 code searches, 2-3 doc searches, 2-3 web searches') and validate against the specified list before proceeding; this prevents conflating 'aggressive tool use' with 'repetitive use of one tool'.
- When a research prompt specifies multiple tool types (code search, web search, documentation search, etc.), explicitly acknowledge each type in planning reasoning and attempt at least one call from each category before narrowing scope—if certain tools prove unavailable, explain why in a clarification turn rather than silently omitting them.
- When a research prompt specifies quantitative tool call targets (e.g., 'at least 5-10 calls'), explicitly confirm interpretation of scope ('per turn' vs. 'per direction' vs. 'total') in the first substantive turn, and flag if diminishing returns suggest earlier completion without hitting the target.
- When a research prompt specifies quantitative tool deployment (e.g., '5-10 tool calls per direction'), explicitly count and document tool calls in intermediate summaries, then cross-reference against the quota before finalizing output to ensure the threshold was met and justify any departures.
- When a research prompt specifies tool-use intensity targets (e.g., '5-10 tool calls per direction'), log the count and distribution of tools used at each decision point; if falling short, explicitly state which tools remain untried and why before proceeding.
- When a research prompt specifies tools in a particular order and emphasizes 'ALL available tools,' begin with local/internal tools (code search, documentation, engineering context) before pivoting to web search, and always ensure the final STRUCTURED_OUTPUT block is explicitly returned in the response text, not deferred.
- When a research question has a clear answer found in primary sources (code, README, config), stop executing corroborating searches after 1-2 additional triangulations. Exhaustiveness does not require gathering redundant evidence beyond convergence threshold.
- When a research request explicitly instructs 'use ALL available tools aggressively' and 'exhaustively', do not compile findings or list follow-ups as deferred work until you have queried every accessible tool class (web, docs, code, data/SQL, messaging, specialized APIs). If gaps remain after tool exhaustion, only then note them as unfillable.
- When a research request seeks granular demographic or personnel data by job level for a specific organization, always surface confidentiality concerns in your initial response and clarify which sources are acceptable (public disclosures vs. internal systems) before executing tool calls, rather than inferring boundaries mid-session.
- When a research request specifies 'MAXIMUM thoroughness' and quantitative metrics (MAU, ROI, conversion %), clarify availability and measurement constraints in the first 5 turns; pivot to alternative sources or suggest methodology upfront rather than discovering gaps at the end.
- When a research request specifies a narrow legal domain (e.g., VPPA) but yields sparse results, pause and ask the user whether to expand scope to adjacent regulatory actions (GDPR, DPA, FTC) before proceeding, rather than expanding unilaterally.
- When a research request specifies multiple tool categories (internal + external), explicitly acknowledge which tools are available in the execution environment at turn 1, and if any requested tool category is unavailable, ask the user whether to proceed with available tools or seek alternative approaches.
- When a research request specifies structured output format with metadata (e.g., recency_class, corroboration levels), explicitly cross-check every claim against multiple independent sources before classifying corroboration as 'multiple_sources' rather than 'single_source'; document which sources independently validate each claim.
- When a research request targets a specific named entity (team, project, tool, person) that you cannot immediately confirm exists in available sources, pause and ask the user to validate the name or provide an alternative identifier before launching exhaustive searches. This prevents false-premise research cycles.
- When a research request targets historical data (dates > 30 days in past) from time-series systems, immediately surface data retention constraints and propose alternative collection methods (documentation, inference, recent proxy data) before launching queries.
- When a research session exceeds 50 turns or 60K tokens without a specified endpoint, proactively emit the final synthesis block before turn 64, even if additional sources could theoretically be gathered; prioritize delivering the required output format over marginal gains in source coverage.
- When a research session reaches 70+ turns with escalating token consumption, proactively summarize intermediate findings in a compact structured format and offer to continue in a follow-up session rather than risk truncation mid-output.
- When a research session requires a structured output format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks, JSON arrays, specific field schemas), explicitly confirm successful generation of that format in a final turn before marking completion, even if you must summarize inline.
- When a research session requires structured final output in a specific format, always synthesize and render the output block immediately upon detecting a tool error or resource exhaustion, rather than terminating mid-execution. Never assume downstream async delivery.
- When a research session requires structured output with specific schema (JSON arrays with defined fields like {claim, source, corroboration, recency_class}), always generate and display the complete block before session end, even if intermediate turns execute research; validate schema completeness in the final turn before considering the session concluded.
- When a research session specifies 'exhaustive' coverage with 'at least N tool calls', establish a stopping condition explicitly after reaching 1.5-2x the minimum (e.g., 15 calls for 5-10 minimum) rather than continuing until context feels saturated; this reduces token waste while maintaining thoroughness.
- When a research target becomes inactive/archived mid-session, immediately reduce its investigation depth to 1-2 validation searches max, then reallocate remaining budget to deprioritized active competitors or deeper analysis of remaining targets. Flag the status change explicitly to the user before pivoting.
- When a research task asks for information about non-existent or confidential products, explicitly state the blocker and offer the closest public alternative rather than silently abandoning the task after tool acknowledgment.
- When a research task asks for output in a specific structured format (like STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END with named sections), explicitly confirm in the final turn that the output is being returned in exactly that format, and include the opening tag before consolidating results.
- When a research task asks to 'find manager + ICs' for an internal org unit, explicitly verify first whether the unit still exists as a cohesive team and whether granular roster data is available in the searched repositories; if not, report the structural discovery and note what data sources would be required.
- When a research task authorizes 'ALL available tools aggressively', explicitly confirm tool scope with the user before deploying filesystem/internal tools not mentioned in the visible prompt context; distinguish between 'tools I have access to' and 'tools aligned with this task's spirit'.
- When a research task calls for 'aggressive' or 'exhaustive' tool usage, establish a stopping rule upfront (e.g., 'continue until three consecutive searches return no new sources' or 'fetch top 5 unique papers'). Avoid open-ended search loops by tracking source novelty across turns and explicitly noting when diminishing returns occur.
- When a research task calls for 'exhaustive' multi-source corroboration, explicitly log the tool-call chain (which tools returned results, which returned empty, which were inconclusive) in the final output's FINDINGS section so the reader understands coverage gaps and source reliability.
- When a research task demands 'aggressive' parallel searches across internal APIs, explicitly track tool call budget (e.g., 'Planning 8–12 calls across 4 tools') and log which prior calls targeted overlapping keywords to avoid quadratic redundancy; halt and consolidate when new queries return diminishing unique information.
- When a research task demands 'aggressive' tool use with minimum call counts, structure searches in explicit parallel waves (discovery → broad search → targeted drill-down) and track cumulative tool calls against the stated minimum to ensure thoroughness before synthesis.
- When a research task demands exhaustive multi-turn investigation, establish explicit stopping conditions in turn 1 (e.g., 'stop when you have ≥3 independent corroborations per major claim' or 'search until no new sources emerge in 2 consecutive batches'), and report stopping condition met before returning final output.
- When a research task directs use of 'prior artifacts' or assumes context from earlier work, explicitly ask the user for file paths, links, or clarification rather than assuming such artifacts exist or referencing them without evidence.
- When a research task emphasizes 'MAXIMUM thoroughness' and 'EVERY available tool,' batch tool calls in waves (e.g., 4 parallel WebSearches, then 6 parallel WebFetches) rather than sequential single calls; this reduces latency and signals confidence in exhaustive coverage.
- When a research task emphasizes internal tools (code search, documentation search, engineering context) and provides internal file paths, prioritize those tools first and invoke them explicitly before falling back to web search; never skip named tool categories.
- When a research task explicitly calls for 'aggressive' multi-tool usage and 'exhaustive' cross-referencing, batch tool invocations into parallel waves (e.g., WebSearch + session_search + table_search in same turn cluster) rather than sequential single-tool calls, to maximize efficiency without sacrificing thoroughness.
- When a research task explicitly demands 'ALL available tools aggressively' and lists specific modalities (local + web + chat), treat web search/fetch as mandatory unless explicitly constrained, not optional. Do not call TaskOutput or TaskStop until all listed tool categories have been exercised.
- When a research task explicitly demands 'MAXIMUM thoroughness' and specifies minimum tool call counts (5-10+), launch tools in parallel batches (6-8 simultaneous or near-simultaneous calls) rather than sequentially, and validate that actual tool call count exceeds stated minimum before concluding.
- When a research task explicitly demands tool exhaustiveness and cross-validation, explicitly narrate the research funnel's phases (reconnaissance, parallel gathering, targeted validation) to the user before executing, and signal phase transitions transparently to show methodology is intentional rather than unfocused.
- When a research task explicitly forbids file writes, do not use TodoWrite or file-creation tools mid-session, even for scaffolding; instead, maintain state in message context or use read-only analysis tools to avoid protocol violations.
- When a research task explicitly lists and emphasizes 'available tools' (especially web search, documentation APIs, or external sources), verify tool availability in the first two turns and activate all specified tool categories before proceeding to analysis. If a tool is unavailable, confirm constraints with the user immediately rather than silently substituting local-file-only methods.
- When a research task explicitly lists specific tools (CodeSearch, ChatSearch, EngineeringContext, PipelineInspection, WebSearch, WebFetch), prioritize internal/codebase tools first before pivoting to public web search. Log the rationale for any tool selection, and if a tool is unavailable, state that explicitly before substituting.
- When a research task explicitly mandates 'aggressive' tool use with minimum tool call counts, begin executing target tools in the first 2 turns rather than spending turns on local context reading. Read local files only if they are explicitly required preconditions for external tool calls.
- When a research task explicitly mentions prior research sessions or internal artifact repositories, prioritize session_search and file inspection tools over broad tool catalog searches; use tool discovery only if the artifact location is unknown after initial session searches.
- When a research task explicitly prohibits file writes ('Do NOT write any files'), avoid tool calls like TodoWrite that produce persisted state. Use internal reasoning/scratch space instead, or confirm with the user first.
- When a research task explicitly requests 'aggressive tool deployment' and '5-10+ tool calls per direction,' establish a tool-call quota counter and explicitly verify coverage before synthesis; pause mid-session to audit tool types used and redirect if falling below specified minimums.
- When a research task explicitly requests 'aggressive' tool usage with numeric minimums (5–10 calls 'per direction'), cluster tool calls into logical batches and confirm the count aloud before finalizing output, signaling to the user that the mandate was met.
- When a research task explicitly requests 'exhaustive' analysis with numbered tool-call minimums, structure the exploration in explicit phases (discovery, validation, synthesis) and state phase transitions aloud before executing, to reduce planning overhead and improve turn efficiency.
- When a research task explicitly requests 'maximum thoroughness' with 'at least 5-10 tool calls per direction,' batch multiple searches into single turns where possible, but use explicit planning turns (without tool calls) to segment conceptual waves—do not skip the planning turns as they provide valuable checkpoints for self-correction.
- When a research task explicitly requests aggressive tool use with specific tool inventories, structure the attack plan in the first turn by listing which tools map to which search angles (e.g., 'Sourcegraph keyword → code presence, Slack → incident history, web search → external threat landscape'), then execute parallel batches rather than sequential exploration.
- When a research task explicitly requests structured output in a specific format (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks with FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS), always conclude with a final synthesis turn that produces the exact format requested, placed immediately before the tool calls that would invalidate it—never end on a tool call.
- When a research task explicitly requires 'MAXIMUM thoroughness' and names prior-failure, execute tool calls in parallel batches with explicit pauses between waves (setup → broad search → deep search → synthesis) to avoid premature convergence and ensure independent evidence gathering before synthesis.
- When a research task explicitly requires a structured output format (e.g., FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS blocks), always include a final turn after tool gathering that synthesizes results into the exact requested format before considering the task complete.
- When a research task explicitly specifies a tool palette (e.g., 'code search, documentation, chat search, engineering context, pipeline inspection'), attempt at least one call with each specified tool type before pivoting to a narrower strategy; document the reasoning if tools are unavailable rather than silently abandoning the original mandate.
- When a research task explicitly specifies tool counts ('at least 5-10 per direction'), interpret this as permission to parallelize aggressively and prioritize breadth of sources over sequential verification. Deploy tools in waves rather than serially, and announce which tools you're calling and why.
- When a research task explicitly targets confidential organizational data (attrition rates, exit interviews, compensation), surface this constraint to the user immediately in the first 2 turns rather than spending >20 turns attempting workarounds; offer to pivot to publicly available proxies (job postings, LinkedIn moves, published departures) with explicit confidence caveats.
- When a research task includes time-sensitive qualifiers (e.g., 'today,' 'current'), explicitly confirm the target date/time window with the user or state assumed interpretations in the findings before dispatching tools.
- When a research task involves primarily web-based sources, prioritize WebSearch and WebFetch over local system tools; if local file search is needed, establish clear hypotheses about what data exists locally before pivoting away from web research.
- When a research task involves proprietary internal systems (memory tiers, cost models, engineering contexts), explicitly ask the user whether internal repos and engineering context tools contain credible data before defaulting to web search; state which tool categories will likely yield null results and propose alternative evidence paths.
- When a research task involves searching for potentially non-existent or future artifacts (e.g., Llama-5), explicitly surface the existence/release status of each artifact early and update the user before spending 20+ turns searching for it.
- When a research task is marked low-priority, confirm whether to allocate 46 turns vs. establish an iteration budget (e.g., max 20 turns) to avoid over-research and optimize token efficiency for low-impact directions.
- When a research task lacks an explicit list of 'named entities' (e.g., 'named judges'), explicitly state the inferred target set early in the session and verify against the user's expectation before executing deep research, to avoid investigating the wrong scope.
- When a research task lacks crisp definitions for key entities (e.g., 'AIMS, Studio, NGP', 'consumer orgs', 'champion'), pause before tool calls to ask the user for 1-sentence clarifications, or note in the first turn that these are assumptions and verify them via initial search results.
- When a research task lacks explicit scope boundaries (org, product, region), state your inferred scope explicitly in the first substantive turn and request confirmation before executing 20+ search queries, rather than inferring silently.
- When a research task lists multiple tool categories (local, web, API-based), explicitly clarify which tools are available in the environment before beginning investigation, and allocate tool usage proportionally across categories rather than exhausting local tools first.
- When a research task lists multiple tool types (code search, doc search, chat search, web search, etc.) and requests 'ALL available tools aggressively,' explicitly attempt each category in turn and log which tools were unavailable or deemed inapplicable before defaulting to web sources alone.
- When a research task lists specific internal tools (code search, chat search, engineering context, pipeline inspection), attempt at least one call to each before defaulting to web search, even if their availability is uncertain. Only switch to web-primary if internal tools return empty or error.
- When a research task mandates 'aggressive' tool usage across multiple modalities (code search, doc search, web search, fetch, etc.), distribute tool calls proportionally across all five modalities in the first 10 turns rather than concentrating on web search; this ensures early discovery of internal engineering context before pivoting to external research.
- When a research task mandates 'aggressive' tool use with explicit call count targets (e.g., '5-10 per direction'), parallelize and cluster tool invocations in early turns rather than spreading them across the session; aim for tool density in turns 2-6 to enable early convergence and pivoting to specialty tools (engineering context, proprietary APIs) by mid-session.
- When a research task mandates 'use ALL available tools aggressively,' immediately enumerate and deploy each tool category (web search, code search, web fetch, CLI inspection, chat search, etc.) in parallel or rapid sequence in the first 5 turns, before attempting synthesis. Do not treat tool categories as fallbacks; treat them as mandatory concurrent streams.
- When a research task references prior session artifacts or outputs (/root/.sagaflow/runs/...), prioritize session/artifact search tools before attempting broad web or code search; internal context is often more valuable for enterprise research tasks than external sources.
- When a research task relies on internal tool definitions (e.g., 'Runway,' 'registry'), confirm the referent against available documentation or explicitly flag the assumption to the user before consuming significant tokens on tool calls that may target the wrong system.
- When a research task requests 'ALL available tools' and specifies tool diversity, attempt each tool type early (one dedicated turn per tool category). If a tool returns no results, explicitly document the null result and move to the next tool rather than substituting repeated calls to a single tool.
- When a research task requests 'EVERY available tool' and 'MAXIMUM thoroughness', explicitly enumerate which tools are being attempted before execution, and after each tool failure, explicitly state the fallback strategy to avoid silent pivots that may leave the user uncertain about coverage.
- When a research task requests 'aggressive' tool use or '5-10 calls per direction', structure the approach with explicit tool type sequences (web search → fetch → local verify) and commit immediately without planning delays; announce the strategy, then execute in rapid succession without intermediate empty turns.
- When a research task requests 'confidential internal financial data' (e.g., company spend breakdowns, budget allocations, cost comparisons), immediately ask the user to clarify which sources are accessible and whether they have authorization to share findings. If primary sources fail (e.g., JIRA authorization denied), do not attempt circular workarounds—escalate with a specific list of what you need and why public sources cannot fulfill the requirement.
- When a research task requests 'controlled comparison' evidence, explicitly search for papers containing phrases like 'controlled experiment', 'A/B test', 'statistical significance', or 'ablation study' rather than treating all comparative literature equally; flag results that lack experimental controls.
- When a research task requests 'exhaustive' cross-referencing with explicit source attribution, decompose the target system into 3-5 sub-queries (one per major component: architecture, ranker logic, refresh cadence, etc.) and run parallel tool sweeps for each sub-query rather than fewer broad queries, to maximize independent corroboration evidence.
- When a research task requests 'exhaustive' investigation across N tools, structure the search in explicit phases (discovery → focused → code-level → validation) and use parallel tool calls within each phase rather than serial execution to reduce total turn count and demonstrate thoroughness.
- When a research task requests 'exhaustive' investigation with 'at least N tool calls,' establish an explicit stopping criterion upfront: either a target claim count, a corroboration threshold (e.g., 'stop when 80% of claims have 2+ sources'), or a time budget. Clarify with the user before beginning if the prompt is ambiguous.
- When a research task requests 'exhaustive' multi-source cross-reference, prioritize independent external sources and explicitly note when pivoting to cached internal research, and clarify with the user whether cached findings satisfy the cross-reference requirement or if fresh research is still needed.
- When a research task requests 'exhaustive' or 'MAXIMUM thoroughness,' set an explicit stopping rule upfront: e.g., 'After 15 tool calls, if evidence is not yet corroborated by 2+ independent sources, pause and write a partial structured output documenting what was found and what gaps remain, rather than continuing to search indefinitely.'
- When a research task requests 'exhaustive' or 'maximum' effort without explicit stopping criteria, propose a stopping rule (e.g., 'I will stop when each claim has 2+ independent sources, or after 25 tool calls') and wait for confirmation before executing; if no response, use pragmatic sufficiency (marginal evidence gain < 10%) as default stop condition.
- When a research task requests 'exhaustive' output in a structured format, explicitly checkpoint and deliver the structured block incrementally (after each research phase or every 10–15 turns) rather than accumulating for a single final turn, to prevent truncation and allow user feedback before completion.
- When a research task requests 'exhaustive' results with underspecified scope (no success criteria, ambiguous entity names, no turn budget), ask for clarification on scope boundaries or success metrics in the first turn before beginning search loops, rather than inferring and iterating.
- When a research task requests a specific output format (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END with named sections), always conclude by delivering that exact format before closing, even if you must briefly summarize findings rather than continue tool calls.
- When a research task requests exhaustive cross-referencing with multiple tool types, explicitly verify at the start which tools are available (via ToolSearch or tool list), then create a search matrix mapping research questions to tool types, executing parallel queries where possible and documenting the source and corroboration status for every claim before final synthesis.
- When a research task requests exhaustive evidence gathering with claim-level provenance, explicitly track tool-use diversity (count unique tool types called) and plan to reach a minimum threshold (e.g., 5+ distinct tool categories) before synthesizing; this forces comprehensive source coverage and prevents over-reliance on a single search method.
- When a research task requests exhaustive tool usage with explicit numerical targets (e.g., '5-10 tool calls per direction'), begin tool execution immediately in turn 1 without intermediate narration; validate tool diversity coverage (code search, web search, fetch, commit search, etc.) explicitly before consolidation.
- When a research task requests exhaustive tool use with explicit output structure, articulate the search strategy and hypothesis in a reasoning turn before launching tools, then pause after each batch of 4-5 searches to corroborate findings and assess whether subsequent queries should shift direction.
- When a research task requests internal tools (code search, pipeline inspection, engineering context) that fail or return empty, explicitly surface this constraint to the user and propose a revised scope (e.g., 'Internal tools unavailable; proceeding with public-web and cached documentation search only') rather than silently pivoting strategy.
- When a research task requests specific tool categories (code search, chat search, internal tools) but you fall back to web-only searches, explicitly state at turn 1 what tools are unavailable and why, and confirm the alternate strategy aligns with the user's intent before proceeding.
- When a research task requests structured output (FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS format), explicitly confirm the output format in an early turn and summarize findings-in-progress at regular intervals (every 10 tool calls) to ensure coherence before final synthesis.
- When a research task requests structured output in a specific format (FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS blocks), always explicitly confirm completion of that structure in your final response before claiming success, and output the full block even if very long.
- When a research task requests structured output with a specific template (FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS), always conclude by generating and displaying that output block in full, even if it requires summarizing intermediate findings. Verify the template is complete before ending the task.
- When a research task requests structured output with specific metadata fields (corroboration, recency_class, URLs), explicitly confirm at the end of the session that all required fields have been populated before marking the task complete.
- When a research task requires 'exhaustive' cross-referencing, explicitly parallelize web and internal source queries after bootstrapping local context; alternate tool types (web vs. internal) to maximize corroboration signal and minimize linear iteration time.
- When a research task requires a structured output block as the sole deliverable, always render the complete STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END block as plaintext in the final turn, even if the formatting is imperfect, and explicitly state the turn number where the block appears so the user can verify completion.
- When a research task requires a structured output block with explicit formatting (FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS), always generate and display that block in full before concluding the session, and confirm that all required fields are populated.
- When a research task requires accessing regulated/proprietary information (e.g., FDA regulatory submissions, internal company docs), explicitly note in the response which sources are likely unavailable and pivot early to alternative strategies (e.g., published summaries, public filings, patent disclosures) rather than exhausting search budget on dead-end queries.
- When a research task requires exhaustive synthesis with a specific output format (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START block), emit intermediate synthesis and partial results after each 3-5 tool calls, then produce the final structured output explicitly before ending the session. Do not chain search-only turns without showing what was found.
- When a research task requires internal/proprietary information that is structurally inaccessible (e.g., Netflix internal processes, private guild negotiation records), explicitly flag this constraint to the user at task start and propose alternative scoping (e.g., 'I can research public statements and contracts, but not internal review processes') rather than proceeding with inherent false expectations.
- When a research task requires returning structured output in a specific format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks, JSON, etc.), confirm that the required deliverable is being constructed after every 5 tool calls, and consolidate and return the output immediately upon reaching the minimum tool call threshold or when tool results plateau, rather than continuing to search indefinitely.
- When a research task requires structured output (FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS blocks), explicitly confirm receipt of all source URLs and key claims before synthesis, and always return the final formatted block inline in the response rather than deferring to a separate turn.
- When a research task requires structured output (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks), always explicitly generate and include that block in the final turn, even if synthesis is incomplete; signal incompleteness via 'IN_PROGRESS' or 'PARTIAL' labels rather than omitting the block entirely.
- When a research task requires structured output in a specific format, confirm the format is understood, attempt to populate at least one example section before invoking tools, and return the complete structured block in full before ending the turn—even if findings are partial.
- When a research task requires structured output synthesis (prose findings, source arrays, claim metadata), always include the complete rendered output block in the final turn, even if lengthy, and explicitly confirm which findings were corroborated by multiple independent sources vs. single sources.
