- When a research task requires structured output synthesis, emit periodic synthesis checkpoints (every 5-7 turns) showing provisional FINDINGS/CLAIMS with source counts to confirm progress and allow early course correction before full session completion.
- When a research task requires structured output with claims, pause before final output generation to explicitly list findings by category (repos, teams, docs, URLs) and confirm each claim has ≥2 independent sources before marking corroboration as 'multiple_sources'.
- When a research task requires structured output with explicit START/END markers, always echo the completed structured output block in full at the session conclusion, even if it duplicates information, to confirm the required format was honored.
- When a research task requires structured output with specific formatting (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END, SOURCES array, CLAIMS with corroboration levels), confirm schema interpretation in the first substantive turn before executing 20+ tool calls, to avoid misaligned synthesis.
- When a research task requires synthesis into a specific structured output format, explicitly add a meta-step after gathering evidence: 'STOP_SEARCH_AND_SYNTHESIZE' check. If tool calls have yielded 5+ sources per major theme without new findings for 3 consecutive searches, synthesize immediately into the required format rather than continuing to search.
- When a research task requires tool invocation, summarize key findings from each tool call before proceeding to the next tool; explicitly note null/empty results and decision logic for tool sequencing so the user can audit evidence gathering in real time.
- When a research task rests on a factual claim about a person's role or affiliation, validate the claim through 2+ independent sources before beginning substantive research. If the claim is contradicted, escalate this immediately with source evidence rather than continuing to secondary angles without user confirmation.
- When a research task specifies 'ALL available tools,' explicitly probe tool availability (e.g., attempt code search, Slack search, internal docs) in early turns; if unavailable, document this constraint and adjust scope in subsequent turns rather than silently defaulting to public web sources.
- When a research task specifies 'MAXIMUM thoroughness' but leaves vendor/scope open-ended, explicitly enumerate and prioritize candidate vendors before launching tool calls, then group calls by vendor to improve source corroboration and reduce redundant searches.
- When a research task specifies 'MAXIMUM thoroughness' or 'exhaustive' coverage, implement a three-phase strategy: (1) breadth-first parallel tool calls to map scope, (2) targeted depth extraction from N top sources, (3) gap-closure loop with explicit termination criteria (e.g., 'no new claims from last 3 searches'), and skip premature completeness self-assessments that could short-circuit coverage.
- When a research task specifies 'aggressive tool use' and a tool inventory count (5-10 calls), prioritize rapid tool discovery (1-2 turns max) before entering the search phase, rather than interleaving discovery throughout execution.
- When a research task specifies 'aggressive tool use' and multiple failure modes, create an explicit search matrix (mode × dimension) and track coverage per mode before concluding, to ensure thoroughness is verifiable rather than inferred.
- When a research task specifies 'aggressive tool use' with quantified minimums (e.g., 'at least 5-10 tool calls'), explicitly design parallel search strategies upfront (e.g., 'search for names + orgs + recent activity + roadmap docs') before executing tools, to minimize redundant or failed queries.
- When a research task specifies 'aggressive' multi-tool exploration with numeric targets (5-10+ calls), schedule tool calls before synthesis—use early turns to saturate all available tool types, then consolidate findings in later turns, rather than interleaving synthesis breaks that reduce tool call density.
- When a research task specifies 'aggressive' tool usage with numerical minimums ('5-10 calls per direction'), insert a brief turn immediately after tool execution pauses to explicitly state which directions were covered and tool call counts per direction, enabling midcourse validation.
- When a research task specifies 'at least N tool calls per direction' and multiple domains are plausible, explicitly state assumed domain scope in the first synthesis turn and ask for correction if misaligned, rather than inferring silently.
- When a research task specifies 'at least N tool calls,' track and report tool-call count before synthesis; explicitly state which tools were used and in what order, and explain why synthesis was reached (saturation vs. time-box) before returning final output.
- When a research task specifies 'exhaustive' analysis with tool-based cross-referencing, explicitly log the tool call sequence (search term, tool type, source count) at key checkpoints (25%, 50%, 75% completion) to enable verification of methodology adherence and detect redundancy early.
- When a research task specifies 'exhaustive' and 'all available tools', cluster tool calls by type (e.g., context tools first, then code search, then external search) rather than alternating randomly; this improves signal-to-noise and allows earlier stopping when saturation is detected.
- When a research task specifies 'exhaustive' and 'at least N tool calls per direction', rotate through distinct tool types (keyword vs. NLS vs. commit history vs. file read) rather than repeating identical queries—this improves signal diversity and reduces redundancy.
- When a research task specifies 'exhaustive' or 'aggressive tool use,' adopt a time-boxed or source-count-based stopping rule: 'Execute 8–12 tool calls per sub-question, then synthesize; do not continue beyond diminishing returns (repeated sources or no new claims).'
- When a research task specifies 'exhaustive' or 'aggressive' tool usage without a stopping criterion, establish an internal diminishing-returns threshold: stop after 3 consecutive tool calls return no new key entities or facts, or after 20 calls total, whichever comes first. Surface this threshold in a clarification turn if the scope is ambiguous.
- When a research task specifies 'exhaustive' with multiple tool types, implement an early stopping heuristic: after 3 consecutive tool calls return zero new claims or corroboration, move to synthesis rather than continuing exploratory queries.
- When a research task specifies 'exhaustive' with tool counts (5-10+ calls per direction), establish a stopping rule upfront: 'Research continues until three consecutive tools return no new claims, or 40 turns are reached, whichever first.' Return interim findings at 50% and 75% of budget so the user can redirect if data is insufficient.
- When a research task specifies 'leading product' or similar evaluative claims, explicitly state assumed evaluation criteria (e.g., adoption >10k users, funding >$5M, or research citations) before conducting searches, rather than inferring weighting silently.
- When a research task specifies 'return ONLY as structured output in text response (not in a file)', do not invoke file-writing or progress-tracking tools (TodoWrite, file creation) during exploration; reserve all tool invocations for data gathering, and emit the final structured block as a single coherent text response.
- When a research task specifies 'use ALL available tools' and lists tool categories, explicitly list the tools attempted (including null/empty results) in the final output to prove coverage, or note why specific tools were deprioritized.
- When a research task specifies 'use ALL available tools,' explicitly inventory and attempt each named tool category in sequence before defaulting to web search; log which tools were unavailable or why each was skipped to enable user transparency on scope limitations.
- When a research task specifies a minimum number of tool calls (e.g., '5-10 tool calls'), explicitly confirm in the response which tool calls were executed and summarize results from each before synthesizing findings; cite tool results inline in claims.
- When a research task specifies a minimum tool-call threshold (e.g., '5-10 tool calls per direction'), establish an explicit tracking mechanism (e.g., 'Tool Call Budget: 8/10') and report progress transparently at midpoint turns to confirm alignment with the user's exhaustiveness requirements.
- When a research task specifies a quantitative threshold (e.g., 'at least 5-10 tool calls per direction'), log tool call counts and direction boundaries explicitly in thinking/narration so the user can verify compliance; this prevents ambiguity about whether the threshold was met.
- When a research task specifies a required output format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END), explicitly confirm receipt of that requirement and return the formatted output as the final turn—never end a research session without the explicitly requested structured format.
- When a research task specifies a required structured output format (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks), always synthesize findings into that exact format and display it to the user before session end, even if tool results are partial or inconclusive.
- When a research task specifies a structured output format (JSON, key-value, or tagged blocks), always end the final turn with the complete output block, even if synthesis is uncertain; never defer formatting to a future turn or assume it will be appended.
- When a research task specifies a structured output format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks with named sections), explicitly confirm the format has been generated before closing the session. Use a turn solely to verify completeness of the output artifact.
- When a research task specifies a structured output format (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START...END), establish an explicit synthesis checkpoint after the first 2-3 tool cycles, review intermediate findings, and commit to synthesis within a bounded turn count (e.g., 'search for 10 turns, then spend 3 turns writing the output block').
- When a research task specifies a structured output format with multiple required fields (FINDINGS, SOURCES, CLAIMS, etc.), explicitly confirm format completion in the final turn before closing, or show a sample of the structured output inline so the user can validate correctness and completeness.
- When a research task specifies exhaustive output format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks) and runs beyond 40 turns, explicitly generate the final structured output block every 10 turns or when productivity drops below 0.85, rather than accumulating evidence indefinitely.
- When a research task specifies exhaustive tool usage, pre-plan a tool-call budget and sequence (broad→deep→cross-validate) to avoid redundant queries, and track distinct tool *types* used (not just call count) to ensure methodological diversity.
- When a research task specifies exhaustive tool use ('ALL available tools'), explicitly enumerate which tools exist in the current environment before committing to a tool strategy, and if certain tools (e.g., code search, internal documentation) cannot be invoked, surface this gap to the user rather than defaulting to web-only search.
- When a research task specifies exhaustiveness and multi-source cross-referencing, explicitly state the source corroboration strategy upfront (e.g., 'will validate each claim against 2+ independent sources before marking as multiple_sources') and track it in a running checklist visible in intermediate reasoning, not just in final output.
- When a research task specifies internal tools (code search, engineering context, pipeline inspection) that are not immediately available or unclear, explicitly ask or attempt discovery before defaulting to web search; document the tooling constraint in your reasoning.
- When a research task specifies multiple tool categories (code search, documentation search, chat search, engineering context, web search, web fetch, pipeline inspection), explicitly attempt at least one query with each before settling on a primary tool; if a tool yields no results, note the attempt and rationale in the session.
- When a research task specifies multiple tool categories but only some are available in the environment, explicitly note at the start of research which tools are available and which are not, and request clarification on priorities if key tools are unavailable.
- When a research task specifies multiple tool types (code search, documentation search, chat search, engineering context), explicitly confirm tool availability and try each category in order before defaulting to web search; if unavailable, state which tools were inaccessible and why web search was chosen instead.
- When a research task specifies multiple tool types and priorities, attempt at least one call to each specified tool category before defaulting to a single tool, and explicitly report which tools are unavailable rather than silently omitting them.
- When a research task specifies multiple tool types to use 'aggressively,' explicitly confirm available tools in the first turn and create a turn budget allocating calls across each tool type; do not default to a single tool even if it is productive.
- When a research task specifies niche benchmark names (LoCoMo, MemoryBench) without providing DOI/URLs, spend the first 2-3 tool calls explicitly verifying whether these are published, arXiv, or internal benchmarks before conducting 20+ search iterations.
- When a research task specifies required output format (e.g., structured blocks with FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS), always render the complete formatted output at the end of research, before closing the session, even if intermediate findings are incomplete.
- When a research task specifies structured output format and tool-call minimums, execute at least 2 tool calls in the first turn before waiting for feedback, and synthesize+output results before the session ends rather than leaving tool execution hanging.
- When a research task specifies structured output format with STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END delimiters, always conclude by explicitly displaying that formatted block in the final response, even if content is partial or interim; do not assume the user will infer synthesis from tool call descriptions alone.
- When a research task specifies structured output requirements (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks with named fields), explicitly confirm delivery of the complete formatted output block before marking the task complete; do not assume synthesis occurred if the final formatted payload is not visible in the transcript.
- When a research task specifies structured output with named fields (FINDINGS, SOURCES, CLAIMS), confirm completion by echoing the final structured block in full before session end; if the user does not see it, mark as incomplete even if internal research was thorough.
- When a research task specifies structured output with validation criteria (e.g., SOURCES must include URLs, CLAIMS must note corroboration level), always perform a final validation pass before marking the task complete, and explicitly confirm to the user that all required fields were populated and cross-reference validation succeeded.
- When a research task specifies tool counts (e.g., '5-10 tool calls'), log each tool invocation with a counter at the start of tool-use phases and emit a summary (e.g., 'Tool Invocation Count: 28 across 4 tool categories') before final synthesis to demonstrate compliance.
- When a research task specifies tool usage minimums (e.g., '5-10 tool calls per direction'), group tool calls into parallel batches and label each batch with the direction/hypothesis it tests, rather than executing sequentially, to reduce transition overhead and improve signal-to-noise ratio.
- When a research task targets metrics (coverage %, trends, subsystem breakdowns) that are not found after 3+ systematic searches across code, Slack, and docs, explicitly surface to the user the data-availability gap and ask whether to (a) continue searching, (b) report 'data unavailable', or (c) pivot to a proxy source—rather than continuing circular re-queries.
- When a research task targets proprietary infrastructure from closed labs, preemptively acknowledge access limitations and state an explicit fallback strategy (e.g., academic papers, public GitHub, patent filings, conference talks) before attempting direct fetches that will likely fail.
- When a research task yields negative findings (absence of evidence), explicitly state the search threshold (how many sources checked, how many queries issued) and offer hypothesis refinement (e.g., 'If partnership exists but is undisclosed, consider: [list of indirect signals]') before concluding.
- When a research topic task requests language identification, always log or echo the key phrases from the source file that justified the selected languages and confidence level, even if not explicitly requested, to enable auditing of language-selection reasoning.
- When a research-generation task requires tool invocation, always: (1) explicitly list the tools you will call before starting, (2) invoke and log results inline, (3) cite results when deriving each new direction, (4) stop and ask for clarification if tools are unavailable or returns are empty.
- When a research/discovery task lists a specific tool hierarchy or enumerated set of tools, execute them in the stated order and confirm coverage of each tool class before transitioning to the next. Log skipped tools and rationale before choosing alternatives.
- When a research/exploration prompt explicitly lists multiple tool categories (e.g., 'code search, documentation search, engineering context'), attempt at least one call from each category before concluding the search is exhaustive, even if web search alone produces definitive results.
- When a response will contain a large structured output (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START...END with FINDINGS, SOURCES, CLAIMS sections), estimate token budget for the complete output BEFORE beginning composition. If insufficient tokens remain, summarize findings in prose instead of incomplete structured format, or emit a warning and defer. Never emit a truncated structured block.
- When a retrieval tool (RAG, code search, or API call) returns an error, explicitly summarize to the user what data may be missing and how it affects corroboration confidence for downstream claims, rather than silently retrying.
- When a search tool (API, RAG, etc.) returns empty or null results across 2+ consecutive calls, explicitly log the failure, record the queries tried, and state why you are pivoting to an alternative tool before proceeding; surface this diagnostic to the user in a verbose session if multiple sources fail.
- When a search-based research task receives null/empty results from 3+ different tool types in succession, immediately: (1) report what was searched, (2) state 'no results found', (3) ask the user whether the source exists and is indexed, or proceed to structured output stating 'sources unavailable'. Do not pivot to unrelated tool types (e.g., Bash) without explaining why.
- When a seed prompt provides a specific personal name or identity claim, always validate against at least 2 independent sources (commits, Slack, NECP, or org charts) within the first 5 tool calls before committing research direction; if validation fails, surface the discrepancy immediately rather than proceeding with the given name.
- When a service/tool fails to respond (status 503, timeout, no results after 1 call), skip it immediately and document the failure; do not retry the same dead endpoint without explicit user guidance or evidence of recovery.
- When a single external request fails (e.g., WebFetch, API timeout), acknowledge the failure explicitly, state what evidence you already have, and pivot to alternative tools within the same category (e.g., WebSearch instead of WebFetch) before concluding that a direction is exhausted.
- When a single tool category (e.g., Bash filesystem exploration) produces unclear or context-free results for 5+ consecutive turns, explicitly reassess whether that path is advancing the research goal and pivot to a different tool or approach rather than continuing iteratively.
- When a single tool class (e.g., RAG searches) shows low hit rate or sparse results after 3–5 attempts with keyword variations, immediately pivot to an orthogonal tool (code search, eng-context, external web search) or explicitly propose narrower scope to the user rather than repeating the same failing approach.
- When a single tool fails (e.g., rag-manuals-prod returns error), immediately switch to an alternative tool for that content type (Sourcegraph, Grep, or web search) on the next turn; do not retry the same failing tool more than once per session without explicit user intervention.
- When a single tool fails with a non-fatal error (4xx, timeout), immediately attempt the next tool in the available toolset rather than reporting the error as terminal. Maintain a ranked fallback list of tools for each research direction and cycle through it until either success or all options are exhausted.
- When a single tool is called 3+ times with identical or near-identical queries, explicitly document the hypothesis being tested and pivot to an alternative tool, fallback strategy, or null-result acknowledgment rather than repeating the same call.
- When a single tool returns repeated 404s or errors despite URL/parameter variations, abandon it after 2 failed attempts and switch to an alternative tool (e.g., Sourcegraph file reads instead of documentation endpoints) rather than retrying with incremental tweaks.
- When a skill document prescribes mandatory environment variables or authentication (e.g., DJINN_USERNAME, Metatron auth), validate their presence and correctness with a brief env check or try-except before executing the skill's Phase 1 query; document the validation result explicitly in the reasoning before proceeding.
- When a source-fetch tool (WebFetch, API call) fails 3+ consecutive times with 403/403/timeout/domain-block errors, immediately fall back to alternative sources without additional retry loops; document the fallback in the session log and proceed.
- When a specialized research task requests 'maximum thoroughness' with a numbered tool target (e.g., '5-10 tool calls per direction'), immediately batch-execute tool calls in parallel clusters by category (WebSearch × 4, then WebFetch × 3, then specialized tools × 2) rather than sequential single calls, then wait for results before the next cluster—this reduces total turns by ~20% while maintaining research quality.
- When a specialized research task requests tool diversity (code search, web search, documentation search), validate tool availability early (turn 1-2) before committing to a multi-turn strategy; if certain tools (Grep, internal repos) prove unavailable after 1-2 probing calls, pivot completely to available tools rather than retrying.
- When a specialized tool (e.g., Netflix-internal MCP) is unavailable, explicitly acknowledge the constraint in the first tool call attempt, then immediately pivot to the next-best alternative without waiting for user re-prompting.
- When a specific primary source (e.g., a named webinar or announcement) cannot be located after 5-10 targeted searches, explicitly switch strategies to corroborating evidence (earnings calls, SEC filings, third-party coverage) rather than repeating the same search queries. Signal this pivot to the user as a 'source not found; pivoting to alternative evidence strategy' before continuing.
- When a specific tool (WebFetch, API call, database query) fails consistently across 2+ attempts with the same error pattern, immediately switch to alternative data sources and log the limitation rather than continuing retry loops; document the failure mode in findings so the user understands coverage gaps.
- When a stated tool (Read, code search, engineering context) produces no output or fails silently, explicitly report the failure and name the alternative tool being used instead, so the user understands the constraint and can adjust expectations.
- When a stop-gate hook or user correction flags incomplete output, acknowledge the gap explicitly, then rewrite/extend the incomplete section in full without claiming prior completeness. Do not deflect or reframe the correction as misunderstanding.
- When a structured API query fails (e.g., opensearch), attempt direct resource fetching as a fallback before reporting failure; document the pivot in reasoning to show adaptive strategy.
- When a structured output block is interrupted (cut off mid-sentence or token limit hit), immediately recognize the incomplete state, explicitly signal the truncation point, and either resume the output from the break point or notify the user that output was incomplete — do not claim completion or defend the output as intact without verifying all closing brackets and terminal punctuation are present in the actual response text.
- When a structured output format is explicitly required (with START/END markers or fixed JSON schema), always validate that the complete formatted output is generated and visible before concluding the turn. If analysis will be long, summarize findings in prose first, then emit the structured block in full.
- When a structured research prompt specifies multiple sub-requirements (successor name, transition plan, scope continuity, morale impact), explicitly surface which sub-requirements have been satisfied with strong evidence vs. partially satisfied or unmet before returning the final output, and note the confidence level and source gaps for each.
- When a structured research task requests 'exhaustive' analysis with 5-10+ tool calls, explicitly track tool call count and source diversity mid-session and report progress at 25%, 50%, 75% checkpoints to validate that coverage targets are being met before final synthesis.
- When a structured research task requests 'total confirmed headcount' for sub-teams, request explicit clarification on acceptable sources (org chart, Slack, manuals, internal API) and corroboration threshold (single source vs. multiple independent sources) before beginning exhaustive search, to avoid wasting turns on redundant tool calls.
- When a subagent or specialized mode is invoked, validate tool restrictions against the user's explicit tool inventory in the current prompt before disabling capabilities. If constraints conflict with user instructions, surface the conflict and request clarification rather than silently disabling tools.
- When a subagent prompt invokes governance or skipping rules not explicitly stated in the user's message, ask for clarification rather than assume and execute—state the assumption and wait for confirmation before proceeding.
- When a synthesis or novelty-assessment task references a prior knowledge base (e.g., cumulative-coordinator-summary.md), explicitly ask the user to provide or link it upfront, before ingesting new files, to ground the INFO_GAIN_RATE calculation against ground truth rather than inference.
- When a synthesis task requires large structured output (>1000 tokens), explicitly estimate token budget in the first turn, break the task into phases (gap analysis → direction generation → validation), and commit to completing each phase before proceeding to the next. If a phase cannot complete in the remaining budget, pause and ask the user to continue in a new turn rather than returning partial output.
- When a task explicitly constrains information retrieval (e.g., 'Do NOT WebSearch' or 'from memory only'), do not invoke file read, search, or lookup tools; instead, state upfront if the constraint cannot be met and request clarification before attempting tool calls.
- When a task explicitly lists multiple tool types (code search, documentation search, web search, etc.), execute at least one call from each category before defaulting to iteration on the most convenient tool; track tool diversity as a completion criterion.
- When a task explicitly requests structured output in a specific format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks), always confirm delivery of that block in the final turn, and never rely on tool invocation logs to substitute for the actual prose findings.
- When a task explicitly requires a specific output format (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END block), verify that format has been produced before closing the session, and if tool calls don't surface results in the transcript, either retry with clarified parameters or explain what went wrong rather than silently ending.
- When a task explicitly requires structured output in a specific format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks), always render the complete formatted output in the final turn, even if intermediate turns include prose summaries. Verify the format matches the template before ending the session.
- When a task explicitly specifies a structured output format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks, JSON schemas, etc.), validate and deliver that exact format at the end before concluding, and confirm delivery in the final turn before assuming task completion.
- When a task explicitly specifies required output format and deliverables (structured output blocks, JSON arrays, etc.), validate that you will produce this format before beginning work, and stop intermediate exploration once you have sufficient evidence to synthesize the final deliverable—do not let tool exploration cycles run without a defined endpoint that produces the contracted output.
- When a task forbids writing files (e.g., 'Do NOT write any files'), treat file-writing tools (Write, TodoWrite, FileWrite) as out-of-bounds for that task and skip them entirely, even if they appear contextually useful; rely on text-based tool returns and final response output.
- When a task involves reading many files (>20), estimate completion with current context before deciding to dispatch parallel agents. If dispatching, explicitly tell the user and wait for approval before proceeding. If not waiting, collect and synthesize all agent outputs before returning to the user.
- When a task prompt includes a complete work package (inline specification + explicit output format) and also references an external file, prioritize analyzing the inline content first and deliver preliminary structured output before attempting external I/O. Ask clarifying questions synchronously only if critical ambiguity blocks analysis.
- When a task requires a final structured output block (JSON, STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END, etc.), always render it before closing the turn, even if incomplete; never assume the transcript will continue or that results will be shown in a later turn.
- When a task requires comparative assessment of novelty (INFO_GAIN_RATE, gap analysis, contradiction detection), explicitly confirm access to and readability of baseline/prior documents in turn 1 before commencing large batch reads; surface missing baselines immediately rather than proceeding with partial information.
- When a task requires delivery of a specific structured output format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks), always emit the complete block in the final turn before session close, and explicitly confirm its presence in a prefatory summary turn.
- When a task requires internal/proprietary data (e.g., Netflix POC results), query accessible resources and check authentication before dispatching external searches; if internal systems fail after 2-3 retries, immediately pivot to prior research artifacts or documentation caches rather than cycling through failed approaches.
- When a task requires reading an external file, always echo back the first 2–3 lines of content to confirm successful load before proceeding with analysis. If file read fails, state that explicitly and ask the user to provide the content inline or verify the path.
- When a task requires reading external files AND producing detailed structured analysis, verify token budget up front and warn the user if the combination exceeds safe margins. If budget is tight, offer to analyze the spec inline or split the task across multiple requests.
- When a task requires reading large external files and producing structured output, explicitly confirm file readability in the response and estimate output size before starting generation; if the response will exceed token budget, summarize the approach and ask for permission to split output into chunks.
- When a task requires structured output in a specific format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks, JSON arrays with required fields, etc.), always emit that block explicitly before the final token of the response, and validate that all required fields (FINDINGS, SOURCES, CLAIMS with claim/source/corroboration/recency_class) are non-empty.
- When a task requires structured output with explicit delimiters (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END) and an array of objects, estimate token cost upfront and reserve space for completion. If approaching limit, truncate analysis gracefully before opening the output block, or output a partial-completion notice rather than an incomplete/unparseable artifact.
- When a task requires structured output with specific formatting (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks), explicitly confirm synthesis is complete and render the full formatted block before concluding the task, even if tool calls are still ongoing.
- When a task specifies a structured output format (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START blocks), explicitly compose and emit that block in the final turn, even if intermediate work was done across prior turns. Do not defer synthesis to implied post-processing.
- When a task specifies an explicit output format (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/FLAWS|[...]), parse the spec/content immediately and populate that structure directly in the first substantive response, rather than opening with prose explanation. Structure first, narrative second.
- When a task specifies an explicit output format (especially structured blocks with delimiters like STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END), always deliver that exact format as the final turn, even if intermediate research has already been conducted. Synthesize findings into the requested format before concluding the session.
- When a task specifies tool usage but the requested tool suite is not available, explicitly list the tool availability gap and propose adjusted methodology before proceeding, rather than silently substituting with available tools.
- When a tool (WebFetch, API call, code search) fails on 3+ attempts with the same error (SSL, 403, timeout), immediately pivot to alternative tools rather than retrying the same approach; log the failure and move on.
- When a tool (WebFetch, code search, etc.) is called but unavailable, immediately switch to a named alternative strategy and explain the fallback clearly to the user rather than silently retrying or continuing with degraded efficacy.
- When a tool (e.g., WebFetch) fails consistently across 2+ attempts, explicitly state the restriction and pivot to alternative tools rather than retrying the same blocked method.
- When a tool (e.g., WebFetch) fails in a research loop, explicitly log the failure, switch to the next tool tier (WebSearch), and note that tool as degraded for future attempts rather than retrying the same tool in the next batch.
- When a tool access pattern fails (e.g., WebFetch blocked), immediately declare the barrier, propose two alternative strategies, and ask the user which to pursue rather than silently falling back to repetitive calls of a different tool.
- When a tool becomes unavailable (e.g., Sourcegraph unreachable), immediately pivot to the next available tool in the same turn rather than acknowledging the failure in a separate narration turn; batch the pivot action and continue gathering evidence without breaking momentum.
- When a tool becomes unavailable (e.g., WebFetch blocked), document the constraint and pivot to an alternative without attempting the same tool repeatedly; prioritize coverage breadth over exhaustive single-tool attempts.
- When a tool becomes unavailable (e.g., WebFetch restricted), explicitly log the constraint and document it in the final output so the user understands what data sources were attempted vs. achieved.
- When a tool call fails (e.g., API error), log the failure once and immediately pivot to alternative sources without retry; do not attempt the same tool twice in succession without explicit user guidance.
- When a tool call fails (e.g., authentication, format error), immediately inspect the error message and attempt a corrected invocation within the same turn or next turn without waiting for user guidance; document the fix inline.
- When a tool call fails (error return), immediately document the failure mode (e.g., 'namespace not found', 'query syntax invalid') before switching tools, and on the next successful result, validate that the workaround is equivalent in coverage to the original intent.
- When a tool call fails (especially fetches), always attempt at least one alternative approach (mirror URL, related endpoint, or different tool) before declaring a source exhausted, unless time/token budget is explicitly exceeded.
- When a tool call fails mid-research (e.g., API error), retry it once immediately before switching strategies, and explicitly log the retry attempt in reasoning. If the second call also fails, then pivot.
- When a tool call fails mid-session, explicitly log and describe the failure in the final output under a 'LIMITATIONS' section, including fallback sources consulted, rather than silently skipping. This preserves transparency and helps users assess coverage gaps.
- When a tool call fails or is skipped (e.g., WebFetch at turn 28), explicitly report the failure to the user with the attempted operation, reason for failure, and fallback strategy taken, rather than silently pivoting to an alternative approach.
- When a tool call fails or returns a block (e.g., access denied, rate limit), explicitly log the failure with the attempted URL and reason, then document which alternate sources or strategies are being attempted as compensation; this prevents silent degradation of research coverage.
- When a tool call fails or returns blocked content after 2 attempts, pivot to data synthesis or return a partial result with confidence caveats rather than re-trying identical queries in a loop; explicitly state data gaps in the output.
- When a tool call fails or returns sparse results (e.g., Slack RAG timeout), immediately pivot to an orthogonal source (e.g., Sourcegraph, NECP, or manuals) rather than retrying the same tool with minor query variations; dedicate at most 1 retry before branching.
- When a tool call fails with a 424 or other API error, immediately: (1) log the error with request details, (2) list which tools are still available, (3) attempt the task with alternative tools, (4) if all tools fail, return a structured error response explaining what was attempted and why, rather than silent failure.
- When a tool call fails with a clear error (e.g., namespace mismatch), immediately retry with correction rather than queuing retries into a batch; report the fix in the same turn to maintain research momentum.
- When a tool call fails with a correctable error (e.g., missing parameter, namespace issue), document the error explicitly in your reasoning before retrying, and note this in your final output under a 'Tool Limitations' section to maintain transparency about data boundaries.
- When a tool call fails with a missing/invalid parameter error, attempt one targeted diagnostic call (e.g., search for tool documentation or try with corrected parameters) before skipping; if the second attempt also fails, skip and log the failure reason explicitly to avoid circular retries.
- When a tool call fails with a name/type error, immediately check tool registry and pivot to alternate tool names rather than retrying the same call more than once. Log all attempted tool names and their errors before retry.
- When a tool call fails with a schema/contract error, immediately inspect the tool's API definition (if available) before retrying, to avoid repeated failures and explain the fix to the user inline.
- When a tool call fails with a timeout/kill signal (exit code 143–144), do not retry the same call within the next 5 turns; instead, declare the constraint explicitly and ask the user whether to continue with partial evidence or adjust the task scope.
- When a tool call fails with an error, immediately inspect the tool signature and error message before retrying; prefer parameter adjustment over retry loops.
- When a tool call fails, always attempt at least one retry or fallback to an alternative tool in the same category before moving forward, especially when the user has explicitly mandated 'aggressive' and 'exhaustive' research.
- When a tool call fails, explicitly log the failure type and immediately cascade to the next planned tool in the research strategy rather than pausing; treat tool redundancy as a feature for corroboration, not an obstacle.
- When a tool call is blocked (WebFetch, API errors, etc.), check for known platform restrictions upfront and skip directly to fallback tools rather than attempting the blocked call.
- When a tool call is issued, always report the result (success, partial data, error, or access denied) before proceeding to the next turn. If a tool returns empty results after 2 attempts, explicitly report this to the user and ask whether to pivot strategy or accept incomplete findings.
- When a tool call returns '(ok)' or unclear success status, explicitly acknowledge the ambiguity to the user and confirm next steps before retrying, rather than silently pivoting; avoid wasting turns on unreliable tool inference.
- When a tool call returns an error (e.g., 404, timeout) during a parallel multi-source research session, explicitly note which source failed, confirm whether the information was already captured from a prior tool call, and move to the next source without re-attempting the same failed endpoint unless there is a strong reason (e.g., auth issue, rate limit that might resolve).
- When a tool call returns an error in a high-priority research task, explicitly note the error in reasoning and retry with an alternative tool or log it as a corroboration gap in the final output rather than silently skipping.
- When a tool call returns an error or null result (especially file reads, list_repos, or API calls), immediately log the failure, state what is unknown, and propose an alternative query before proceeding. Do not silently switch tools without acknowledging the gap.
- When a tool call returns an error, always pause to diagnose the failure mode (transient vs. permanent, rate limit vs. invalid query, etc.) before retrying. If the cause is unclear, escalate to the user with the error message and ask for clarification rather than blindly retrying.
- When a tool call returns null, empty results, or '(ok)' with no visible output, explicitly note the failure and decide whether to retry with refined parameters or move to an alternative source; do not silently continue to the next query.
- When a tool call returns transient failure or timeout, immediately pivot to an alternative tool or data source rather than retrying the same endpoint; explicitly signal the pivot to the user with reasoning (e.g., 'Tool X failed, switching to Tool Y for corroboration').
- When a tool category (bash/internal repos) fails twice in succession without explicit user confirmation of access, pivot immediately to maximize other tool types (web search, fetch, public APIs) rather than retry with syntax fixes; budget remaining turns for breadth over depth on a single failing approach.
- When a tool category fails with an auth error (e.g., Jira in turns 13–14), do not retry that category later unless the user explicitly re-enables it or you detect a credential refresh. Instead, commit fully to alternative sources and document the unavailability.
- When a tool class (e.g., WebFetch) fails repeatedly, explicitly log the failure pattern and document which information sources are becoming inaccessible, rather than silently pivoting, so the user can assess completeness of findings.
- When a tool class (e.g., WebFetch, ToolSearch) fails repeatedly across multiple turns, do not increase volume of similar calls to a different tool in the same class; instead, explicitly reassess whether the information goal is achievable with remaining tools, and document the boundary of what is knowable before proceeding further.
- When a tool encounters transient errors or rate-limit constraints, immediately communicate this to the user with a summary of affected sources and a rationale for fallback strategies, rather than silently pivoting to secondary tools.
- When a tool encounters transient upstream errors, implement exponential backoff (1s, 2s, 4s) between retries and reduce parallelism by 50% for subsequent batches rather than retrying the same parallelism level.
- When a tool fails (e.g., API error, permission denial), explicitly log the failure and the alternative tool or query strategy used, so the research transcript shows resilience and helps users understand gaps in internal tool coverage.
- When a tool fails (e.g., WebFetch TLS errors), log the failure explicitly, attempt one fallback, then continue; do not retry the same tool silently or assume transient failure without evidence.
- When a tool fails (e.g., grep returns error, file not found), explicitly note the failure and attempt at most one retry with a different query/approach; if the retry also fails, move to alternative tools rather than repeating the same tool later in the session without new context.
- When a tool fails consistently (>2 consecutive errors), explicitly surface the failure to the user within the next reasoning turn and request clarification on whether to: (a) persist with alternatives, (b) treat requested data as inaccessible/confidential, or (c) constrain scope to available sources. Do not silently pivot without acknowledging the constraint.
- When a tool fails consistently (e.g., WebFetch blocked for 5+ turns), immediately implement an alternative search ontology (author names, repo searches, citation graphs, temporal filters, semantic variations) rather than cycling the same queries in WebSearch. Log strategy pivot reasoning explicitly and evaluate success within 3-5 turns before further adaptation.
- When a tool fails or returns partial results, immediately attempt 2-3 alternative tools from the specified toolkit before retrying the original tool; avoid repeated calls to the same tool without first exhausting complementary tools.
- When a tool fails with auth/resource errors (Jira, Atlassian, WebFetch), test access with a single low-cost query first; if it fails, acknowledge the inaccessibility upfront and reframe scope to available tools rather than iterating retry attempts across 25+ turns.
- When a tool fails with permission/auth errors (e.g., Jira), skip it immediately and do not retry; instead, diversify to alternative tools. Mark such failures explicitly in reasoning so the user can unblock access if needed, rather than silently consuming turns on non-recoverable paths.
- When a tool invocation fails with parameter/namespace errors, document the error, explicitly state the corrected approach, and verify the fix works before proceeding — do not silently retry with identical parameters.
- When a tool is known to require authentication (e.g., WebFetch on internal Netflix URLs), skip it in favor of authenticated internal tools (Sourcegraph, NECP, rag-slack) and note the constraint explicitly rather than attempting and failing.
- When a tool returns 'unhealthy' or error status on first use, abandon it immediately and switch to the next tool in the arsenal within a single turn; do not retry the same tool more than once per session without explicit user instruction.
- When a tool returns a retriable error (e.g., TLS cert), skip retry and pivot to alternative tools immediately rather than attempting 3+ retries; document the pivot in reasoning and proceed.
- When a tool returns a transient error (e.g., 'upstream error', rate limit, temporary service unavailability), skip it after the first retry and move to alternative tools within 1-2 turns; do not attempt the same tool 2+ times in a row without clear evidence it has been fixed.
- When a tool returns an error (403, blocked, unavailable), fail-fast after 1-2 attempts and explicitly document the degradation rather than attempting the blocked tool 3+ times before pivoting; state the alternative strategy immediately to the user.
- When a tool returns an error (404, timeout, auth failure), attempt one corrective action (URL normalization, auth refresh, parameter validation) before pivoting to an alternative tool. If the correction succeeds, document the pattern for future use.
- When a tool returns an error or no results after 3+ consecutive attempts, immediately pivot to alternative tool classes rather than retrying the same query pattern; implement a 'tool class rotation' strategy (code → context APIs → chat/Slack → web) to maximize coverage velocity.
- When a tool returns an error or produces out-of-scope results (e.g., internal docs for external research), immediately pivot to the next tool without attempting rework; explicitly state the scope mismatch in reasoning before moving forward.
- When a tool returns an error, explicitly pause and log the failure (tool name, query, error message, next action); do not silently switch tools without communicating why or whether the error signals a data access issue vs. a query malformation.
- When a tool returns empty output 3+ times in a row, explicitly pause, diagnose (call tool_list or check error logs), and report the failure to the user rather than retrying blindly. Frame as: 'Tool X is not returning results—this may indicate [cause]. Switching to [alternative approach].'
- When a tool returns empty results or an error, always retry at least once with an alternative query formulation or parameter adjustment before proceeding; if the second attempt fails, log the failure explicitly and note impact on coverage in final synthesis.
- When a tool returns high-volume or noisy results, do not unilaterally reduce scope or deprioritize the tool without explicit user permission. Instead, refine the query, narrow the search parameters, or ask the user for guidance before abandoning the direction.
- When a tool returns monolithic or deeply nested JSON output, immediately attempt to parse it with native JSON parsing before falling back to line-oriented tools like grep; this prevents unnecessary tool pivots and preserves output fidelity.
- When a tool returns oversized results (>10K tokens), immediately constrain the next query by adding specific keywords, date ranges, or explicit NOT clauses rather than retrying the same query format; log the constraint decision to maintain transparency.
- When a tool-specific research direction (web search, code search, documentation) yields initial results, set a maximum retry budget (e.g., 3 failed queries before pivot) and explicitly declare the tool 'exhausted' before switching strategy. This prevents circular retries and forces re-evaluation.
- When a transient tool failure occurs during an exhaustive research task, explicitly re-attempt the tool before downgrading its priority, and log the decision to defer a source with rationale and timestamp.
- When a user demands 'aggressive' or 'parallel' execution of multiple tools, batch tool calls in a single turn using concurrent requests rather than sequential turns; explicitly confirm parallelization in the response frame to show alignment with intensity expectations.
- When a user demands 'aggressive' use of tools, validate whether target URLs are publicly accessible or robot-blocked before attempting WebFetch; pivot to WebSearch summaries for blocked sources rather than consuming turns on predictable failures.
- When a user deprioritizes a research direction explicitly ('Priority: low'), treat it as a scope-bounding directive: focus on delivering that direction completely rather than attempting the full multi-part audit in one session. Acknowledge the prioritization and confirm the narrowed scope before executing.
- When a user emphasizes 'aggressive' multi-tool research with numeric thresholds ('at least 5-10 tool calls'), batch parallel tool calls within a single turn rather than sequential single-tool turns; this reduces token overhead and surfaces correlations earlier.
- When a user explicitly authorizes aggressive/exhaustive tool use with quantitative targets ('at least 5-10 tool calls per direction,' 'use ALL available tools'), interpret this as permission to conduct parallel/cascade searches without optimizing for cost or brevity; prioritize coverage and corroboration over efficiency until the research direction is exhausted.
- When a user explicitly enumerates multiple specific tools and requests 'ALL available tools aggressively,' generate a tool execution plan before starting queries, explicitly listing which tools will be used in what order and why. If only one tool class (e.g., WebSearch) produces results, pause and attempt at least one alternate tool category before continuing the primary tool pattern.
- When a user explicitly enumerates multiple tools to use (e.g., 'code search, documentation search, chat search, engineering context, pipeline inspection, web search, web fetch'), treat this as a requirement for tool diversity, not just tool availability. Track tool usage distribution and aim for at least one call to each specified tool type before declaring research exhaustive.
- When a user explicitly enumerates specific tools to use and requests 'at least N' invocations with 'exhaustive' cross-reference requirements, validate tool availability and map each requested tool to at least one actual invocation before beginning synthesis. Return the list of intended tool calls to the user for confirmation if any tools appear unavailable.
- When a user explicitly forbids an action (e.g., 'Do NOT write any files'), treat it as a hard constraint and verify tool invocations against it before execution. If a tool would violate the constraint, either skip it or note the conflict in reasoning before proceeding.
- When a user explicitly forbids artifact writes (e.g., 'Do NOT write any files'), do not create TodoWrite or file operations at any point in the session, even for intermediate scratchpads. Use only text responses and in-context reasoning instead.
- When a user explicitly forbids file writes ('Do NOT write any files') or specifies 'return ONLY as structured output in text response', do not invoke file-writing tools (TodoWrite, file creation, etc.) for intermediate drafting — use internal reasoning only, or explicitly ask before deviating.
- When a user explicitly forbids file writes, treat all file-writing tools (TodoWrite, WriteFile, etc.) as unavailable in that session, and log a warning if the AI attempts them.
- When a user explicitly forbids writing files (e.g., 'Do NOT write any files'), interpret TodoWrite and similar persistence tools as file-writes and avoid them unless the user explicitly permits them. If you need to track state mid-research, use inline summaries in your text response instead.
- When a user explicitly lists 'ALL available tools' and demands aggressive use, verify tool availability at the start and attempt at least one call from each category; if unavailable, report which tools cannot be accessed rather than silently defaulting to subset.
- When a user explicitly lists available tools by name in a prompt, proactively attempt to invoke each one and report which are unavailable before defaulting to a subset; if certain tools cannot be accessed, state this clearly to the user rather than silently omitting them from the execution plan.
- When a user explicitly lists multiple tool categories and requests 'ALL available tools,' sample and attempt each tool category within the first 3 planning turns before settling on a dominant strategy. If a tool is unavailable, acknowledge the constraint; do not silently omit requested tools.
- When a user explicitly lists preferred tools (e.g., 'code search, documentation search, chat search'), attempt at least one call to each before defaulting to general-purpose tools. If a tool is unavailable, report the unavailability and justify the substitution strategy before proceeding.
- When a user explicitly lists preferred tools or methods and the AI cannot access them, communicate this clearly (e.g., 'Code search and engineering context tools are unavailable; proceeding with web search instead'). Do not silently switch strategies without reporting the constraint.
- When a user explicitly lists required tools (code search, documentation search, engineering context) before general tools (web search), probe for access and use those specialized tools first; if unavailable, explicitly report the limitation and its impact on research completeness before proceeding with fallback methods.
- When a user explicitly lists required tools (e.g., 'code search, documentation search, chat search, engineering context tools'), attempt all of them before pivoting to alternative strategies. If an internal tool yields null results, log the null result and continue to next tool rather than abandoning the suite. Only pivot after all requested tools are demonstrated to return insufficient evidence.
- When a user explicitly lists required tools by name (web search, documentation search, code search), verify availability before execution; if unavailable, state this clearly and propose alternatives, do not silently substitute with unrelated filesystem operations.
- When a user explicitly lists required tools in an initial directive, log which tools are actually available in the environment and, if any mandatory tools are unavailable, proactively flag this gap before proceeding; do not proceed with a narrower toolset without explicit confirmation that the narrower scope is acceptable.
- When a user explicitly lists required tools to use (e.g., 'Use ALL available tools aggressively: [tool1, tool2, tool3]'), treat each tool category as mandatory and invoke at least one per category early in the session. If a tool is unavailable, acknowledge this gap explicitly before proceeding with alternatives.
- When a user explicitly names multiple tool categories and emphasizes 'ALL available tools' and 'aggressively', prioritize tool diversity over search volume. If internal tools (code search, engineering context, chat, pipeline) are unavailable, state this explicitly in the first response before pivoting to web-only search; do not silently substitute web searches as the primary method without acknowledgment.
- When a user explicitly names multiple tool types (e.g., 'code search, documentation search, chat search, engineering context, pipeline inspection, web search, web fetch'), systematically use at least one call of each type before defaulting to any single tool, and log tool selection decisions when narrowing scope.
- When a user explicitly prohibits an action (e.g., 'Do NOT write any files'), treat it as a hard constraint and never invoke related tools (e.g., TodoWrite, FileWrite) during the session, even in planning phases. If you need to draft interim outputs, use internal reasoning only.
- When a user explicitly prohibits file writes ('Do NOT write any files'), treat any artifact-generating tool (TodoWrite, file operations) as out-of-scope; validate each tool call against stated constraints before executing, and log the rationale for any tool that approaches the boundary.
- When a user explicitly requests 'aggressive' or 'exhaustive' tool usage with numeric minimums ('at least 5-10 tool calls'), begin tool invocation immediately in the first turn without planning preamble, and log each tool call with brief rationale inline to maintain transparency on progress.
- When a user explicitly requests 'maximum thoroughness' with tool counts, deploy tools in purposeful phases (local→web→internal→code-level), pausing briefly between phases to consolidate; avoid premature synthesis to prevent backtracking.
- When a user explicitly requests 'tool calls' as method and specifies 'name sources, repos, URLs, docs' as output requirements, execute direct tool calls rather than dispatching sub-agents. Agent delegation loses visibility into tool-call-level evidence needed for source attribution and corroboration claims.
- When a user explicitly requests exhaustive multi-tool research with a minimum tool call count (e.g., 5-10 per direction), execute a structured tool-cycling loop (code search → documentation search → web search → chat search → engineering context) before narrative explanation. Track and report tool call count in real-time to ensure compliance with the minimum threshold before concluding the research phase.
- When a user explicitly requests exhaustive tool usage with specific tool names listed, use each named tool at least once before declaring research complete, and always deliver structured outputs in the exact format requested before ending the session.
- When a user explicitly requests minimum tool call counts or 'aggressive' tool usage, prioritize executing the named tools before invoking stop/skip rules, and deliver structured output as specified in the prompt before marking the turn complete.
- When a user explicitly specifies a structured output format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START...END, JSON, YAML, etc.) with 'return results ONLY as', always append the completed structured output block before session end, even if research is incomplete, and explicitly state completion status in a summary line.
- When a user explicitly specifies tool usage requirements (tool count, tool types, cross-reference instructions), treat these as non-negotiable constraints and invoke the specified tools in turn 1 output before any prose analysis. If tools are unavailable, attempt public alternatives (web search, web fetch) and explicitly state why internal tools could not be invoked.
- When a user explicitly specifies tool usage targets (e.g., '5-10 tool calls per direction'), execute those calls immediately in the first response cycle rather than ramping up over multiple turns; batch tool invocations and report results together.
- When a user forbids file writes and requests only structured output, do not invoke file-writing tools (TodoWrite, Write, etc.) even for internal caching; serialize all findings into the response payload instead.
- When a user inserts a secondary task mid-session, explicitly ask which takes priority before deferring, rather than making a unilateral decision. E.g.: 'I see you've shared the mako-gpu-status skill. Should I run this in parallel with the framework-distribution research, or finish the current task first?'
- When a user lists multiple tool types and emphasizes 'ALL' or 'aggressively,' distribute tool calls across all listed modalities proportionally; don't default to the most familiar tool. For each major research direction, explicitly allocate calls to code search, documentation search, and specialized context tools before relying on web search.
- When a user lists specific tools and mandates 'ALL available tools aggressively,' explicitly attempt each named tool category in sequence before pivoting to fallback tools; if a tool fails, acknowledge the failure and explain why, rather than silently omitting it from the plan.
- When a user lists specific tools to use and marks them as 'available' in a research prompt, verify tool invocation against the list and explicitly invoke each category at least once before defaulting to fallback methods; report which tools were unavailable or why certain classes were deprioritized.
- When a user lists tools in a specific sequence and uses 'ALL' or 'every', attempt at least one call to each tool type in order before converging on the most productive tool category; if a tool returns empty, explicitly note it and continue to the next rather than silently skipping.
- When a user marks a task 'Priority: low' but also requests 'MAXIMUM thoroughness', explicitly ask for clarification on effort allocation rather than defaulting to maximum effort. Stop searching once you have independent corroboration of a finding or clear evidence of absence.
- When a user marks one direction as 'Priority: high', allocate at least 5-10 tool calls to that direction before exploring secondary questions, even if the prompt presents them together. Announce this priority allocation explicitly in your first substantive turn to confirm scope understanding.
- When a user provides a detailed specification/context document in the prompt, begin analysis immediately rather than requesting or fetching supplementary files unless explicitly asked or the primary document references an undefined term.
- When a user provides a full specification or large block of code inline in the prompt, do not request the same content from an external file without explicit user consent. Verify inline content is complete before triggering file reads.
- When a user provides an explicit structured output format requirement (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks with FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS), always generate and emit that format in the final response, even if content is sparse, and confirm completion of the format contract before ending the session.
- When a user provides an explicit structured output template (especially with capitalized section headers like STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END), always deliver that exact format in full at the end, even if the final turn is used entirely for synthesis. Treat template-based deliverables as non-negotiable completion criteria.
- When a user provides both a broad mandate and a narrow 'baseline' subtask with conflicting scope, explicitly confirm prioritization in the first response before committing 20+ turns to either path. Avoid spending >5 turns on a tool that repeatedly fails without escalating, trying alternatives, or requesting access credentials.
- When a user provides explicit constraints (e.g., 'Do NOT write files'), treat them as hard requirements and audit all tool calls before execution to verify compliance; if a tool call (TodoWrite, file write, etc.) would violate a stated constraint, skip it and notify the user rather than executing.
- When a user provides multiple research directions with explicit priority labels, immediately parse and honor priority ordering; allocate initial tool-call budget to high/medium priority directions first, and treat lower-priority context-gathering as supplementary rather than primary focus.
- When a user request includes an explicit output format requirement (especially STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END), always generate and emit that structured output before declaring the session complete, even if it requires 1-2 additional turns.
- When a user requests 'aggressive' tool usage, distinguish between search breadth (multiple query angles) and fetch depth (extracting content from fewer sources multiple times). Prefer depth after achieving coverage; avoid redundant queries on the same topic with minor wording variations.
- When a user requests 'aggressive' tool use with explicit minimums (5-10+ calls), execute tool invocations immediately in the same turn rather than deferring to future turns; every planning statement without concurrent execution counts as a turn lost to coordination overhead.
- When a user requests 'aggressive' tool use with specific counts, execute tool calls immediately without meta-hedging or rule inventions; proceed on the assumption that all named tools are available and that the user's explicit quantitative request (e.g., '5–10 calls per direction') is a minimum performance bar, not a suggestion.
- When a user requests 'all available tools' but specifies tool names that may not exist in the environment, explicitly enumerate which tools were actually available and used within the first 3 turns, and flag any requested tools that could not be accessed.
- When a user requests 'all available tools,' first attempt one cheap discovery call (e.g., a help command or list operation) to confirm which tools are actually available before committing to aggressive multi-tool campaigns; if discovery fails silently, move immediately to known-high-ROI tools (WebSearch, WebFetch, Bash) rather than retrying opaque tools.
- When a user requests 'comprehensive' synthesis with explicit 'no truncation' instructions, confirm depth expectations upfront if the output would exceed 20KB: ask whether to include all N items in-line, use reference tables, or aggregate with exemplars.
- When a user requests 'exhaustive research' with structured output and explicit tool minimums (e.g., '5-10 tool calls'), parallelize search calls in batches of 5-8 and then fetch in a second phase, rather than interleaving; this reduces cognitive overhead and produces cleaner evidence chains.
- When a user requests 'exhaustive' research with 'aggressive' tool deployment, maximize tool call diversity (at least 4+ distinct tool types) and batch similar queries in parallel rather than sequential; measure exhaustiveness by source diversity and cross-referencing, not raw tool call count.
- When a user requests 'exhaustive' research with explicit minimum tool-call thresholds and structured output templates, confirm in the first response that you understand the output format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks, FINDINGS prose scope, recency metadata) before launching tool calls, and state your stopping criteria (e.g., 'I will search until sources saturate or X cross-references are achieved').
- When a user requests 'exhaustive' research with explicit tool-call minimums (e.g., '5-10 per direction'), immediately inventory available tools and assign them to distinct sub-questions in parallel, then log tool-call counts per sub-question to verify the minimum is met before synthesis.
- When a user requests 'exhaustive' research with no explicit stopping criterion, confirm the target number of sources, claims, or token budget before beginning, and periodically surface coverage metrics (e.g., 'Found 23 claims across 8 sources; shall I continue?') to ensure scope doesn't drift.
- When a user requests 'parallel' or 'aggressive' tool use with specific numeric thresholds (e.g., '5-10 tool calls'), execute batch tool calls within a single turn rather than sequential single-tool turns, and confirm batch completion before proceeding to analysis.
- When a user requests 'top N' items without explicit ranking criteria (e.g., frequency vs. severity vs. impact), ask for clarification in the first turn rather than inferring; explicitly state your assumed ranking order and ask for confirmation before executing.
- When a user requests aggressive tool usage with explicit output structure (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks), after tool execution, always synthesize findings explicitly and return the structured output before ending the session. Do not assume the user will parse raw tool calls; always provide the deliverable.
- When a user requests exhaustive research across internal systems, explicitly acknowledge which tools are available and which are inaccessible before beginning; if outputs are truncated, ask the user to resubmit with continuation markers to allow verification of the final deliverable.
- When a user requests exhaustive research using multiple tool types, map each tool type explicitly before execution and invoke at least one tool from each requested category. Do not default to a single tool type (e.g., WebSearch) even if it seems sufficient; the user's explicit enumeration of tools is a requirement, not a suggestion.
- When a user requests exhaustive research with 'aggressive' tool use and resource-intensive operations, explicitly confirm the intended scope and estimated token/cost allocation before executing, and offer a summary checkpoint at 50% completion to allow course correction.
- When a user requests exhaustive research with a specific output format (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START), always complete and deliver that format before ending the session, even if intermediate results are partial. Show the structured output block explicitly in your final response.
- When a user requests exhaustive research with a specific tool list ('code search, documentation search, chat search, engineering context, web search, web fetch, pipeline inspection'), explicitly verify availability of each tool at the start (TURN 1–3) and note which tools are unavailable, rather than silently skipping them.
- When a user requests exhaustive research with aggressive tool use, avoid self-imposed process steps (TodoWrite, note-taking, checkpointing) unless explicitly requested; redirect all effort to tool calls, evidence gathering, and synthesis instead.
- When a user requests exhaustive research with explicit output structure, validate the output schema early (turn 1–5) and map tool strategy to each schema field; explicitly state which tools target FINDINGS, which target SOURCES, and which target CLAIMS corroboration, then execute in phases.
- When a user requests exhaustive research with explicit tool minimums and corroboration requirements, frontload turn 1 with a clarification question about corroboration sufficiency threshold (single independent source, two independent sources, majority consensus, etc.) to avoid producing unvalidatable claims later.
- When a user requests exhaustive research with multiple specific tools and a required structured output format, confirm tool availability in turn 1 and show the final structured output block in full within the response, not truncated or deferred to a separate file.
- When a user requests exhaustive research with multiple tool types (code search, docs, web, engineering context, etc.), execute them in parallel or sequence BEFORE summarizing understanding; treat 'aggressively use ALL tools' as a constraint that must be satisfied before reporting findings.
- When a user requests exhaustive research with specific structured output format and tool quotas, always echo back the exact output schema (FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS with field names) before starting research, and explicitly confirm minimum tool call targets per research direction to avoid ambiguity about completion criteria.
- When a user requests exhaustive research with structured output, always echo back the expected schema before starting tool calls, and end the session by outputting the structured block in full, not just tool calls and intermediate reasoning.
- When a user requests exhaustive structured output (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START...STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_END), generate and display that block explicitly at the end of your final turn, even if intermediate analysis is shown first. Confirm schema compliance before returning.
- When a user requests exhaustive structured output (with explicit start/end markers), confirm synthesis phase completion and emit the full formatted block before ending the session, even if intermediate outputs are visible during research.
- When a user requests exhaustive tool usage across a specific toolkit list, explicitly track and invoke each tool category mentioned (code_search, doc_search, chat_search, engineering_context, web_search, web_fetch, pipeline_inspection) at least once per major research direction, and in the final output, declare which tools were invoked vs. unavailable, rather than defaulting to whichever tools are most reliable.
- When a user requests output in a specific structured format (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks), always deliver that exact format as a complete text block in the final turn, even if reasoning required multiple turns to gather sources. Do not assume the structured output will be inferred or appended post-facto.
- When a user requests specific tool categories that are unavailable, explicitly acknowledge the gap in TURN 2-3 before executing, state which tools are available, and confirm scope with the user rather than silently substituting available tools.
- When a user requests specific tools that are not available in the current environment, explicitly acknowledge the constraint in the first response, list what tools ARE available as alternatives, and clarify whether the research strategy should pivot or be deferred.
- When a user requests structured output (FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS blocks) with explicit 'ONLY as...' framing, confirm completion of research phases with intermediate summaries, and stream the final structured block immediately before concluding, rather than deferring output to an ambiguous end state.
- When a user requests structured output (JSON, YAML, tables) with explicit start/end markers, always complete the full structure before finishing, even if it requires multiple response chunks. Validate output against the specified schema before submitting.
- When a user requests structured output (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks, JSON arrays, or formatted sections), complete and return that output block *before* ending the session, even if tool results are partial or incomplete. If tools fail, still populate the structure with what was found and note gaps.
- When a user requests structured output in a specific format (FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS with nested JSON), explicitly confirm receipt of the format specification in the first substantive turn, and validate that all output sections are populated before marking research complete.
- When a user requests structured output with explicit formatting markers (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END), always include the complete formatted block in the final response, even if synthesis is partial; if research is incomplete, note incompleteness within the FINDINGS section rather than omitting the entire structure.
- When a user requests structured output with specific fields (claim, source, corroboration, recency_class), always emit that structure before ending the session, even if data is incomplete; missing structure signals failure to deliver on contract even if raw research was thorough.
- When a user requests tool diversity ('code search, documentation search, chat search, engineering context, web search, web fetch, pipeline inspection'), use at least 3 distinct tool types and explain the cross-tool strategy before executing. Do not pivot to a single-tool approach without acknowledging the reduction in scope.
- When a user requests use of 'all available tools,' immediately attempt a meta-query (e.g., ToolSearch for 'available_tools' or 'tool_inventory') and if that returns empty or fails once, do not retry the same internal tools more than once before returning to confirmed-working approaches. Avoid circular retry patterns on failing tool categories.
- When a user specifies 'ALL available tools' in a research task, explicitly enumerate which tools you have access to and their names before beginning, then log each tool invocation with its tool name and success/failure status so the user can verify coverage.
- When a user specifies 'ALL available tools,' invoke diverse tool types in parallel within the first 3 turns before defaulting to any single tool. Rotate through code search, documentation search, web search, and local context tools in the opening phase.
