- When given an exhaustive research task with ambiguous parameters (time periods, scope boundaries, evidence standards), explicitly surface those ambiguities and propose definitions early, rather than inferring silently. Document assumptions in the structured output's corroboration field to show which claims depend on those assumptions.
- When given an exhaustive research task with an explicit minimum tool-call budget, maintain a visible tally of tool calls per logical direction and explicitly note when transitioning between directions, to ensure thoroughness is both achieved and auditable.
- When given an exhaustive research task with an unfamiliar domain (NADK agents), execute 2-3 tool calls to **validate scope and terminology** before committing to 20+ searches; communicate early findings to confirm the user is satisfied with target precision rather than optimizing tool call count.
- When given an exhaustive research task with explicit structured output requirements, always conclude with the full structured output block before ending the session, even if output is truncated elsewhere.
- When given an exhaustive research task with explicit tool directives and quantified targets, execute a multi-phase strategy: (1) bootstrap context reads, (2) parallel broad searches to identify candidate sources, (3) targeted fetches to deepen highest-signal sources, (4) structure findings with explicit corroboration and recency metadata rather than summary prose alone.
- When given an exhaustive research task with explicit tool lists, parallelize tool invocation across topics within 2-3 turns to minimize discovery overhead; validate tool availability once at session start rather than iteratively per topic.
- When given an exhaustive research task with explicit tool-call count targets and structured output requirements, log a tool-call tally at the halfway point and before final output to confirm coverage targets are met, then include the tally in metadata.
- When given an exhaustive research task with explicit tool-use counts, batch parallel tool invocations (fetch multiple URLs in one turn block) rather than serializing, and escalate tool complexity progressively (web search → fetch → code inspection) to maximize information coherence and minimize redundant accesses.
- When given an exhaustive research task with multiple tool sources and a fixed token budget, explicitly estimate feasibility in turn 1 (e.g., 'This scope requires ~N tool calls and ~M tokens; I will prioritize [pillars] first and adapt if token pressure emerges'). Then track tokens and adjust strategy before running low, not after.
- When given an exhaustive research task with multiple tool types available, prioritize aggressive sequential tool use (search then fetch) over analytical pauses; consolidate synthesis only at the end unless user asks for intermediate checkpoints.
- When given an exhaustive research task with multiple underspecified dimensions (e.g., 'severity classification rules' without clarifying which layer), make one explicit clarifying query to the user or document assumptions before launching repeated tool calls, to avoid redundant exploration.
- When given an exhaustive research task with quantified tool-call minimums ('5-10 tool calls'), establish a stopping rule in advance (e.g., 'stop when three independent sources corroborate each major claim' or 'deploy tools until diminishing returns observed for 3 consecutive searches'). Document the stopping rule in your first reasoning turn to avoid open-ended search loops.
- When given an exhaustive research task with structured output requirements, always output the final STRUCTURED_OUTPUT block immediately before session termination, even if research is incomplete, to ensure the user receives the promised format and can verify it matches the specification.
- When given an exhaustive research task with tool constraints, after reaching declared confidence (turn 18+), implement a final validation loop: for each major claim, explicitly verify minimum 2 independent sources and document corroboration before submitting structured output. Reference the specific file/tool/URL for each claim in internal notes.
- When given an exhaustive research task with tool diversity expectations, deploy tools in escalating specificity waves: start with broad searches, then fetch promising sources, then cross-reference findings with secondary searches; pause only to synthesize direction, not to narrate processing.
- When given an exhaustive research task with tool-call minimums and cross-reference requirements, explicitly read any provided vocabulary bootstrap or seed files before beginning searches to ensure domain terminology is grounded in the user's source material, not inference alone.
- When given an exhaustive research task with vague acronyms or entities, proactively ask for or infer definition/scope in the first two turns before launching 50+ tool calls; document the assumption explicitly in the structured output.
- When given an exhaustive research task, explicitly define and track coverage metrics (e.g., 'X queries completed, Y sources found, Z corroborations achieved') at major checkpoints (every 10 turns) to maximize confidence and avoid redundant searches.
- When given an explicit 'Do NOT' constraint (e.g., 'Do NOT write files'), exclude those tools from your planning phase and tool-call decisions before invoking any tools, rather than invoking and then self-correcting.
- When given an explicit directive to 'use ALL available tools' or 'be exhaustive', do not self-impose role constraints or skip capability classes without explicit user confirmation of scope limits. Execute the stated task first, then flag resource limits if they are hit.
- When given an explicit structured output format (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START...STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_END with named sections), begin tool execution immediately and synthesize findings into that format before the session ends, even if incomplete. Do not spend >3 turns on tool discovery; assume tools exist or fail gracefully.
- When given an explicit structured output format with clear delimiters (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END), emit that structure as the first line of substantive output, before any prose. Validate format closure before ending the turn.
- When given an open-ended 'research exhaustively' prompt with multiple tool types available, deploy tools in parallel batches (e.g., 3-4 web fetches per topic before pivoting) rather than sequentially, and explicitly track which sources have been queried to avoid redundant grep/bash calls on overlapping search spaces.
- When given an open-ended research task with explicit tool-use and output-formatting instructions, explicitly confirm the availability and applicability of each tool class before commencing research, and if a tool class is unavailable, briefly note this in reasoning to close the loop on the user's directive.
- When given exhaustive research directions with tool counts as success criteria, execute tool calls before narrating intent; avoid intermediate commentary turns that state 'I will do X' without actually calling tools. Group corroborating queries tightly (e.g., same topic across 2-3 tool types in sequential turns) rather than spreading them over turns of commentary.
- When given exhaustive research directives with explicit structured output requirements, confirm at the session's end that the full STRUCTURED_OUTPUT block has been returned in your response, and do not declare completion until that format requirement is satisfied.
- When given exhaustive research prompts with structured output requirements, alternate between tool-execution and synthesis turns in a 2:1 or 3:1 ratio; use synthesis turns to evaluate coverage gaps and prioritize the next search direction rather than continuing with low-signal queries.
- When given exhaustive research tasks with explicit tool counts and cross-reference requirements, document and display a running tally of tools invoked and corroborating sources per claim during synthesis to provide transparency on coverage depth and justify stopping points.
- When given exhaustive research tasks with explicit tool requirements, immediately enter a high-velocity discovery phase: batch 3–5 tool calls per turn and explicitly articulate findings + gaps before transitioning to validation phases, rather than interspersing reflection turns.
- When given exhaustive research tasks with explicit tool-usage targets (e.g., 'at least 5-10 tool calls per direction'), organize searches into named phases with explicit transition markers and log the tool-call count per phase to enable transparency and early detection of under/over-research.
- When given exhaustive research tasks with local context files (vocabulary bootstrap, seed topics), proactively load and inspect these artifacts before external tool calls to establish domain terminology and reduce redundant searches.
- When given exhaustive research tasks with multi-tool strategies, explicitly log which tools yielded results vs. empty/access-denied, and flag if any tool category returns zero corroborating evidence before concluding the investigation.
- When given exhaustive research tasks with multiple sub-questions, explicitly confirm scope (e.g., 'I will prioritize the GOVERNANCE direction on data residency; the other 9 audit questions are noted but deferred') before launching tool calls, to avoid ambiguity about what 'completion' means.
- When given exhaustive research tasks with multiple tool types available, batch semantically related queries and execute them in parallel (e.g., all Slack queries together, all web searches together) rather than strict sequential order to maximize velocity while maintaining coherence.
- When given exhaustive research tasks with structured output requirements, explicitly enumerate tool deployment strategy at turn 3 and confirm tool diversity (min. 3 tool types) before proceeding; label each tool call batch with investigation phase (e.g., 'Phase 1: Provider policy discovery', 'Phase 2: Router implementation prior art') to improve transparency and enable early redirection.
- When given exhaustive research tasks with tool-use instructions, launch parallel tool calls for all target entities in the first batch (don't serialize) and explicitly label each research phase (baseline search → deep documentation → architecture deep-dive → corroboration) to show systematic progression and avoid perception of redundant queries.
- When given exhaustive research tasks with unspecified metrics (e.g., 'blast radius', 'repeat offender'), pause after tool discovery to explicitly confirm metric definitions and data boundaries with the user before executing the full search campaign, or document assumptions prominently in the final output.
- When given exhaustive research tasks, declare upfront the stopping criteria (e.g., 'I will conduct searches until all major topics yield redundant results or confirmed absences') and periodically report coverage against that criterion rather than searching until subjectively confident.
- When given exhaustive research tasks, explicitly inventory all available tools before dispatching queries, and use tool enumeration results to design parallel investigation strategies across orthogonal sources rather than sequential single-source queries.
- When given explicit 'aggressive tool use' directives with specific tool counts (5-10+), adopt a wave-based parallel search pattern with synthesis pauses every 4-5 turns, rather than sequential single-tool exploration; cluster similar tool types (WebSearch + WebFetch pairs) to maximize cross-reference potential.
- When given explicit 'be exhaustive' research directives with tool budgets (5-10+ calls per direction), implement a batched search→fetch→synthesis loop and explicitly signal confidence threshold reached before final output, pausing only when multiple independent sources corroborate the same claim.
- When given explicit 'exhaustiveness' mandates in research tasks, execute tool calls in parallel batches (3-4 simultaneous calls) rather than serial chains to reduce latency while maintaining tool output interpretability; document the parallel strategy in reasoning before executing.
- When given explicit 'use ALL available tools' instructions with a specialized environment context (e.g., internal tool runners, company-specific APIs), prioritize specialized/internal tools in the first third of the session, not the last; reserve web search for validation/corroboration.
- When given explicit constraints (e.g., 'Do NOT write any files'), scan the plan before executing any write operations and cite the constraint aloud if the task naturally suggests a write action.
- When given explicit constraints like 'Do NOT write any files', do not attempt file-writing tool calls (TodoWrite, etc.) unless directly requested to do so for scratch work; prefer terminal commands or in-context state management instead.
- When given explicit counts or quantitative targets ('at least 5-10 tool calls per direction'), acknowledge the count in your first response and articulate your exploration plan upfront (e.g., 'I will issue ~60+ calls across 6 research vectors: [list]') before executing, so the user can validate scope before commitment.
- When given explicit instructions to use tools 'aggressively' and to 'make at least N tool calls', deploy a diverse tool portfolio within the first 10 turns—pair local exploration (Bash, Read) with external research (WebSearch, WebFetch) in parallel, rather than sequentially, to maximize cross-referencing and reduce session length.
- When given explicit maximalist directives (use ALL tools, be exhaustive, make 5-10+ calls per direction), interpret them as permission to run parallel tool batches without seeking approval, and maintain steady tool execution velocity across the entire session to demonstrate comprehensiveness.
- When given explicit multi-tool directives, attempt each named tool at least once before defaulting to a single tool strategy. If a tool fails or is unavailable, report the failure and justify the fallback approach rather than silently switching strategies.
- When given explicit output constraints (e.g., 'Do NOT write any files') or structured format requirements (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks), verify compliance before invoking file-writing or output-generation tools; if any invocation violates constraints, pause and clarify with the user rather than proceeding.
- When given explicit output format requirements (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/FLAWS|[...]/END) in a prompt, always deliver that exact format in full, even if analysis is incomplete; mark incomplete analysis with status flags rather than truncating.
- When given explicit prohibitions (e.g., 'do not write files'), implement them as hard constraints: before calling any file-writing or persistence tool, check the prompt for restrictions and raise an error or skip the tool rather than proceeding with a violation.
- When given explicit structured output format requirements (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks), always display the final output block in full before marking the research complete, even if output is lengthy. Verify the format is syntactically valid (JSON arrays parse, markdown renders, etc.) before concluding.
- When given explicit structured output requirements (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks), prioritize completing and returning the full block before session end, even if research is incomplete; truncated structured outputs are worse than partial findings in valid JSON format.
- When given explicit tool count targets ('at least 5-10 tool calls'), execute tools on odd turns and reserve even turns for synthesis/meta-commentary to maintain 1:1 ratio of execution-to-planning; avoid consecutive planning turns.
- When given explicit tool-call minima and multi-phase research prompts, pre-plan research phases (discovery → targeted → validation) and sequence tool modalities to escalate from broad context to code-specific to primary-source fetches; report phase transitions in planning turns to avoid mid-research ambiguity.
- When given explicit tool-call minimums and 'exhaustive' directives, show each tool invocation as a separate, timestamped action with visible inputs/outputs (even if brief), and summarize tool-call count after each section to prove alignment with minimum thresholds before proceeding to synthesis.
- When given explicit tool-usage quotas in research tasks, log at turn midpoints (every 10-15 turns) the count of each tool type deployed and remaining sources to evaluate, enabling course-correction if quota targets will be missed.
- When given explicit tool-use instructions with quantified targets (5-10 calls, ALL available tools), do not self-redirect or reinterpret the scope—proceed immediately to tool discovery and parallel execution without intermediate bootstrap steps, unless clarification is requested.
- When given explicit tool-use mandates with minimum thresholds (e.g., '5-10 tool calls per direction'), plan and annotate tool execution upfront: list the specific repos, search strategies, and APIs to be queried before beginning, then execute the list methodically to ensure coverage and auditability.
- When given explicit tooling instructions (e.g., 'use ALL available tools aggressively'), instrument each major phase with internal synthesis checkpoints before pivoting; log the tool call count per phase to ensure alignment with stated thresholds and to provide the user with transparency on whether 'exhaustive' criteria are being met.
- When given file paths or directory references in a research synthesis task, explicitly request and read any contextual 'seed' or 'topic' files BEFORE processing findings files. If the topic is missing or unreadable, ask the user to provide it inline, even if it delays synthesis by one turn.
- When given inline specification text and asked to perform analysis with explicit output format requirements, begin analysis immediately on turn 1 rather than reading external files; ask clarifying questions only if the inline content is genuinely ambiguous or incomplete.
- When given internal Netflix document URLs (identified by netflix.net, google.netflix.net, or common internal doc patterns), prioritize netflix_search_data over WebFetch; only use WebFetch for public/external URLs. Validate tool choice before executing calls on auth-gated resources.
- When given multi-wave research tasks with explicit tool budgets ('5-10 tool calls per direction'), explicitly count and report tool calls completed per wave before moving to the next phase, and verify budget compliance before delivering final output.
- When given open-ended research mandates with 'aggressive' or 'exhaustive' instructions, define explicit stopping criteria upfront (e.g., 'until 3 consecutive searches yield <10% new claims' or 'minimum 2 sources per claim') to avoid circular verification and diminishing-return iterations.
- When given research tasks requesting structured output, always display the final structured output block in full before the session ends, and explicitly confirm that all required fields (FINDINGS, SOURCES, CLAIMS) are populated with data from the tool calls executed.
- When handling multi-phase research requests, explicitly confirm the final output format (file vs. text response, structured markup, etc.) with the user before beginning, and note the confirmation in your first planning turn to prevent misaligned delivery.
- When handling research tasks with 403/access-restricted resources, document which domains failed in the final output and note retrieval success rate per source category (e.g., '8/10 web fetches succeeded, 1/2 internal API queries successful'); this provides users transparency on evidence gaps.
- When inferring temporal signals (departure dates, retirement timelines), always explicitly validate the recency of the source material (e.g., 'This doc is dated 2024-03' or 'Sourcegraph shows last commit 2y ago') before anchoring claims to specific years. Flag stale documentation with a recency class and avoid projecting source dates onto current time.
- When ingesting >50 items from a filesystem, default to bulk extraction via grep/awk/bash piping rather than sequential Read operations; check file count in the index and select the appropriate strategy before beginning.
- When inheriting multi-phase research tasks with aggressive tool-use directives, do not self-impose constraints based on inferred protocols (e.g., 'SUBAGENT-STOP'). Acknowledge constraints explicitly stated in the prompt or ask for clarification, but treat user directives to use 'ALL available tools' as literal unless overridden.
- When inheriting research directives from prior reports (e.g., 'Build on MDM findings — don't re-research'), explicitly list what NOT to investigate before starting tool calls, to avoid redundant searches and clarify scope boundaries early.
- When initial research reveals that the user's premise or framing is factually incorrect, surface this finding immediately (after confirming via 2+ independent sources) rather than continuing to gather evidence in the original frame. Clearly state the contradiction and offer to pivot the research direction.
- When initial search attempts (filesystem, code, docs) yield sparse results across 5+ iterations, explicitly pivot to secondary evidence sources (commit history, architecture documentation, incident reports, team communication records) or declare the target likely undocumented rather than repeating the same tool/query pattern.
- When initial tool calls fail due to auth/access, immediately retry with authenticated variants (e.g., WebFetch → mcp__jira__getConfluencePage) without waiting for user guidance, and log the pivot in your reasoning.
- When initial tool invocations (Read, file access) produce no output, explicitly report the failure, list the attempted paths, and pivot to alternative tools within the same turn rather than silently abandoning the tool chain.
- When initial tool strategy yields zero results after 3+ iterations, explicitly evaluate alternative tool categories before continuing with the same approach; document the pivot decision and rationale in a turn comment.
- When initiating exhaustive research, open with a rapid parallel batch of tool calls (4-6 simultaneous queries) rather than sequential execution, then document findings in temporary files for later synthesis rather than keeping all state in context.
- When initiating parallel tool calls that share parameters (e.g., namespace, query), pre-validate parameter schemas against tool signatures before the first batch, and include parameter validation logic in the prompt-handling layer to prevent repeated malformed calls.
- When instructed 'Do NOT write any files,' do not invoke write/create tools even for internal working notes; use only read tools and in-context reasoning during the investigation phase. Reserve write operations exclusively for final deliverables or when explicitly requested.
- When instructed 'Do NOT write any files,' treat internal checkpoint tools (TodoWrite, note-taking) as violations unless explicitly whitelisted; instead, organize findings in intermediate reasoning blocks within the response thread.
- When instructed 'Do NOT write any files,' treat this as a hard constraint and audit all tool calls before execution—never invoke file-writing tools (Write, TodoWrite, etc.) unless explicitly asked to save a named artifact.
- When instructed to 'use ALL available tools aggressively,' proactively list which tools will be attempted and in what sequence before executing, then report blockers (WebFetch, auth failures) immediately with a fallback plan rather than silently substituting alternative tools.
- When instructed to 'use all available tools aggressively,' always apply a relevance filter: verify that each tool's domain (product, codebase, knowledge base) actually bears on the research question before invoking it. Log the rationale briefly in your reasoning turn.
- When instructed to 'use all available tools to discover gaps', explicitly log which tools were invoked (web search, file read, knowledge base query), what queries were issued, and how many results/insights were returned before generating output. Include a brief summary of tool findings in the response preamble.
- When instructed to 'use tools aggressively,' explicitly track and report total tool call count and tool diversity at conclusion; summarize which tool types (code search, web fetch, etc.) contributed most evidence, enabling future users to validate thoroughness.
- When instructed to be 'exhaustive' or 'aggressive,' begin tool execution immediately without meta-announcement turns; declare intent through action, not commentary. Only pause for tool-loading if tools require explicit initialization that cannot occur mid-query.
- When instructed to conduct 'exhaustive' research with explicit tool counts, batch tool calls in coordinated waves (3-5 parallel calls of the same type) rather than serial execution, then use the results to inform the next wave. This maximizes coverage while respecting rate limits and providing strategic decision points.
- When instructed to conduct research with multiple tool types, explicitly report tool execution status (success/failure/unavailable) for each tool call, and synthesize intermediate findings after every 3-4 tool calls to avoid orphaned research efforts that produce no final output.
- When instructed to deliver structured output in a specific format, monitor token consumption against remaining budget in real time; if within 85% of typical limit, begin final output immediately and split large findings into multiple response blocks rather than risk truncation.
- When instructed to execute a high-volume tool call strategy, immediately enumerate and execute tools in the specified categories before responding. If any tool is unavailable, explicitly state which tools failed and offer to proceed with available alternatives; never silently omit tool execution without explanation.
- When instructed to fetch external URLs in research tasks, perform a quick availability check (HEAD request or DNS query) on 2–3 sample URLs before committing to bulk fetching. If >30% fail, declare the source class inaccessible and pivot to alternate tools (code search, internal docs, or explicit uncertainty) within 2 turns.
- When instructed to find quantitative metrics (query rates, hit rates, usage splits), prioritize searching for audit notebooks, monitoring dashboards, and data pipeline outputs before querying endpoints directly; endpoints often surface qualitative architecture over quantitative telemetry.
- When instructed to produce structured output with arrays of claims/sources, confirm completion by echoing a summary count (e.g., 'Compiled 47 claims from 23 sources') before truncating, so the user can verify delivery without re-reading the full block.
- When instructed to produce structured output with specified sections (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END with FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS), verify all sections are complete and well-formed before signaling synthesis is done. If synthesis is interrupted mid-section, explicitly state 'Section X incomplete, continuing' rather than signaling completion.
- When instructed to produce structured output, always emit the complete STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END block in the final turn, even if synthesis is incomplete; use 'preliminary' or 'in-progress' markers rather than delaying output until perfection.
- When instructed to read a file before responding, always echo or summarize key contents from that file in your response to confirm successful access and inform your analysis.
- When instructed to research exhaustively with multiple tools, execute tool calls immediately within the same turn rather than announcing intent in a separate turn, then tooling in the next turn. Batch intent declaration with at least one actual tool invocation per turn.
- When instructed to search exhaustively across internal/proprietary systems, explicitly test tool availability within the first 2-3 turns and state constraints immediately rather than continuing exploratory attempts; pivot to public/accessible alternatives within a single turn of recognizing a constraint.
- When instructed to search for internal organization/people data, check local file-system availability with 'find' or 'ls' before attempting RAG/API queries, to avoid redundant roundtrips if documents must be parsed locally.
- When instructed to synthesize research across multiple sources, maintain a local index of all fetched documents in the context window. Before executing a new search query, check the index to avoid redundant fetches. Prioritize consolidation over incremental discovery once initial sources are saturated.
- When instructed to use 'ALL available tools aggressively' with a minimum call count, log and display the tool-call sequence at session end with call count and source coverage (e.g., '12 calls across NECP, RAG-manuals, RAG-Slack, Sourcegraph, web') to prove the directive was met and allow user verification without inspecting raw transcript.
- When instructed to use 'ALL available tools' for research, explicitly inventory which tool categories are available (web, internal RAG, Slack, engineering context, code search, etc.) in the first 2 turns, and note which ones return substantive data vs. no-ops, to avoid wasting turns on unproductive tool calls.
- When instructed to use 'all available tools,' explicitly test each tool early (turns 1-5) with a simple query; if a tool returns empty or errors, mark it as unavailable and do not retry unless user explicitly requests. This prevents sunk cost fallacy in tool exploration.
- When instructed to use 'all available tools,' first enumerate which tools are actually available in the current environment before attempting invocations; only call tools that are explicitly confirmed to exist, to avoid silent failures or attempt-then-fail cycles.
- When instructed to use 'every available tool,' first validate tool availability and ROI (turns 1–5) before committing to sustained queries; abandon tools returning empty results after 2 unsuccessful calls and pivot to high-signal sources rather than persisting with exploration.
- When instructed to use ALL available tools, inventory and validate correct tool names and parameter formats before executing parallel queries. If a tool invocation fails, do not retry the same malformed call more than once; instead, document the failure, note it in reasoning, and pivot to confirmed-working tool alternatives rather than delaying subsequent research.
- When instructed to use a specific set of tools (especially with 'ALL' or 'aggressively'), explicitly attempt each tool class before defaulting to a subset. If a tool is unavailable or inaccessible, state this early and negotiate scope rather than silently omitting it from the plan.
- When instructed to use multiple tool categories (web, code, internal context), maintain a tool diversity histogram and warn if any category is unused after 50% of turns are complete.
- When instructed to use multiple tools aggressively with an explicit minimum (e.g., '5-10 tool calls per direction'), explicitly count and categorize queries by direction at a midpoint checkpoint (e.g., turn 50 of 90) rather than assuming compliance; report shortfalls and course-correct immediately.
- When instructed to use tools 'aggressively' with a specific minimum (e.g., '5-10 per direction'), execute the minimum in the first turn after planning, not as a delayed announcement; avoid 'I will now search' turns without immediate tool invocations.
- When instructed to use tools 'aggressively' with explicit call-count targets, maintain a visible turn-by-turn tool tally and verify you meet the minimum before synthesis phases; pause turns should explicitly announce the intent (e.g., 'Synthesis checkpoint: 6 tools used so far on this direction') to reinforce deliberate methodology rather than appearing silent.
- When instructed to use tools 'aggressively' with high volume targets ('5–10 tool calls per direction'), checkpoint after each batch (every 3–5 calls) to confirm novel evidence before continuing; avoid sequential repetition of the same query across tools without explicit differentiation.
- When instructed to use tools 'aggressively' with minimum thresholds, explicitly log tool invocation counts per category and pause before final output to verify minimums were met; if not, conduct additional targeted calls to close gaps.
- When instructed to use tools 'aggressively' with minimum tool-call thresholds, estimate tool budget upfront and allocate turns to meet minimums; explicitly signal when minimums are met to avoid over-searching and token waste.
- When instructed to use tools 'aggressively' with specific minimums (5–10 calls), explicitly log each tool invocation attempt (success or failure) and note if unavailable tools are skipped, to provide transparency on tool-use coverage against the directive.
- When instructed to use tools 'aggressively,' interpret as targeted depth-first with rapid fallback to alternate tools if initial approach yields unwieldy results, not blind breadth-first expansion. Prioritize tool diversity (e.g., NECP, RAG, Slack) over repetition of a single tool.
- When internal API calls fail with transient errors, inspect the error message for missing parameters or schema issues, fix them once, and apply the fix across all similar tools in parallel rather than retrying each tool independently; if errors persist after one retry cycle, pivot to an alternative information source (local files, web search) rather than entering a retry loop.
- When internal API searches (Slack, internal docs) fail silently or return no results, explicitly pivot strategy: acknowledge the failure, document why (auth, namespace, timing), and either retry with alternative namespaces or commit fully to public sources rather than continuing scattered retry attempts.
- When internal authentication fails (Jira/Confluence), explicitly state the failure mode, remaining tool availability, and the corroboration impact in the next synthesis (e.g., 'Jira unavailable; claims from web sources only are marked single_source').
- When internal or proprietary tools time out or return empty results after 2–3 attempts, immediately escalate to public authoritative sources (academic databases, official company publications, web search) rather than retrying the same tool category.
- When internal sources (Slack, engineering context) become available and return results, deprioritize non-local web fetches that require external authentication; cache and reuse internal RAG output rather than retry 403'd URLs.
- When internal system queries (RAG, API calls) error, explicitly log the error, state what it means for the research, and declare a strategy shift (e.g., 'falling back to web search' or 'expanding query scope') before proceeding.
- When internal tool calls fail or return unclear status codes, immediately log the error, pivot to an alternative source (web search, fetch, grep), and explicitly note the pivot in reasoning rather than retrying the same tool with minor syntax variants.
- When internal tools (CodeSearch, EngineeringContext, PipelineInspection) are unavailable, explicitly flag this constraint to the user before pivoting to external tools (WebSearch/WebFetch), and confirm the strategy change is acceptable given the research goal.
- When internal tools (Jira, MCP APIs) fail during research, log the failure once and commit to a fallback strategy (e.g., web-only research) rather than retry-looping; if user later asks why internal sources weren't used, explain the unavailability upfront in the structured output.
- When internal tools (MCP, RAG, etc.) fail after 1–2 attempts with no error context, immediately switch to public web-based alternatives rather than retrying with guessed parameter permutations. Explicitly note in reasoning why the tool was abandoned.
- When internal tools (ToolSearch, Bash, code/repo queries) return no results after 2-3 attempts, explicitly diagnose why (permissions? tool misconfiguration? wrong scope?) before reverting to external search. Document the failure mode and state new strategy clearly, rather than silently resuming previous approach.
- When internal tools (code repos, engineering context) are unavailable, explicitly signal the pivot to external sources in a summary turn rather than silently shifting; help the user understand coverage gaps caused by access constraints before proceeding with alternatives.
- When internal tools (code search, documentation search, engineering context) return empty results in the first 2-3 attempts, explicitly acknowledge the limitation and pivot to public web research rather than continuing to attempt inaccessible tools.
- When internal tools (code search, internal docs, chat search) return minimal results, explicitly confirm with the user that scope is shifting to external web research before proceeding, rather than silently pivoting strategy and focus area.
- When internal tools (codebase search, context retrieval, pipeline inspection) are requested but unavailable, explicitly report this constraint in the first response rather than attempting silently for multiple turns; flag the impact on evidence quality and specificity upfront.
- When internal tools are unavailable or tool strategy must pivot, explicitly communicate the constraint to the user before proceeding with alternative approach; never silently abandon requested tool categories without explanation.
- When internal-only tools are unavailable, explicitly state the public-vs-internal research boundary at the pivot point and estimate coverage gaps; do not continue attempting similar failures. Explicitly note in final output which claims are from internal vs. public sources.
- When internal/MCP tools fail silently or return no results, immediately log the tool name, parameters, and error response (or lack thereof) to stderr, and attempt one clarifying retry with verbose output before pivoting to alternative sources. If the retry also fails, report the failure explicitly to the user with timestamps and parameters rather than silently moving to Plan B.
- When internal/local tools return null or minimal output, explicitly acknowledge the failure and state the pivot strategy rather than silently switching approaches. This prevents implicit misalignments and maintains transparency about tool availability.
- When investigating internal organizational sources (code repos, Slack, internal docs) for external regulatory compliance work, explicitly cross-check abbreviations and team names early. If a promising lead (e.g., 'DSA') appears, verify its meaning in the organizational context before investing multiple turns of investigation.
- When invoking external tools (APIs, bash commands, knowledge bases), always log the tool name, input, and output verbatim in your response. If a tool returns no results or errors, immediately pivot to a fallback strategy (e.g., using seed data, admitting uncertainty) rather than claiming context sufficiency without evidence.
- When invoking internal MCP tools (RAG, slack search, manuals) for the first time, always check tool schema for required parameters (namespace, context, scope) before dispatching; if unsure, do a test call with minimal parameters and surface the error proactively rather than in a subsequent turn.
- When invoking internal workflow state machines or skipping procedural steps, explicitly name and justify the decision to the user before proceeding, e.g., 'I'm skipping X workflow and proceeding directly to investigation because [reason]. Confirm this approach works for you.' rather than asserting it as a given.
- When invoking specialized internal RAG or MCP tools (core-tools, internal knowledge bases), validate tool signatures and namespacing before execution; if validation fails, fall back to alternative sources without delay rather than attempting retry sequences.
- When invoking specialized internal tools (MCP endpoints like rag-manuals, rag-slack, NECP), always check for required namespace/context parameters in the initial tool call, not after failure. If parameter schema is unclear, emit a clarification query before the first invocation rather than iterating on failures.
- When invoking unfamiliar MCP tools for the first time, always request or inspect the tool schema/signature before making calls; log parameter names and types, then validate against the schema before executing queries.
- When large file results are returned from searches (e.g., Slack, manuals), immediately inspect file size and use streaming/grep/head rather than attempting full Read operations; prioritize extracting key sections inline.
- When launching exhaustive research on a specific quantitative claim (e.g., 'what fraction of 200k tokens'), explicitly checkpoint after 20 tool calls to assess whether published data exists; if not, explicitly tell the user 'this specific threshold does not appear in public sources; here are the closest estimates' rather than continuing unfiltered searches.
- When launching multiple parallel agents to satisfy an 'aggressive tool usage' requirement, explicitly confirm in the next turn that each agent completed successfully and aggregate their findings before final synthesis; avoid truncating structured output.
- When launching parallel tool calls or agent orchestration, emit a brief plan before execution ('I will spawn N parallel searches targeting [source1], [source2], [source3] to cover [dimension1] and [dimension2]'), and after completion, emit a summary of what each returned before synthesis.
- When local tools fail silently, explicitly announce the fallback strategy and request user confirmation before pivoting to a fundamentally different research method (e.g., 'Local repos not found; switching to web search—is this acceptable?').
- When making multiple API calls to the same endpoint (e.g., mcp__NECP__get-netflix-engineering-context), explicitly log cache hits, null results, or access failures after the first 2 calls; if 3+ consecutive calls return no new data, declare the endpoint exhausted and pivot to alternative sources rather than continuing to query redundantly.
- When multiple tool/service discovery attempts fail sequentially with connection or availability errors, pause after 2-3 failures and assess for systemic outage rather than retrying the same endpoints 5+ times.
- When operating as a primary agent in research mode with explicit tool inventory, do not self-designate as a subagent or invoke SUBAGENT-STOP without explicit user instruction; instead, confirm which tool ecosystem is in scope and begin work immediately.
- When operating as a research subagent, never reference system directives, skill names, or constraints not explicitly stated in the user prompt. If internal workflows or skip-logic apply, confirm them only after the task is complete, not during tool selection.
- When operating as a subagent or in a specialized research mode, never invoke constraints or rules that are not explicitly defined by the user in the current prompt; if applying standards from implicit organizational conventions, state them transparently and ask for confirmation before reducing tool usage or parallelization.
- When operating as a subagent, do not assume or invoke internal directives (e.g., 'SUBAGENT-STOP') that are not explicitly stated in the user's prompt; instead, use all available tools as instructed unless the user or calling context provides explicit constraints.
- When output format is explicitly constrained (e.g., 'in text only, no files'), implement token-budget partitioning *before* final synthesis: summarize long sources in-line with [truncated] markers, or warn the user explicitly that synthesis will exceed context rather than silently writing files.
- When outputting large structured blocks (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END), estimate token cost upfront and reserve 15% buffer; if approaching token limit mid-block, emit incomplete marker and request continuation rather than truncating mid-field.
- When outputting large structured data (JSON, tables, code blocks), always validate completeness before returning: verify all opening tags have closing tags, all array elements are fully formed, and no placeholder values (ellipsis, `...`, `<placeholder>`) remain in the final output.
- When outputting structured blocks (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START...END), always ensure the entire block completes in a single turn without truncation. If content is too long, split across multiple turns but preserve the opening marker in the first turn and closing marker in the final turn, and explicitly signal the multi-turn delivery.
- When outputting structured data (JSON, XML, tables), always validate the full structure is present and properly closed before sending. For large outputs, count array/object completeness: if producing N items, verify all N are present before transmission. If output will be truncated, emit a completion notice or request a follow-up turn explicitly.
- When outputting structured data in multiple formats (JSON + delimiters), validate that both formats are syntactically complete and matching before returning; ensure no quotes are left unclosed or fields are duplicated across format variants.
- When parallelizing reads of N items with subagents, start with batch_size = floor(sqrt(N)) and double only after confirming memory headroom; log token/time metrics for each batch to detect thrashing before it cascades.
- When parsing CSV data with complex quoted fields containing special characters, default to Python csv module or a single-pass parser instead of shell utilities; test the parser on a sample row before bulk processing.
- When performing design critique on large specs, proactively estimate token requirements and warn the user if the task may exceed context limits; offer to break it into staged critiques (e.g., 'architecture flaws first, then data model, then APIs') rather than attempting a single comprehensive response that may truncate.
- When performing exhaustive research with RAG/search tools, track results from each query and explicitly skip similar follow-ups if prior results were thin; annotate when shifting search strategy (e.g., 'RAG produced N results, pivoting to code search') rather than silently repeating similar queries.
- When performing exhaustive research with multiple tool types, alternate tool categories (WebSearch → WebFetch → RAG → WebSearch) rather than exhausting one tool type sequentially, to build evidence density faster and reduce false negatives from single-source bias.
- When performing exhaustive research with structured output requirements, explicitly list expected claims/sources in the planning phase (TURN 1-3), then verify coverage before compilation to ensure no corroboration gaps.
- When performing large batch file reads (10+ files), announce strategy upfront, execute in semantic groupings rather than arbitrary sequential chunks, and provide progress updates every 10-15 operations to maintain user visibility and allow mid-course corrections.
- When performing large structured output analyses (>5 KB), explicitly notify the user of output length and offer to break analysis into sections rather than risking truncation mid-delivery.
- When performing large-scale document analysis tasks (5k+ lines), explicitly state reading progress and check point understanding before generating output. If the response is expected to be very long (>2k tokens), outline the structure and section headings before diving into detail, to maximize the chance of completing the delivery.
- When performing lengthy structured analysis of complex specs, estimate token count for full output before starting; if near budget, either compress sections or explicitly flag to user that full analysis will be continued in next turn.
- When performing multi-phase research requiring sequential tool calls, batch and parallelize tool invocations within each thematic block rather than interspersing pauses; use internal reasoning between blocks to minimize turn overhead.
- When performing research across internal systems with potential transient failures, execute all queries in parallel batches and continue synthesis once a quorum (e.g., 70%+) of parallel calls succeeds; report which calls failed but do not block output.
- When performing research tasks with 'exhaustive' or 'aggressive' tool usage requirements, explicitly announce strategy shifts and corroboration milestones mid-session (e.g., 'Pivoting to X source now', 'Have corroborated Y claims') to create checkpoints and allow early correction if scope is misaligned.
- When performing structured output analysis, emit STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START immediately upon beginning analysis; do not announce findings in a separate turn before producing the structured block. If analysis is in progress, continue reading and emit the complete output in a single turn.
- When pivoting between tool categories (public web → internal tools), explicitly acknowledge the pivot and explain why internal sources are more likely to yield answers. Confirm user has access before investing 10+ turns in internal RAG/Slack searches for compliance documentation.
- When pivoting from a failed tool (e.g., WebFetch with TLS errors), explicitly ask the user for confirmation of access constraints (internal repos, authentication, VPN) before adopting a fallback strategy; do not silently assume the new tool will succeed.
- When pivoting search strategies due to tool blocking, track attempted tool classes explicitly and skip redundant retry patterns; prioritize code-level exploration and direct file access over repeated web searches with minor query variations.
- When pivoting to an unfamiliar internal tool (e.g., MCP-based resources), validate all required parameters and confirm availability before invocation; if parameters are unknown, query tool documentation or skip to verified fallback sources rather than failing with an incomplete call.
- When planning multi-stage research with RAG/Sourcegraph, sketch the query taxonomy before executing tools: deduplicate searches, sequence them to avoid re-querying the same corpus, and reserve parallel execution for fundamentally different sources (e.g., Slack + Sourcegraph simultaneously, not RAG + RAG with slight variations).
- When presented with a broad research mandate and a narrowed 'priority direction' within it, explicitly confirm scope intent with the user before committing >5 turns, rather than inferring scope from context clues alone.
- When presented with multiple research directions of different priorities, explicitly ask the user to rank them or confirm focus scope before investing 9+ turns of execution; otherwise, make the scope reduction decision transparent and document it in the final output's FINDINGS section.
- When primary data sources fail (WebFetch 4xx/5xx/timeout), explicitly attempt at least one alternative retrieval method (search for cached versions, look for published extracts/summaries, or note the gap in findings with severity) before pivoting to secondary sources.
- When producing large structured outputs with explicit format requirements (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END, specific sections like coverage reports), always reserve output tokens by testing the full template length before generation; if near limit, request user approval to either truncate gracefully with a footer note or split across multiple turns.
- When producing large structured outputs with multiple sections (FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS), write each section incrementally and explicitly confirm completion of each block before moving to the next, rather than attempting to generate the entire output in a single forward pass. If context pressure is detected (e.g., repeated timeouts or cut-offs), split the output into two responses: one for framework summaries, one for claims and sources.
- When producing structured JSON output with arrays of objects (e.g., multiple flaws and gap reports), explicitly count and verify array length before closing the schema. If approaching token limits, truncate non-critical entries and close the JSON properly rather than leaving it malformed mid-element.
- When promising parallel or simultaneous execution, either deliver it (via concurrent tool invocation) or explicitly reframe as sequential investigation with clear phase markers (e.g., 'Phase 1: Bootstrap', 'Phase 2: Parallel NECP queries', 'Phase 3: Synthesis') to avoid framing misalignment.
- When prompted for exhaustive research across multiple tools, prioritize substantive searches (code/documentation queries) over tool discovery; delay generic ToolSearch calls until a specific gap in coverage is identified, to reduce wasted turns.
- When prompted to generate structured output with explicit closing markers (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START...STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_END), verify the complete output is emitted before signaling completion. If approaching token limits, summarize incompleteness and offer to continue in next turn rather than cut off mid-structure.
- When pursuing a single evidence thread, explicitly log the signal's strength (weak=email list/naming convention, medium=single Slack mention, strong=multiple independent sources) and immediately seek independent corroboration before committing >5 turns of investigation to that thread. Flag hypothesis shifts in reasoning.
- When pursuing external web sources (LinkedIn, GitHub public, etc.) for employees of closed companies, limit retries to 1-2 attempts per URL; after first 404/auth failure, pivot immediately to internal-only sources rather than cycling the same fetch 3+ times.
- When querying for person/role metadata from internal systems, set a hard limit of 3-5 failed lookups per person before escalating that unknown as 'unresolvable from available sources' and consolidating the output. Do not retry similar failed queries more than once per person.
- When reading external files for structured analysis, validate file readability and log any read failures explicitly before retrying, and always return complete, properly-closed JSON output regardless of source complexity.
- When reading external files to inform structured output, always echo back the key content from the file in your response to allow the user to verify that you read and interpreted the correct source material.
- When reading from a prior run or external state, validate that the input state matches the user's implicit assumptions (e.g., non-empty findings, successful prior execution). If a mismatch is detected, flag it explicitly before proceeding to synthesis or recovery.
- When reading large specs for critique, scan for race conditions in concurrent workflows first (any multi-actor write to shared state), then for missing error budgets in external API calls, then for semantic mismatches between stated goals and implementation. Report partial results if hitting length limits rather than truncating mid-output.
- When reading long inline specifications in a prompt, search the prompt text for '--- SPEC START ---' or similar delimiters before invoking file-read tools; if found, parse the inline spec directly rather than fetching external files with the same content.
- When receiving a research or investigation task with explicit tool-call quotas (e.g., 'at least 5-10 tool calls'), begin immediately by enumerating available tools, confirming feasibility, and executing the first batch of tool calls before drafting any prose response. If tools are unavailable, state this explicitly rather than proceeding without attempting them.
- When receiving a structured task that depends on external file content, always verify that referenced files are readable and contain expected data before proceeding; if data is missing or malformed, request clarification rather than proceeding with empty context.
- When repeated tool calls return consistent null or sparse results (3+ iterations), pause and explicitly assess whether the tool is appropriate for the goal; pivot to alternative tools, request access clarification, or inform the user of data access limitations before continuing identical queries.
- When requested to deliver structured output with multiple sections (FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS), verify that all sections are complete and properly formatted before considering the research phase finished. Use final turn to emit a completion confirmation with section counts.
- When requested to search 'all available tools aggressively,' attempt web/external sources first and only use internal RAG tools (Slack, manuals) if web searches yield insufficient corroboration, to avoid wasting turns on speculative internal queries that may have low hit rates.
- When required to return a structured output block, generate and return it after each major research phase, and before consuming additional turns; do not exceed turn budget without delivering the contractual output format.
- When research completeness is required by a structured format, explicitly track progress through each required output section (FINDINGS, SOURCES, CLAIMS) during the investigation and consolidate early, rather than consolidating only at the end where timeout risk increases.
- When research concludes that a metric or artifact does not exist, explicitly require corroboration across at least 2-3 independent search tools or authoritative sources before finalizing the 'not tracked' claim, and always report the specific absence pattern (e.g., 'zero results in search_api, zero results in search_data, no Slack discussion') rather than just declaring non-existence.
- When research direction includes explicit tool quotas (e.g., '5-10 calls per direction'), organize tool calls into labeled phases and track count-per-phase in reasoning to show quota compliance and enable user audit of tool coverage.
- When research direction is open-ended (e.g., 'exhaustive' without success criteria), explicitly ask for or infer the stopping rule in the first few turns and communicate it back to the user before deep recursion (e.g., 'I will gather data until I have lead-time signals from 6+ pillars and 15+ independent sources')
- When research findings contradict the user's initial premise or framing, pause at a natural checkpoint (e.g., after discovering an inactive account or zero recent activity) and explicitly surface the contradiction to the user rather than continuing mechanically. Ask: 'Should I pivot the investigation given this finding?' This prevents wasted effort on a false premise.
- When research involves future-dated conference papers (e.g., ICLR 2025), explicitly note in the structured output if the paper is not yet publicly available or is still in preprint, to avoid false authority in synthesized findings.
- When research or analysis prompts contain underspecified metrics (e.g., 'most-used'), ask for clarification before executing tool calls: 'I'll search for memory inspection tools. To measure "most-used," should I prioritize GitHub stars, download counts, community mentions, or documentation activity?'
- When research or investigation tasks require structured final output, explicitly confirm completion and emit the full output block before ending the session, even if intermediate findings are still being gathered.
- When research output is requested in STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END format, always execute that format block in a dedicated final turn before acknowledging system notifications, and explicitly confirm the output format completion to the user.
- When research premise contains unverified entity relationships (e.g., 'person X's joint role at organizations Y and Z'), validate the assumption within the first 3-5 tool calls and report any contradictions explicitly before proceeding with exhaustive verification.
- When research prompts ask for specific metrics (e.g., 'pulls per week,' 'bypass rate'), explicitly acknowledge early if those metrics appear inaccessible in searchable sources, rather than spending iterations on extraction. Flag likely internal-only data by Turn 10-15 so user can provide direct access or reframe scope.
- When research prompts explicitly list multiple tool categories, log tool coverage at checkpoints (e.g., every 10-15 turns) to ensure systematic use of each tool type and avoid over-relying on accessible tools while neglecting harder-to-access internal tools.
- When research prompts request 'ALL available tools' or list specific tool classes, prioritize diversifying tool use across at least 4 distinct tool types within the first 20 turns. If a tool class (e.g., code search, documentation search) returns no results within 2–3 attempts, note the null result explicitly before deprioritizing that tool.
- When research prompts request exhaustive cross-referenced findings, make explicit tool deployment logs (e.g., 'Invoking NECP [query], RAG-Slack [query], Sourcegraph [query] in parallel') and emit a running tally of source credibility / corroboration counts every 10–15 turns to prevent blind synthesis and catch coverage gaps early.
- When research reveals that a user's premise is factually incorrect, explicitly pause and surface the contradiction with evidence in a dedicated clarification turn (e.g., 'I found that X is actually Y, not Z as specified. Should I continue investigating X's true nature, or would you like to revise the research direction?') before proceeding with exhaustive verification.
- When research reveals that the underlying premise may be false (e.g., a feature doesn't exist or is pre-release), pause and explicitly surface this contradiction to the user before finalizing output, rather than composing the structured response while noting the contradiction in passing.
- When research targets are internal org structures, prioritize discovery of authoritative org charts and team rosters (e.g., engineering manuals, Workday exports, HRIS snapshots) before relying on scattered Slack/email evidence; note gaps in primary source availability explicitly in final output.
- When research tasks explicitly define required output format with templates, synthesize and emit that format inline before concluding the session; validate that all mandatory fields (FINDINGS, SOURCES, CLAIMS) are present and populated before marking research complete.
- When research tasks request structured output with claims, sources, and corroboration tracking, explicitly log each tool result retrieved and map claims to sources in real time during the research phase, not just at the final synthesis step, to enable auditable traceability.
- When research yield plateaus (no new distinct leads after 5+ consecutive identical tool queries), stop searching and compile final output immediately. Recognize that exhaustiveness means thorough coverage of high-signal sources, not repetitive low-signal queries.
- When researching Netflix internal systems, prioritize mcp__NECP and rag-manuals tools in parallel rather than sequential; batch similar queries to avoid redundant calls. If a tool call fails, immediately try an alternative source rather than retrying the same tool.
- When researching a complex enterprise system, apply a three-phase tool strategy: (1) broad web searches to identify authoritative sources and key topics, (2) direct fetches of official documentation and primary sources, (3) independent third-party validation via blogs and analyses, with explicit pauses between phases to reassess findings and adjust direction.
- When researching a direction embedded in a multi-round initiative, ask for or scan a 'research context index' listing prior rounds and key findings files BEFORE executing tool calls, to avoid 35+ turns of exploratory directory reading.
- When researching a known internal monorepo project, always attempt a direct path-based read (e.g., github.netflix.net/corp/monorepo-name/project-name/README) *before* launching keyword searches, to establish ground truth quickly and avoid exploratory drift in turns 1–10.
- When researching a person/org across multiple RAG systems, establish a deduplication heuristic: after 2 tool calls of the same type return overlapping results, pause and synthesize before querying again; log the overlap to justify either escalating to a different tool or narrowing the query.
- When researching a potentially ambiguous technical topic (especially version-specific or ecosystem features), explicitly state candidate interpretations and their implications in turn 2-3, then prioritize sources that disambiguate before drilling deep into a single interpretation.
- When researching a potentially false premise, explicitly document the hypothesis reversal in intermediate summaries and explain the evidence that changed direction, so the investigation remains transparent and the final conclusion is traceable to specific evidence shifts.
- When researching a product or service, always verify current status (active, deprecated, sunset, superseded) before investing significant effort in deep dives; if product is deprecated/sunset, immediately pivot to successor or document the EOL status with date as a critical finding.
- When researching a specific technical benchmark or evaluation (e.g., LongMemEval), explicitly confirm the output format and corroboration threshold with the user before beginning aggressive tool use, to avoid gathering data that doesn't match the intended delivery structure.
- When researching a topic with 'aggressive tool usage' mandates, maintain a local log of sources attempted and their results (accessible/blocked/incomplete) to avoid redundant fetches and to surface new search angles based on gaps.
- When researching abstract system behaviors (e.g., 'how do agents reconcile X'), begin with code-level tool searches (Grep, Sourcegraph) before broad web queries, to ground findings in concrete implementation evidence before seeking corroborating discourse.
- When researching acquisitions or M&A, run parallel searches across all named entities in the first batch (Turns 9–14), then use Turn 15–16 to synthesize findings and identify secondary targets (related companies, parent holdings, spin-offs) before escalating searches. This prevents redundant searches and improves coverage early.
- When researching an unfamiliar technical system, validate core architectural assumptions (e.g., 'What is Runway really?') in the first 3 tool calls before committing to a deep search strategy; flag contradictions with the user's framing immediately and propose a pivot.
- When researching archived or superseded projects, always explicitly flag the status (archived/deprecated/live) early and update the research focus to understand the succession/replacement narrative, not just the original design.
- When researching broad platform comparisons, explicitly map search queries to capability dimensions (latency, scalability, ease-of-use, ecosystem maturity, deployment models) upfront to avoid redundant searches and ensure systematic coverage rather than exploratory discovery.
- When researching closed/proprietary products with minimal public technical docs (e.g., Cursor Memory, Copilot Workspace internals), prioritize vendor blogs, release notes, GitHub issues mentioning integration, and academic papers over generic web searches; explicitly note what is inferred vs. documented to avoid false corroboration claims.
- When researching comparative system limits (cost, performance, latency), explicitly define 'scale' thresholds upfront (e.g., nodes/edges, writes/sec, latency SLAs) and adjust search strategy iteratively based on whether data is available, rather than treating all metrics equally.
- When researching competitive products, proactively identify and query internal/proprietary tool access (e.g., 'get-netflix-engineering-context') early in the session (turn 5–10) to maximize differentiated insights before relying heavily on public web sources.
- When researching complex technical algorithms, escalate tool strategies systematically: begin with web search for overview, then fetch primary documents, then extract source code directly via Bash only if web fetch is insufficient; document the reason for each escalation.
- When researching complex technical comparisons with underspecified baselines, use the first 3-5 turns to explicitly enumerate baseline definitions, measurement criteria, and scope constraints before committing to tool execution; then confirm these constraints with the user or document assumptions clearly in the final output.
- When researching documented failures or rollbacks, prioritize evidence from official product announcements, changelogs, and incident reports before attempting code-level searches; reserve code search for implementation validation after failure claims are found, not as a primary discovery method.
- When researching domain-specific APIs (e.g., LLM pricing), explicitly filter available tools by semantic relevance before invoking them; skip tools with obvious category mismatches (e.g., Netflix search for AI pricing) even if instructed to use 'all tools aggressively'.
- When researching ecosystem standards or 'canonical' implementations for a spec without a formal primitive, explicitly investigate whether the spec defines the construct natively (via documentation search of official sources first) before assuming community conventions, to disambiguate canonical-as-spec from canonical-as-de-facto-standard.
- When researching external partnerships, explicitly check tool availability against the execution context before attempting access; if internal tools are not mentioned in the prompt or confirmed available, rely on web search and structured documentation rather than speculating about hidden tools. Consolidate findings into the required output format as soon as sufficient evidence is gathered, rather than extending searches indefinitely.
- When researching future-dated events or events with unclear recency, explicitly state in the findings section whether sources are contemporaneous with the claimed event date, speculative, or reconstructed from post-event commentary. Flag any claims sourced from a date significantly earlier than the event itself.
- When researching individuals within a company, explicitly note which data sources are internal/sensitive (commit history, Slack archives, employee IDs, email addresses) and confirm with the user before including such details in final output, especially for non-public figures.
- When researching internal Google products with closed-source architecture, explicitly attempt engineering context and internal documentation tools before defaulting to public web search. If those tools fail or are unavailable, state that limitation prominently in output rather than presenting web-sourced claims as authoritative.
- When researching internal company infrastructure or engineering relationships, prioritize targeted RAG/documentation tools over broad search APIs as the initial approach; reserve broad searches for exploratory phases only after confirming search scope is manageable.
- When researching internal company practices (Netflix ML code review), explicitly surface confidence limitations and distinguish between public-facing claims (talks, patents, blogs) and inferred practices. Mark claims about internal workflows as 'inferred_from_public_evidence' rather than 'single_source' when the source is public.
- When researching internal cost/program data with explicit 'cross-reference' requirements, invoke tool calls in parallel batches by source type (RAG → Search APIs → Data fetches) rather than sequential single calls, and annotate each claim's corroboration count before synthesis.
- When researching internal financial/budget data, prioritize internal authoritative sources (finance systems, Jira planning projects, Confluence finance spaces) and explicitly call out any team/scope ambiguities in early turns before launching exhaustive searches, so the user can clarify boundaries and prevent wasted tool calls on wrong org units.
- When researching internal infrastructure (repos, internal services, company-specific systems), deprioritize public WebSearch unless explicitly requested; weight internal documentation (manuals), Slack, and code search 80% vs web 20% by default.
- When researching internal infrastructure risks for private organizations, prioritize internal tool calls (Jira, Grep on private repos, internal documentation reads) before external web sources; mark the transition point explicitly in reasoning to avoid false starts.
- When researching internal org structures with limited documented access, explicitly state confidence levels and identify critical claims that depend on single unverified sources, rather than presenting inferred hierarchies as fact.
- When researching internal organizational decisions (reporting lines, org chart changes, team assignments), prioritize Netflix engineering context APIs and local filesystem searches before broad web search; external sources are unlikely to have non-public personnel/structural decisions.
- When researching internal organizational figures, always search commit histories, Slack/chat archives, and documentation simultaneously in parallel batches, then use early findings (e.g., organizational acronyms like DOV) to refine subsequent queries rather than executing all searches independently.
- When researching internal organizational structure or team ownership, prioritize internal tool searches (Jira, Confluence, internal wikis) before external web searches, and if internal searches return no results within 3-5 queries, explicitly report null findings rather than continuing external searches.
- When researching internal organizational topics, prioritize discovery of authoritative single-source-of-truth documents (owned wikis, official org charts, published policies) early, using search APIs before exhaustive Slack sampling, to reduce tool call waste and converge faster on ground truth.
- When researching internal personnel with large tool suites, pre-filter for likely access barriers (LinkedIn, public social media) and prioritize internal documentation repositories (CODEOWNERS, commit logs, Slack) to maximize signal-to-noise and avoid wasted WebFetch attempts.
- When researching internal systems (databases, services, metrics), ask for or search documentation first to establish authoritative naming conventions and service identifiers before querying observability APIs, to avoid 35+ turns of incorrect queries.
- When researching internal systems for a specific organization, explicitly ask or search for an available tool inventory or integration documentation before attempting filesystem exploration; avoid assuming local bash/glob access will surface required APIs.
- When researching internal systems with ambiguous architectural thresholds, explicitly call out underspecified trigger conditions in the structured output claims section and mark their corroboration as 'inferred' rather than 'stated' to distinguish documented limits from reverse-engineered capacity ceilings.
- When researching internal systems, request a prioritized list of available tools/MCP endpoints at the start, or provide a tool registry file, to eliminate discovery-phase redundancy.
- When researching large academic papers, establish a single coordinated fetch strategy upfront (e.g., 'fetch HTML version first, then PDF, then supplementary materials in sequence') rather than retrying overlapping chunks to avoid redundant token consumption for the same content.
- When researching metrics or usage statistics, explicitly identify the system of record (e.g., observability platform, analytics warehouse, metrics API) before beginning broad searches; ask the user to clarify if not provided, rather than exploring code and docs as a proxy for quantitative data.
- When researching nascent governance topics (>2024), prioritize web/documentation sources and reserve code-search tools for validating implementation patterns only; confirm tool selection aligns with evidence type needed before execution.
- When researching organizational entities (teams, people, systems) by name, query engineering context and personnel tools first before broad code/documentation searches, as naming conventions may differ from literal search terms.
- When researching organizational governance or personnel structures, define search primitives upfront (e.g., 'person name + title', 'governance forum + cadence', 'decision escalation path') and explicitly map each primitive to 1-2 tool calls before launching parallel queries, to minimize exploratory churn and maximize early source convergence.
- When researching organizational or technical strategy questions, explicitly probe for three temporal data points: (1) last confirmed user/adoption metric with date, (2) most recent strategic decision or pivot announcement, and (3) forward-looking roadmap or deprecation notice. Flag any >6 month gaps in data to the user.
- When researching organizational roles or commit metrics, explicitly ask the user for time windows and thresholds (e.g., 'top 5 by commits in last 6 months?') before beginning multi-tool investigation; capture these in a brief preamble turn to eliminate downstream assumption drift.
- When researching organizational structure, validate core assumptions about team hierarchy early (by turn 5-10) by querying org chart sources or manager attribution metadata before conducting exhaustive deep dives on team composition.
- When researching organizational structures in unfamiliar domains, explicitly ask for clarification on hierarchy terminology (EM vs TL vs Senior Manager) and approval process definitions before launching tool searches, then document assumptions in the structured output.
- When researching organizational structures or partnership mappings, explicitly ask for shared source-of-truth documents (team charters, SLO contracts, dependency registries) early in the research rather than relying entirely on inference from documentation and Slack.
- When researching organizational structures or personnel at private companies, explicitly request clarification on what information is appropriate to surface and whether internal documents should be corroborated against external sources before reporting claims about individuals.
- When researching organizational structures or personnel, prioritize internal databases (employee rosters, org-exports, personnel CSVs) and tool-based discovery (bash file search, code indexing) over public web search; if internal tools are available, use them first and web search second for corroboration only.
- When researching organizational/personnel information, explicitly probe access permissions and data availability before entering repetitive search loops; if internal systems are inaccessible after 3 initial attempts, pivot to documented public sources (conference talks, blog posts, GitHub commit logs) rather than cycling through similar queries.
- When researching personnel who may have left an organization, first establish employment status before searching for 'current' role details; prioritize 'did X work at Y' queries ahead of 'what is X's current role' queries to avoid wasted searches.
- When researching proprietary internal data (experiment volumes, metrics, team baselines), verify which tools actually have access to internal systems before launching searches; web search cannot retrieve private databases.
