Clarify with the user which internal systems to query by name (e.g., 'Should I use the Experimenter API, data warehouse views, or metrics dashboard?') before burning tool budget on unavailable sources.
- When researching proprietary systems (e.g., Netflix MGP), establish the target system's API contract and constraints BEFORE fetching upstream library docs; prioritize internal documentation first, then validate against external libraries.
- When researching proprietary systems (e.g., Netflix internals) where internal tools are unavailable, explicitly signal this constraint to the user and clarify which sources (public blogs, patents, conference talks) will constitute primary evidence, rather than silently deprioritizing unavailable tools.
- When researching proprietary systems with limited public documentation, explicitly identify and document unavailable primary sources early (e.g., 'Mako is Netflix-internal; no public repo/benchmarks found'), then pivot to secondary evidence (blogs, talks, academic papers) with explicit confidence qualifiers in the FINDINGS prose.
- When researching proprietary/non-public information from specific companies, explicitly state upfront what sources are public vs. speculative (press releases/conferences vs. rumors/leaks) and clarify confidence levels per source type before compiling findings.
- When researching questions with no known authoritative published answer, explicitly flag in early findings whether you are searching for primary data (peer-reviewed studies, vendor telemetry) vs. secondary/tertiary evidence (user reports, forum discussions, vendor claims), and scope the search strategy accordingly to avoid wasted queries on non-existent sources.
- When researching regulatory/compliance topics across jurisdictions, explicitly separate findings by jurisdiction in the claims array and note which regulatory bodies/documents anchor each claim to enable readers to verify compliance implications independently.
- When researching security incidents, prioritize tool diversity early: start with code/documentation search for internal postmortems (if available) before web search, to avoid over-relying on public web results that may miss proprietary incident reports or internal remediation work.
- When researching security/vulnerability topics, always invoke internal company context tools (engineering docs, Slack, run histories) after establishing baseline external research, to uncover deployed vs. proposed defenses and triangulate claims across public and internal sources.
- When researching team ownership of repositories, establish and document the authoritative source (e.g., CODEOWNERS file, team manifest) early, then use it as a validation gate for candidate repos to avoid redundant confirmation loops.
- When researching technical claims about production systems, explicitly track recency and source type (empirical vs anecdotal) separately in the CLAIMS array to help users distinguish between documented and speculative findings.
- When researching technical claims with no explicit success criteria, explicitly enumerate success metrics in the structured output preamble (e.g., 'Claim corroboration: requires 2+ independent sources for high-confidence claims; 1 source acceptable for vendor-specific deployment details'). This prevents ambiguity in later turns and grounds cross-referencing decisions.
- When researching technical integrations across multiple systems, structure tool calls into phases (discovery → validation → corroboration) and narrate the transition between phases to signal progress and allow early user intervention.
- When researching whether a negative result exists (e.g., 'Are there impossibility theorems for X?'), explicitly acknowledge if direct matches are absent and define what partial analogues count as corroborating evidence before exhaustive search.
- When researching with 'exhaustive' or 'aggressive' directives, establish explicit tool batching (e.g., 'Batch 1: broad searches; Batch 2: documentation fetches; Batch 3: code/internal search') and announce transitions to users to maintain transparency and avoid perception of redundancy.
- When researching with 'exhaustive' requirements, explicitly pause at midpoints (every 8-12 tool calls) to summarize interim findings and identify coverage gaps before continuing; this prevents redundant searches and maintains cognitive coherence in long sessions.
- When responding to a long-form design critique task with a large spec document (>10KB), proactively check remaining token budget after reading and before composing analysis. If <3,000 tokens remain, explicitly warn the user and propose either streaming output in chunks or splitting the task across multiple turns.
- When responding to a structured output request (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END block), always deliver the complete block in a single response, or explicitly signal continuation/resumption. Never emit a partial structure without clear indication of completion status or reason for pause.
- When responding to exhaustive research requests, articulate explicit evidence thresholds and termination criteria (e.g., 'I will conclude when I have 3+ independent sources or have exhausted all relevant tool queries for each claim'). State this threshold before beginning research to avoid ambiguity about sufficiency.
- When responding to exhaustive research requests, output the required structured format (FINDINGS|SOURCES|CLAIMS) explicitly before the end of the session, and confirm in intermediate turns that findings are accumulating to avoid ambiguity about deliverable status.
- When responding to long technical specifications requiring structured output, always generate and validate the complete output block before transmitting, including final closing markers (e.g., STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_END). If response will be long, preview token count and estimated completion before starting to help prevent truncation.
- When responding to open-ended research directions with underspecified terminology, explicitly call out assumed definitions and unknown scope boundaries early (turn 3-4), and offer to refine before launching tool calls, rather than inferring silently.
- When responding to research requests with explicit methodology (e.g., 'use ALL tools,' 'cross-reference sources,' 'at least N tool calls'), validate tool diversity during intermediate summaries: explicitly name tool types deployed so far and identify untested tool categories before final output to ensure all available modalities were exercised.
- When responding to structured design critique tasks, pre-calculate token budget for the full spec text + analysis output, and if near limit, split analysis into multiple turns or request chunked spec review rather than attempting full response that will truncate.
- When responding to structured-output requests involving large documents (>5k tokens), verify the full output fits within remaining budget and either split across turns or explicitly warn the user if truncation is likely before beginning the analysis.
- When resuming a multi-phase task after context compaction, do not emit a preamble or recap; jump directly to the next logical phase without signposting. Use the prior turn's context block to understand state and continue seamlessly.
- When retrieval-based queries return large unstructured text, prefer direct tool-specific search refinement (e.g., targeted rag-slack queries with date/author filters) over local parsing loops; if local parsing is necessary, validate syntax once before iterating rather than trial-and-error with grep/awk offsets.
- When searching for a specific individual or role across internal systems, if not found after exhausting primary sources (manuals, Slack, org docs), explicitly check public job postings and recruitment sites, as they often contain the most current org structure information—treat absence of internal evidence as a signal to expand scope rather than increase repetition.
- When searching for integration patterns returns zero positive evidence across multiple independent sources, explicitly state the negative finding as a validated conclusion and cease further searches to avoid diminishing-returns iterations; negative results are legitimate research outcomes if corroborated by multiple search strategies.
- When shifting tool strategy (e.g., from web search to internal tools), always emit a brief explanation of why the new tool is expected to yield relevant results, especially if the tool is domain-specific or internal. This prevents opaque context-gathering and helps users validate research methodology.
- When sourcing organizational claims (leadership names, team membership, sub-structure), always cross-reference at least two independent sources (e.g., email alias + org chart doc + Slack thread) before asserting names/roles, and explicitly state source verification status in reasoning.
- When starting multi-turn research tasks requiring tool orchestration, explicitly track which tool categories have been exercised (WebSearch, WebFetch, LocalCodeSearch, FileRead, etc.) and ensure coverage of all categories before synthesis turns; document 'search exhaustion' criteria before declaring research complete.
- When stating planned parallelism or strategy pivots (e.g., 'I'll use multiple tools in parallel', 'I'm switching to alternative approaches'), follow through immediately with tool invocations matching the stated plan in the same turn or next turn, or explicitly note why the plan cannot be executed.
- When streaming structured output in a single turn, monitor token budget and prepare a continuation strategy; if truncation occurs, explicitly signal incompleteness with a clear flag (e.g., '[OUTPUT_TRUNCATED]') rather than breaking mid-JSON, and offer to resume in a follow-up turn.
- When synthesizing 50+ source documents into a structured summary, prioritize sequential confirmation of document completion (e.g., 'files 1-20 read, 21-40 read, 41-60 read') over attempting full parallelization. Use parallel batching only after sequential verification, to avoid token waste on failed/malformed reads.
- When synthesizing cross-file research summaries, query for the prior cumulative summary location or request it explicitly in the first clarification turn, rather than searching for it mid-session, to avoid delays in novelty assessment.
- When synthesizing large document sets (>50 files), explicitly estimate token cost upfront, offer parallel vs. sequential tradeoffs to the user, and if delegating to a sub-agent, include a summary of sub-agent reasoning and key claims in your response so the user retains auditability.
- When synthesizing large structured outputs with multiple sections (FINDINGS, SOURCES, CLAIMS), pre-calculate estimated token cost of full output; if synthesis risks truncation, summarize findings into a concise intermediate summary first, then confirm it fits within response budget before final emission.
- When synthesizing multi-source findings with aspirational vs. shipped distinctions, explicitly flag inferred features as '[aspirational / not yet documented in shipped code]' to prevent reader confusion between intent and current implementation.
- When synthesizing research findings across internal and external sources, explicitly include a 'corroboration_matrix' or footnote explaining which claims are supported by internal evidence only, external only, or both; this prevents conflation of internal design intent with public best practices.
- When synthesizing research findings, always enumerate: (1) sources consulted, (2) pillars/entities found, (3) explicit gaps ('could not find data on X'), (4) recency of oldest source used, before delivering structured output, so the user can assess completeness in real-time.
- When synthesizing research findings, explicitly verify that the final STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START block answers the core research question (the WHAT clause) and is not truncated; if token budget is tight, summarize findings concisely before truncation risk rather than after.
- When system warnings (stale-todo, deprecation notices, or environment alerts) appear during execution, explicitly log the warning, state whether it will be honored or overridden, and briefly explain why to maintain audit trail and prevent silent bypasses of potentially important guidance.
- When targeting time-series metrics (e.g., 'P99 trend'), always prompt for explicit time window specification (last 7/14/30 days, specific date range, or period-over-period comparison) before executing metric queries, to avoid ambiguity in temporal scope.
- When tasked with 'aggressive parallel' research across multiple tool types, maintain a deduplication log of fetched URLs within each wave to avoid re-fetching the same sources, then begin the next wave with fresh searches for uncovered topics.
- When tasked with 'aggressive' multi-tool research, explicitly enumerate available tools at the start, immediately invoke at least 3-5 distinct tool types in the first response, and if any critical tools are unavailable, surface this to the user with a revised execution plan before continuing.
- When tasked with 'aggressive' tool usage and research of internal systems, use a documented search pattern (e.g., ToolSearch → RAG → Bash → Slack) and log the rationale for each tool choice to avoid redundant exploration and signal when saturation is reached.
- When tasked with 'aggressive' tool use and explicit volume targets (5-10+ calls per direction), begin tool invocation immediately in the next turn rather than spend turns on planning; frame intermediate findings after each batch of tool calls to show iterative refinement of queries based on prior results.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive cross-referenced research,' explicitly track tool call count and source diversity per direction, and report mid-session (every 10-15 turns) to validate alignment with quantitative targets like '5-10 tool calls per direction' before final synthesis.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive research across N internal sources', explicitly plan and name the multi-phase research strategy in the first response, itemizing which tools address which sub-questions, and state the stopping criterion (e.g., '3 independent corroborations per claim', 'coverage of all N sources') before beginning.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive research' across multiple commercial products, explicitly request quantitative cost inputs ($/month baseline, transaction volume, query rate) and scale definitions (agents, sessions, users) early in conversation, before tool execution begins, to ground the analysis in concrete parameters rather than requiring post-hoc assumptions.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive research' across multiple data sources, adopt a three-phase strategy: (1) bootstrap context from seed files and vocabularies, (2) launch parallel searches across tool types in priority order, (3) iterate to deeper specificity only after broad reconnaissance is complete. Log tool calls and sources explicitly to enable client verification of thoroughness.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive research' across multiple tool categories, track cumulative tool calls per category and proactively rotate tools every 2-4 turns rather than clustering calls; explicitly log which tool categories have been exercised and which remain to avoid tool clustering bias.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive research' on internal systems, batch Netflix API calls in parallel within the first 3 turns and prioritize document fetch over bash extraction—reserve bash for structured metadata extraction only after fetching full document bodies.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive research' on multi-faceted topics, explicitly map architectural patterns or dimensions to separate tool-call clusters; execute each cluster to saturation (e.g., 'LiteLLM: 4–5 searches, then WebFetch, then RAG') before moving to next pattern, rather than interleaving.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive research' requiring structured output, explicitly confirm the final synthesis step in-turn before the session ends; do not rely on implicit understanding that output will be generated outside the turn log.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive research,' explicitly define success criteria in the first reasoning turn (e.g., 'I will consider this complete when I have: (1) the exact system prompt, (2) at least 3 corroborating sources, (3) a test of prompt behavior against example inputs') and use that checklist to avoid redundant verification loops.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive research,' explicitly partition the tool strategy into discovery phase, parallel search phase, and synthesis phase, and narrate which phase you're entering. Use phrases like 'Now entering parallel search phase to cross-reference findings' to signal deliberate progression and build user confidence in systematic coverage.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive research,' explicitly state your stopping criteria (e.g., 'I will search until X sources confirm finding, or Y turns/tokens elapse, or Z tool endpoints are exhausted') before beginning, and report against those criteria in final output.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive' cross-source research, batch tool calls in parallel waves (4–5 per cycle) and follow each search with fetches to retrieve full-text evidence; alternate between breadth (new search angles) and depth (source validation) to avoid redundant queries.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive' internal research with an explicit tool-call minimum, establish a corroboration threshold rule upfront: after a claim is confirmed across N independent sources (N≥2), document it and pivot to new questions rather than accumulating identical evidence.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive' open-ended research, establish a stopping criterion in the first 3 turns by asking: 'Do you want me to stop when I have 10 corroborated claims, or when I reach N tokens/time, or when new sources stop yielding new insights?' Default to stopping when corroboration converges.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive' or 'aggressive' research requiring tool synthesis into structured output, explicitly track search query diversity (not just count) and reserve final 10% of turns for output assembly and cross-referencing claims before returning the structured block; do not end mid-research.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive' or 'aggressive' research, distinguish between tool diversity (use different tool types) and tool volume (make many calls). After the first round of each tool type returns results, synthesize findings before launching follow-up searches; avoid repeating similar queries with minor keyword variations across the same tool without evidence of new discovery.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive' research across fragmented systems, explicitly state the hypothesis (e.g., 'T&S likely runs on parallel stack, not AIP') and iteratively test via targeted tool queries rather than exhaustive enumeration — this prevents circular Sourcegraph sweeps and focuses token spend on high-signal findings.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive' research across internal + external sources, pause after the first 3-5 tool calls to verify access to internal sources (Slack, internal repos, OKR docs) and report back to the user if any are unavailable, rather than assuming access and potentially fabricating evidence.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive' research across internal systems, explicitly confirm tool execution counts and source corroboration thresholds with the user before pivoting to output compilation, to avoid incomplete delivery of promised multi-source validation.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive' research across multiple domains, first query for the complete inventory (e.g., full AIP org chart, all 17 pillars) before drilling into each domain. This prevents scattered coverage and ensures no major areas are missed.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive' research across multiple tools, explicitly log tool call count and planned search coverage in an intermediate checkpoint turn before synthesis, and defer final output block composition until all queries complete, to prevent truncation of deliverables.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive' research without explicit token/turn budgets, establish a stopping rule mid-stream: after the first 25% of tool calls, pause to evaluate whether corroboration threshold is being met, and communicate token spend + projected remainder to the user before continuing deep drilling.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive' research, execute tool calls in parallel batches (5 WebSearch, then 5 WebFetch, then Agent/code tools) rather than sequential single calls, and avoid meta-commentary turns that describe research strategy instead of executing tools—move directly to structured output synthesis.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive' research, explicitly define a stopping criterion in the system prompt (e.g., 'stop after 3 independent corroborations of each major claim, or after 40+ tool calls') to clarify when thoroughness is achieved and prevent unbounded exploration.
- When tasked with 'exhaustive' research, explicitly define and track quantitative completion criteria (e.g., 'minimum 3 independent sources per claim', 'search across all 6 tool categories', 'max 10 turns per topic area') before beginning, and report final metrics in structured output.
- When tasked with GitHub repository research, prioritize pre-authenticated `gh` CLI queries (`gh api repos/OWNER/REPO`) over unauthenticated REST calls; avoid attempting unauthenticated API access if a CLI tool with built-in auth is available.
- When tasked with a research audit across multiple tool sources, preemptively use Bash/jq for large file processing rather than attempting Read on single-line JSON. Launch parallel tool calls in batches of 5+ to maximize throughput.
- When tasked with adversarial critique of governance or security-critical systems, explicitly list the failure modes you are *not* reporting (e.g., 'Out of scope: formal verification, cryptographic primitives') and explain why, to preempt misalignment on scope.
- When tasked with adversarial/critical analysis of a specification, explicitly call out the scope boundary (e.g., 'I will evaluate each component individually but not cross-component systemic risks, which were not requested') to prevent ambiguity about what dimension of critique is in scope.
- When tasked with aggressive parallel research across multiple sources, explicitly partition the search space by source and execute 4+ simultaneous tool calls per phase, then wait for results before pivoting. State the corroboration threshold (e.g., 'a claim requires 2+ independent sources to be marked multiple_sources') before searching.
- When tasked with analyzing large specs (>10KB) in adversarial/critic mode, first outline the expected number and categories of flaws to be found, then deliver the structured analysis in pages rather than a single response. This prevents token exhaustion mid-analysis and allows streaming completion.
- When tasked with batch processing >50 items (files, records, etc.), always propose a batch/streaming/glob strategy upfront rather than defaulting to serial iteration. Confirm the user's preference before executing.
- When tasked with bootstrapping structured knowledge from multiple sources, explicitly log the extraction criteria and deduplication strategy before consolidating, to enable verification and reproducibility of term selection.
- When tasked with comparative novelty assessment (e.g., INFO_GAIN_RATE), always explicitly ask for or verify access to the prior reference artifact at the outset; if unavailable, state the heuristic approach and confidence level upfront rather than inferring silently.
- When tasked with cross-source research on proprietary engineering decisions, explicitly ask the user in turn 2-3 which sources are publicly available vs. confidential, and adjust tool allocation accordingly to avoid wasting calls on inaccessible internal postmortems.
- When tasked with data extraction from multiple sources, validate output format early (e.g., write a sample JSON structure after the first or second successful fetch) rather than only at the end, to catch schema mismatches sooner.
- When tasked with deep research, establish explicit filtering criteria for 'production systems' and benchmark thresholds early in execution; if none are provided, output assumptions in turn 1 before tool deployment and ask for confirmation.
- When tasked with deep research, validate source availability (paper publication status, GitHub repo existence) in the first 2 turns; if sources are embargoed, non-existent, or inaccessible, communicate this explicitly to the user immediately and suggest fallback directions rather than cycling through redundant tool calls.
- When tasked with emitting large structured outputs, pre-compute required token budget and either emit in explicitly-labeled chunks across multiple turns or verify completion before confirming synthesis is done. Never emit 'now generating output' messages that cut off mid-content; this signals misplanning rather than progress.
- When tasked with exhaustive comparative research across multiple methods without evidence of prior comparative studies, validate literature availability in the first 2-3 turns via targeted meta-searches (e.g., 'Method A vs Method B benchmark comparison') before committing to 30+ tool calls. If no direct comparisons exist, clarify scope with the user rather than treating absence of evidence as opportunity for untargeted search.
- When tasked with exhaustive cross-referenced research, execute tool calls in waves of 5-6 parallel queries per phase, alternating tool types (RAG → API → Slack → context) and search angles (team-level → IC-level → feature-level) to maximize triangulation and minimize redundant queries.
- When tasked with exhaustive cross-referenced research, explicitly document the search strategy upfront (which tools will be used for which aspects, what queries will be tested, how corroboration will be verified), and then report results with inline evidence snippets and source URLs in-line, not deferred to end-of-session output.
- When tasked with exhaustive internal research across multiple Netflix tools, execute tool discovery and capability checks in parallel within the first 3 turns, then explicitly summarize available data sources before beginning substantive searches to avoid wasted queries on inaccessible repos.
- When tasked with exhaustive internal research, prioritize discovery of canonical/master reference documents early (via doc/manual searches) before conducting broad parallel searches; use the master doc as baseline to identify gaps, then fill gaps with targeted queries. This reduces redundant broad searches and accelerates convergence to complete coverage.
- When tasked with exhaustive literature research on specialized topics (MoE, calibration), document tool call strategy upfront (e.g., 'Phase 1: X searches for A, Phase 2: Y fetches for B, Phase 3: synthesis') and deliver a turn-count checkpoint mid-session to confirm alignment before final synthesis.
- When tasked with exhaustive multi-directional research, establish a tool priority order (high-signal first: engineering context > internal docs > Slack > web) and confirm pivot points with a checkpoint summary before moving to lower-signal sources, to avoid redundant queries.
- When tasked with exhaustive multi-source research requiring corroboration classification, explicitly document the corroboration heuristic used (e.g., 'single_source' = found in one place, 'multiple_sources' = independently found in 2+ unrelated sources) and apply it consistently to all claims in the output, rather than inferring the standard from the example format.
- When tasked with exhaustive multi-source research using tool variety, prioritize breadth-first discovery (repos, teams, docs) in the first 20% of turns, depth-first extraction in the next 60%, and synthesis in the final 20%. Avoid re-enumeration without explicit gaps; if a tool returns partial results, note the limitation in findings rather than re-querying unless directed.
- When tasked with exhaustive multi-source research with explicit 'aggressive tool use' language, launch tool calls on every turn including synthesis turns—use interim turns to batch-fetch auxiliary context (repo READMEs, related Jira epics, Slack user profiles) rather than reserving tool calls only for primary searches.
- When tasked with exhaustive multi-source research with specific output formatting requirements, explicitly confirm the final structured output block has been generated and formatted correctly before concluding the session, or include it in the final response even if synthesis is still ongoing.
- When tasked with exhaustive multi-source research, establish explicit stopping criteria upfront (e.g., 'stop after 3 no-result queries per tool' or 'max 50 tool calls') and periodically announce progress against that target to avoid silent token budget depletion or truncation.
- When tasked with exhaustive multi-source research, explicitly log each tool call with source name and result snippet in real-time before proceeding to synthesis, rather than deferring all tool calls to parallel agents without user-visible intermediates.
- When tasked with exhaustive multi-source research, maintain a running deduplication log of prior tool calls and query prompts; skip queries that would replicate >80% of prior search surface area unless explicitly asked to re-verify.
- When tasked with exhaustive multi-source research, parallelize tool calls across all available APIs in batches (web, internal, RAG, engineering context) within the first 5 turns, explicitly note transient failures without retry loops, and defer synthesis to final turns to maximize signal before final output generation.
- When tasked with exhaustive multi-source research, proactively identify and resolve disambiguation (multiple entities with the same name, overlapping topic boundaries) at the start, before executing tool calls, to prevent downstream rework.
- When tasked with exhaustive multi-tool research requiring structured output, explicitly signal task completion by producing the STRUCTURED_OUTPUT block as a delimited final artifact before concluding, even if partially collected, rather than ending with 'I have comprehensive evidence' without delivering the required format.
- When tasked with exhaustive multi-tool research with explicit tool-use quotas, plan tool deployment in bursts (3-5 calls per synthesis cycle) and announce the cycle structure to the user upfront to establish transparency and set expectations for token consumption.
- When tasked with exhaustive multi-tool research, explicitly map available tools before searching: use `help`, environment inspection, or a single reconnaissance query first. Then allocate search attempts across different tools/strategies rather than repeating identical queries.
- When tasked with exhaustive multi-tool research, use explicit turn budgeting early (e.g., 'I will allocate ~40 turns to search, ~15 to document fetch, ~5 to synthesis') and report cadence updates to avoid inefficient tool rediscovery; parallel tool batching should be explicit and tracked.
- When tasked with exhaustive research across 62+ turns, structure information gathering into explicit phases (broad search → targeted fetch → synthesis) and announce phase transitions to users to maintain transparency on session progress and methodology.
- When tasked with exhaustive research across a named list of discrete entities, parallelize tool calls by batch (e.g., WebSearch first wave, then second wave) before entering validation/fetch phase, and explicitly track call count to ensure threshold is met before synthesis.
- When tasked with exhaustive research across internal and external sources, explicitly state the research phases (local exploration → external validation → synthesis) and confirm the user's success criteria for 'sufficient evidence' before commencing, to avoid over-exploration or misaligned depth.
- When tasked with exhaustive research across internal code repos, prioritize Sourcegraph NLS (natural language semantic) search over keyword search in early turns, as it better captures conceptual relationships; reserve keyword search for anchoring specific artifact names once identified.
- When tasked with exhaustive research across internal systems, explicitly state stopping criteria after initial broad searches (e.g., 'I'll consolidate after 10 tool calls and then target 3-4 specific gaps'), then announce when that threshold is met before pivoting to targeted searches, to avoid ambiguity about thoroughness.
- When tasked with exhaustive research across internal tools, explicitly track which tools have been queried and report remaining unexplored sources before synthesis, to confirm coverage completeness and justify stopping point.
- When tasked with exhaustive research across local and external sources, explicitly partition the session into phases (local code exploration → external research → synthesis) and announce the pivot point to the user, providing confidence that depth is being prioritized over breadth.
- When tasked with exhaustive research across many directions, proactively batch tool calls into parallel rounds (e.g., 3-5 simultaneous WebSearch queries per turn) and then pause to compile interim findings before continuing; this maximizes signal-to-noise and allows earlier detection of access/rate-limit barriers.
- When tasked with exhaustive research across multiple dimensions, organize tool calls into thematic waves with explicit strategy announcements between waves, and reserve WebFetch for detail-deepening only after broad search discovery.
- When tasked with exhaustive research across multiple internal sources, execute tool calls in a planned sequence (manuals → Slack → thread fetch) rather than single-threaded iteration; batch similar tool types to reduce turn overhead and improve signal-to-noise ratio.
- When tasked with exhaustive research across multiple nested questions (seed audit + priority direction), explicitly break down the scope into sequential sub-tasks, show tool invocation results after each batch, and return intermediate structured output to allow the user to redirect before the final turn. If a tool call fails (e.g., access denied to internal repo), log the error and pivot to public alternatives rather than silently retrying.
- When tasked with exhaustive research across multiple regimes or domains, explicitly confirm or infer the scope of key terms (e.g., 'agent memory' = conversation logs, vector embeddings, persistent storage, or all?) before launching searches; document the definition in a metadata turn to anchor subsequent findings.
- When tasked with exhaustive research across multiple regions/regulations, structure tool calls in three phases: (1) breadth-first parallel searches to map the landscape, (2) targeted deep-fetches of promising primary sources, (3) cross-reference searches to validate claims across independent sources. Log the hypothesis being tested in each phase.
- When tasked with exhaustive research across multiple sources, alternate between WebSearch and WebFetch in waves (search → refine → fetch full content → synthesize) rather than front-loading all searches, to maximize signal-to-noise ratio and avoid redundant queries.
- When tasked with exhaustive research across multiple tool categories, explicitly rotate through tool types in sequence (web search → web fetch → code search → internal docs) rather than clustering all instances of one tool type together, to maximize serendipitous cross-domain discovery.
- When tasked with exhaustive research across multiple tool types, explicitly sequence public sources before proprietary tools to avoid false negatives; pivot to internal/proprietary sources only after diminishing returns are observed on external research.
- When tasked with exhaustive research across multiple tools, batch tool invocations within single turns where possible (e.g., WebSearch + WebFetch in a single turn) rather than separating them across turns, to reduce reasoning overhead and improve productivity ratio.
- When tasked with exhaustive research across open-ended tool availability, front-load a search strategy phase: before making tool calls, articulate candidate search queries, prioritized source types (official docs > announcements > third-party analysis), and early stopping criteria (e.g., 'stop web searching after 3 sources confirm the same finding') to maximize signal-to-noise ratio within token budget.
- When tasked with exhaustive research across proprietary internal systems, explicitly flag any confidentiality or disclosure concerns before synthesizing output, and ask whether internal system findings should be redacted or anonymized.
- When tasked with exhaustive research across undefined domains, explicitly enumerate the conceptual dimensions of key terms (e.g., 'memory' = vector DB size vs. context window vs. conversation history) early, then parallelize searches across each dimension to maximize tool efficiency and source diversity.
- When tasked with exhaustive research across unknown evidence landscape, establish a stopping rule explicitly: e.g., 'Stop web search when 3 consecutive queries return <2 new unique papers or when recency plateaus at same publication window.' This prevents late-session diminishing returns without requiring user intervention.
- When tasked with exhaustive research and 'maximum thoroughness', explicitly mark synthesis/pause turns with a brief summary of findings-so-far to enable easy tracking of research phases and help users understand when research is complete enough to stop.
- When tasked with exhaustive research containing underspecified scope (target systems, evidence tiers, success criteria), ask 2-3 clarifying questions before tool execution, then proceed only after confirmation or reasonable inference is documented.
- When tasked with exhaustive research deliverables requiring structured output (STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_START/END blocks), explicitly confirm the final output block is rendered to the user before closing the session, and if required sources fail to parse (e.g., PDFs), fallback to cached summaries or note the gap in claims corroboration.
- When tasked with exhaustive research for internal metrics on a specific company, prioritize specialized internal context tools (engineering dashboards, chat archives, wiki searches) before public web search, and explicitly pivot to proxy metrics (investor calls, blog posts, proxies) if direct sources are null within the first 10 tool calls.
- When tasked with exhaustive research on a potentially non-existent entity, explicitly state the hypothesis shift (from 'does it exist?' to 'it likely does not exist') with a turn number and evidence threshold, rather than silently pivoting, to maintain transparency with the user and allow early course correction if the user disagrees with the negative hypothesis.
- When tasked with exhaustive research on a specialized regulatory or compliance topic, execute multiple sequential rounds of searches with progressively refined terminology rather than single broad queries; after initial results, immediately fetch primary sources (trust pages, docs) and then conduct targeted follow-ups on gaps and specific mechanism details (e.g., 'Standard Contractual Clauses' + platform name).
- When tasked with exhaustive research on a specific question, create a tool call checklist upfront (1-2 turns) listing sources to query (Sourcegraph, RAG Slack, NECP, manuals, search APIs), then execute in parallel; skip generic tool discovery and move directly to domain-specific MCPs.
- When tasked with exhaustive research on an organization's internal systems, always check for prior research artifacts (e.g., run directories, knowledge bases, prior session outputs) in the first 2–3 turns before external searches; internal cross-reference is often more signal-rich than external sources.
- When tasked with exhaustive research on emerging/fast-moving tools, prefer WebFetch over WebSearch alone after initial broad searches; validate findings against official documentation and announcements rather than news articles or speculation.
- When tasked with exhaustive research on forward-dated roadmaps (H2 2026 at time of session), explicitly ask or note whether you are searching embargoed/internal planning docs vs. public disclosures, and flag corroboration confidence for speculative future items separately from present-state facts.
- When tasked with exhaustive research on internal systems, first map which tools have access constraints (auth, SSO, rate limits) in a single discovery phase; then design searches to avoid blocked paths, rather than discovering blockers mid-investigation.
- When tasked with exhaustive research on internal systems, prioritize reading persisted findings and prior run outputs before launching fresh tool queries; confirm search-result saturation via targeted gap-filling rather than brute-force tool enumeration.
- When tasked with exhaustive research on internal/proprietary systems, prioritize access-controlled sources (internal repos, engineering context tools, config files) over speculative external sources; invest turns in primary source discovery and deep extraction before pursuing secondary paths like discussion threads or notebooks that may not exist.
- When tasked with exhaustive research on potentially proprietary internal systems, always execute a sequential validation gate: (1) exhaust internal code/doc searches with explicit 'not found' confirmation, (2) only then escalate to public web search, and (3) report the scope determination before commencing heavy web operations. This prevents wasted turns on public results when internal sources are the target.
- When tasked with exhaustive research on products with unclear release status (e.g., GPT-5-Codex), begin with a quick verification search to confirm the product exists and has public documentation; if not found, explicitly report 'Product not found in public sources' rather than continuing deep investigation.
- When tasked with exhaustive research on proprietary internal systems, explicitly note and flag any confidential/embargoed findings before including them in output; offer a redacted version if needed. Prompt the user to clarify source credibility hierarchy before finalizing claims array corroboration status.
- When tasked with exhaustive research on proprietary/competitive intelligence (benchmark methodologies, internal evaluations), explicitly confirm in the first response whether to include confidential claims, restrict to public disclosures only, or note all sources' visibility status. This prevents silent assumption-making about inclusion criteria.
- When tasked with exhaustive research on technical literature, explicitly surface any definitional ambiguities (e.g., 'frontier scale', 'effective substitute', corroboration strength thresholds) before committing to tool strategy, to ensure claims align with unstated expectations.
- When tasked with exhaustive research on three distinct technical approaches, structure tool use in labeled batches (e.g., 'Searching pHash+OCR approaches (searches 1-4)', 'Searching vision embedding approaches (searches 5-8)') and explicitly track coverage to avoid search drift and ensure equal investigative depth across branches.
- When tasked with exhaustive research producing structured output, explicitly confirm receipt of the full structured block (FINDINGS, SOURCES, CLAIMS with all metadata) before marking the task complete; log the final output in a verification turn if the primary output is not visible.
- When tasked with exhaustive research requiring 'ALL tools aggressively', explicitly name the tool set available (code search, documentation search, chat search, engineering context, web search, web fetch, pipeline inspection) and attempt each in sequence per research direction, even if Web tools dominate the visible results.
- When tasked with exhaustive research requiring 5-10+ tool calls, batch calls in groups of 4-5 and execute sequentially without intermediate synthesis pauses until reaching minimum call threshold, then synthesize once to avoid checkpoint overhead.
- When tasked with exhaustive research requiring N-way cross-corroboration, explicitly log each source's findings before moving to the next tool, and maintain a running deduplicated source registry to prevent redundant keyword searches on the same backend.
- When tasked with exhaustive research requiring a specific structured output format, explicitly state the output format requirements at the midpoint of data collection and commit to a synthesis phase after N tool calls or when confidence thresholds are met; never let tool invocation become an open loop.
- When tasked with exhaustive research requiring cross-referenced claims, explicitly track which tool calls have been executed and which search angles remain unexplored; after each major pivot in strategy, summarize findings gathered so far to avoid redundant searches and to stage the final synthesis.
- When tasked with exhaustive research requiring multiple tool sources, always invoke ListMcpResourcesTool or equivalent capability-mapping tool in turn 1 before dispatching any searches; this prevents redundant tool discovery delays and ensures systematic coverage of all available sources.
- When tasked with exhaustive research requiring parallel tool calls, explicitly track and emit tool-call counts per phase in reasoning steps (e.g., 'Phase 1: 3 tool calls; Phase 2: 7 tool calls') to ensure the minimum threshold is met and to give the user real-time visibility into progress against their explicit '5-10 per direction' requirement.

- When tasked with exhaustive research, establish diminishing-returns checkpoints: after tool calls show repeated/overlapping results, explicitly note saturation before continuing, and ask for pivot direction rather than executing rote additional queries.
